# Chapter 1: Sixty-One Days

*The Combine · FY 2291*

Kostya's tether let go in the forty-first minute of second shift, and Sol Dace, who was eleven meters away with both arms inside a phase coupling, heard it go before she saw it: a small dry tick in her helmet, the sound a cheap clip makes when the spring inside it has finally had enough.

She did not look up at once. Twelve years on the Ring had taught her that a sound in a suit could be anything. Ice crystals off your own visor. Your own heart. The hardsuit's joints settling as the cold got into them. You looked up for every tick and you never got a coupling seated, and the Company docked you for that too.

Then Kostya said, quite conversationally, "Oh," and she looked up.

He was a little above her and to the left, where he had swung out on his line to guide the cassette in. The cassette was forty tonnes of gold-laced ceramic and steel the size of a shuttle hangar, and it hung in the cradle of the tug's arms a hand's width off the Ring's inner face, waiting for them to seat it. Kostya had been riding its leading edge, calling the gap. He was still close enough to touch it. He reached for it. His glove went past the edge by about the width of a finger.

"Line," Sol said. "Kostya. Line."

"It's on," he said. "It's—" and she watched him look down at his own chest, where the tether ran to the clip on his harness, and watched the tether float there in a slow loop with nothing on the end of it, the clip still closed on the hardpoint behind him, the line's eye torn clean out of the clip's throat.

He was moving away from the cassette at less than a meter a second. It did not look like falling. It looked like a man who had decided to step back and get a better view of the work.

"Teague," Sol said. "Teague, I need a line on him."

"I see him." Teague was at the far end of the cassette, the gang's tether man, sixty-three years old, and he had a coil of spare line on his hip because Teague always had a coil of spare line on his hip. She heard him breathing hard, the wet catch in it that he said was nothing. "Kostya. Arms in, son. Don't spin."

"I'm not spinning."

"You will if you flap. Arms in."

Teague threw. Sol watched the line go out, a thin pale snake uncoiling across the black, the weighted end turning over once, twice, and she watched it reach the end of its length and stop, three meters short, and come back, and Teague say a word she had never heard him say in eight years.

"Juno," Sol said. "Bring the tug."

"I've got the cassette."

"Drop it on the face. Set it down and come get him."

"If I set it down crooked it'll crack the—"

"I don't care what it cracks. Set it down."

There was a pause on the channel that was not Juno thinking. Sol knew Juno's pauses. This one was Juno's hands already moving on the board.

"Platform, this is *Patience*," Juno said on the open channel, in her calm pilot's voice, the one she used when she was most frightened. "I've got a man off the line at Segment Two-Eleven. I'm setting down cassette Two-Eleven-B on the face and breaking to retrieve. Advise."

The answer took eleven seconds, which was the time it took for the duty officer on the platform to call someone who could say no.

"*Patience*, Platform. Negative. Hold the cassette."

"Platform, say again. I've got a man off the line."

"We heard you, *Patience*." It was not the duty officer now. It was a man's voice, unhurried, with the flat clipped vowels of the Proctor school on Ostrava. Sol had heard it on the channel a hundred times and in person twice, both times at a disciplinary. Proctor-Captain Lusk. "Release of cassette Two-Eleven-B outside of a seating cycle is not authorized. Retrieval in free drift requires a certified retrieval crew. A retrieval crew is being tasked."

"How long," said Sol.

"Identify yourself."

"Dace. Gang chief, Four. How long."

"The retrieval crew is on rest cycle. They'll be tasked at shift change."

Shift change was nine hours away. A rigger's hardsuit carried six hours of air and a forty-minute reserve, and Kostya had been out for three of them already.

"Juno," Sol said, on the gang channel.

"I'm trying." Juno's voice had lost its calm. "They've got the drive. Sol, Control's got the drive. I've got the arms and I've got attitude and I've got nothing else. They've locked me out."

Kostya was twelve meters out now. Fifteen. He had his arms in, as Teague had told him. He was turning very slowly, not a spin, just the long patient roll of a body with nothing to push on, so that now they saw his visor and now the back of his helmet and now his visor again, the platform lights sliding across it gold and white.

"Chain," Sol said. "Everybody. Chain."

They did it the way they had drilled it and never once used it, because the Company's retrieval protocol said it was not to be used: Teague's spare coil and the two spares Juno kicked out of the tug's locker and their own working lines unclipped one at a time, each of them hanging on a single short lanyard while they did it, which the protocol also said was not to be done, all of it clipped end to end, Bracken anchored on the cassette cradle with his boots locked and his great arms wrapped around a strut, then Teague paying out, then Sol on the end. Five lines of thirty meters each, minus the bights for the clips. A hundred and twenty meters, if everything held. She went out along it hand over hand with her heart going so hard she could hear it in her ears over the hiss of the suit fans, and she did not look at the clips as she passed them, because she knew what they were. Lindqvist clips. Thirty marks a hundred from a factory on Halvard Four. Dov had had a Lindqvist clip.

At the end of the chain she let herself float out to the full stretch of it and held out her arm, as though that would add anything.

Kostya was sixty meters past her fingers.

"Hey, chief," he said.

"Hey."

"I can see the whole thing from here. I never saw the whole thing."

She turned her head, because he was right, and from the end of the chain she could see it too. The Ring. Thirty-eight kilometers across, and from here, out from the face, you could see the curve of it running away on both sides, a band of dark metal with the inner face laced over in phase-lines like frost on a window, if frost were made of gold, and lit along its length by the work lights of the other gangs, little stars crawling on it. And inside the Ring, filling it, filling everything, Kettle.

Kettle was a brown dwarf, which the Combine's brochures had described as *a failed star*, and which the riggers called a lot of things, none of them failed. It was the color of a bruise four days old: purple and oxblood and a deep sour yellow, banded, turning, with storms in its bands the size of worlds. Lightning ran under the cloud tops in long slow sheets you could watch with the naked eye. When Kettle flared, which it did every few weeks, the radiation alarms went in every suit on the Ring and the gangs went in behind the steel and sat in the dark and played cards until it passed. When it did not flare it simply hung there, two hundred thousand kilometers down, and filled half the sky, and you worked with it at your back, and you did not think about it, any more than you thought about the Company.

Far off past Kettle's limb, very small, the system's real star burned, a dim red dwarf the survey had named Hearth, the color of a coal someone had forgotten to bank.

"It's ugly," Kostya said. "I always thought it was ugly. It's not."

"No," Sol said.

"Chief, how much air have I got."

She could see his suit telemetry on her visor. Everyone's suit sent to the gang chief. She had looked at it the second his clip went.

"Two hours forty," she said. "And reserve."

"Retrieval crew's in nine."

"They might come sooner."

"Chief."

"Yes," she said.

He laughed. It was a good laugh, a little shaky. "That's what I thought."

* * *

They seated cassette Two-Eleven-B at the fifty-third minute of the third hour of second shift, because the cassette was still there and still hanging a hand's width off the face and the Company had not tasked anyone else to seat it, and because Sol could not think of anything to do with her hands that was not that.

They seated it the way they seated everything, which was by the Count.

A coupling on the Ring was held by ten bolts in a star pattern around a collar a meter across, and if you torqued the bolts in order round the circle the collar went on crooked and the phase-lines would not meet, and the Ring would not light, and some Company engineer would come out in a clean suit with a clean tablet and tell you so and dock you for the rework. So you torqued them across the star: one, then the bolt opposite, then the one two places round, then its opposite, and so on, so the collar came down even. Every gang on the Ring had its own way of keeping the order. Gang Four sang it.

"One," Sol said.

"For the hook," said Teague, and Bracken, and Juno over the tug channel, and she put the driver on the first bolt and felt it bite.

"Two."

"And two for the line."

It was not much of a song. Somebody had made it up on the Ostrava docks a hundred years ago or a hundred and fifty, nobody knew, and riggers had carried it out to every yard the Combine had ever built, and it had picked up verses and lost them, and in Gang Four it went like this:

*One for the hook and two for the line,*  
*three for the dog and four for the time,*  
*five for the air and six for the seal,*  
*seven the hand on the cold steel wheel,*  
*eight for the dark and nine for the drop,*  
*ten for the Company — ten, and stop.*

And then the gang, all together, the bit that went between rounds while the driver was reset to the next torque value:

*Count it up and count it slow,*  
*count it back down and home we go.*

They did it three times, three rounds of torque, and she called every number and every number got its answer, and on the third round when she said "Ten," Bracken, who almost never spoke, said very loud, "And damn the Company," which was the old way, the dock way, the way you were not supposed to do it on an open channel. Nobody told him not to.

Kostya had been singing with them. Sol had heard his voice the first round, faint and thin behind theirs on the gang channel, a quarter-beat late. The second round he was quieter. The third round she called *eight* and listened and did not hear him.

When the collar was down and the cassette's phase-lines met the Ring's in a hairline seam of gold that you could not have slid a fingernail into, she let go of the driver and let it float on its lanyard and looked up.

He was a long way out now. Past the reach of the work lights. She could only see him because his suit's own lamps were on, two small white points, turning, turning.

"Kostya," she said.

"Nice seat," he said. His voice was very close and very quiet in her helmet. "I heard. I couldn't keep up."

"You did fine."

"I want to say something to my mother."

"Go ahead. I'm recording."

So he talked for a while, to his mother, who lived on a terraced farm on Lemberg Station with the rest of his family, and was owed, he said, sixteen hundred marks of his wages under the remittance clause, which she was to be sure to claim, because they would try not to pay it. He told her about the Ring. He told her Kettle was not ugly. He told her about a girl on Lemberg he had meant to write to and had not, and asked his mother to tell her, and then laughed and said not to, actually, it would only make things strange. He talked about his brothers. He said he was not frightened, and then he said that he was, and then he said it was not as bad as he had thought it would be, and that the stars out here were very bright, much brighter than at home, and that he could not see the Ring's lights anymore but he could see the platform, and Kettle, and the stars.

Sol listened to all of it with her hand on the cassette and the rest of the gang silent on the channel. Juno was crying. You could always tell, with Juno, because she went very precise.

At some point Kostya said, "Chief?"

"I'm here."

"I've got about forty minutes. Of the regular."

"I know."

"I don't want to do the reserve. I don't want to do the bit where it gets thick."

"You don't have to," Sol said. She had known what he was going to ask before he asked it. Everybody on the Ring knew how, and nobody said it out loud. The suit's purge valve. The emergency vent for a fire in the suit. Two turns and a pull, and it was quick, the old riggers said, quicker than you would think. "You don't have to do anything you don't want."

"Would you stay on? Till."

"Yes."

"Would everybody—" and his voice wobbled and then came back. "No. Actually. Can you all go off the channel? Just the chief. I don't want everybody to hear."

They went off. She heard each of them go, a small click: Juno, Bracken, Teague last of all, Teague saying "Good man, Kostya," before his click. Then there was just the hiss of the carrier and Kostya's breathing.

"Chief, you've got sixty-something days, right? Left."

"Fifty-seven."

"Fifty-seven. Christ. You're nearly out."

"Nearly."

"What are you going to do?"

She had not thought about it. That was the truth. For twelve years she had not let herself think about it, because thinking about it made the days longer. She thought about it now, for him.

"There's a bar on the Ostrava docks," she said. "Pier Six, under the old cranes. They do a fish soup. Real fish, from the tanks, with the bones still in. I'm going to sit in there and have a bowl of fish soup and nobody's going to tell me when to get up."

"That's it?"

"That's it."

"That's a good plan," Kostya said. "I like that plan. Have one for me."

"I will."

"Okay," he said. "Okay. I'm going to turn it off now, chief. The radio. I'll do the rest by myself."

"All right."

"Thank you for the chain. That was a good chain."

"It was too short."

"Still," he said, and then the carrier went, and there was only silence, the special silence of a channel with nobody on it, and she looked out at the two small white points of his suit lamps turning, a long way out past the work lights, and watched them until she could not tell them from the stars.

* * *

Quartermaster Fenn came to the bunkroom at the end of the shift.

He did not have to. There was a terminal by the bunkroom door, as there was by every bunkroom door in Hab Ring C, and the Company's adjustments appeared on it like weather, and you read them or you did not. But Fenn liked to come in person when it was a big one. He said it was a courtesy. He stood in the door of Bay Fourteen with his tablet held against his chest like a hymnal and his clean grey coverall zipped to the collar, and he looked at the five of them sitting on the lower bunks still in their suit liners, still smelling of suit, and he cleared his throat.

"I'm sorry about your man," he said.

Nobody answered him.

"The retrieval crew went out at shift change, as tasked. They weren't able to recover. He'd gone further than the projection. The suit's beacon had—" He glanced at the tablet. "The beacon was off."

"He turned it off," said Juno, very precisely.

"Yes, well. That's against protocol, obviously, but under the circumstances, nobody's going to—it's not going in the record against him. I made sure of that." Fenn looked, for a moment, as though he expected to be thanked. "The other thing is the equipment."

Sol had known it was coming. She had known it since the clip went. Still, she heard herself say, "The equipment."

"One rigger hardsuit, type nine, with helmet and pack, unrecovered. Replacement value eighteen thousand four hundred marks. Under the bond schedule, loss of Company equipment in the course of gang operations is charged to the gang."

"The clip failed," Sol said. "The *clip*. It's a Lindqvist. It's thirty marks a hundred. The eye pulled through the throat. You can see it. The line's still on the hardpoint at Two-Eleven; I left it there. Go and look at it."

"There'll be an inquiry. If the inquiry finds a defect in Company equipment, the charge will be reversed. You're entitled to file a request."

"Dov filed sixteen."

Fenn's face did something complicated. He had been on the platform as long as she had. He had come to Bay Fourteen three years ago, too, with his tablet against his chest, and stood in that door.

"I can't speak to that," he said. "The charge is spread across the gang. Five members, including the deceased. The deceased's share is waived. So four shares." He looked at the tablet. "At bonded rates, four thousand six hundred marks a share is four days added to each bond."

"Four days," said Teague, from his bunk. He said it mildly, the way he said most things. He was sitting with his hands on his knees, and every so often he coughed, a dry deep cough, and spat into a cloth he kept for the purpose. Glass lung. The dust off the cassette ceramic got into everything. "Four days each. For a man's suit."

"For the suit," said Fenn. "Not for the man. Obviously."

"Obviously," said Teague.

"It's on your terminals." Fenn hesitated. He looked as though he wanted to say something else, and then he looked at Sol, and whatever it was he did not say it. "There's a replacement coming in on tonight's shuttle. A transfer from the Tamsin yards. He'll take the open bunk."

"The open bunk," said Juno, "is Kostya's."

"Yes," said Fenn. "That's why it's open." And he went.

They sat for a while without talking. The bunkroom was four meters by six, with three bunks against each long wall, three high, a table bolted to the floor in the middle with a cribbage board burned into its top, and a single hot plate in the corner on which Bracken, who had worked the ice mines on Halvard Four before this and could cook anything on anything, sometimes made a stew out of the protein ration that you could almost believe had once been an animal. There was a small round port at the end, triple-glazed, scratched milky on the outside by twelve years of micrometeoroid dust, through which, if you put your face right up to it, you could see a slice of Kettle. Somebody before Sol's time had stenciled C-14 above the door in Company yellow, and somebody after that had scratched under it, very small, *abandon hope*. Nobody had ever painted it out.

Juno got up and went to Kostya's bunk, the middle one on the left, and began to take his things out of the net. There was not much. A spare liner. A comb. A little carved animal, a goat, he had been whittling for his youngest brother out of a scrap of packing foam. A paper book of puzzles, all done. She folded the liner and put the comb and the goat and the book inside it and tied the arms around it like a parcel, and stood holding it.

"Remittance clause," Sol said. "He said his mother's owed sixteen hundred under the remittance clause."

"They'll say the four days come out of it," Juno said. "You know they will. They'll say his share was waived, and then they'll take it out of the remittance. They'll find a line in the schedule."

"Then we write her," said Teague. "We write her and tell her what to ask for."

"The mail's read."

"Then we write it so it reads all right."

Sol stood up. She went to her own bunk, the bottom one on the right, nearest the port, and sat on it, and turned to the wall.

The wall by her bunk was the same pale grey Company polymer as every wall on the platform, but by her bunk, at the height of her head when she lay down, it was covered in marks. She had scratched them with the point of a rigger's scribe on her first night aboard, twelve years ago: a mark for every week of her bond, six hundred and twenty-four of them, in rows. She had crossed one off every week. At the end of last year, when there were few enough of them left that she could bear it, she had started again in a new place lower down, smaller, a mark for every day.

Fifty-seven of them this morning, in neat rows of ten, and seven at the end. She had crossed off this morning's when she woke.

She took the scribe out of the thigh pocket of her liner, where she always kept it, and scratched four more on the end of the last row. They were not as neat as the others. Her hand was not quite steady.

Sixty-one.

"It's four days," Juno said behind her. "Sol. It's only four days."

"I know."

"They do it to everyone."

"I know." She put the scribe away. Then she reached up into the net over her bunk and took down her flask.

It was the one good thing she owned. A rigger's flask, Company issue, the old heavy kind they had stopped making: steel inside and out with a vacuum between, dented on one shoulder from a fall she had taken in her first year, stamped on the base TTH-9 · 0417, her platform and her bond number. They had given it to her the day she signed. She had been twenty-nine. She had thought, at the time, that it was a kind of welcome. It held a liter of anything and kept it hot for a whole shift, and every shift for twelve years she had filled it in the galley with chicory, because the coffee was charged at bonded rates and the chicory was free, and drunk it through the feed in her helmet during the dark part of the shift when the cold got into her hands.

She unscrewed the cap. There was a mouthful left in it, gone cold since morning. She drank it. It tasted of what it always tasted of, which was burnt roots and steel.

"He wanted me to have a fish soup for him," she said. "At Pier Six."

"I'll come with you," Juno said. "When you go. I'm only eleven months behind you. I'll be there before the soup's cold."

"It'll be cold."

"Then I'll have mine cold."

Bracken got up and went to the hot plate and began, without a word, to make something.

* * *

The shuttle came in at the second hour of third shift. Sol heard it dock through the floor, the long grinding shudder of the clamps, the hollow boom of the lock cycling. She was lying on her bunk with her face to the marks. She did not sleep. Nobody in Bay Fourteen slept, except possibly Bracken, who could sleep anywhere and did so with his arms folded on his chest like a corpse in a church.

At the fourth hour there was a knock on the door.

Nobody knocked on bunkroom doors. Proctors did not knock and riggers did not need to. Sol lay and listened to it and thought that she was not going to get up, and then Juno said "Oh for God's sake," and Sol got up.

The boy in the corridor was nineteen if he was a day, and he looked younger. He had a kit bag over one shoulder and a Company-issue liner on that was too big for him in the shoulders and too short in the legs, and his hair was cut very close to his skull in the transport-ship way, so you could see the shape of his head, which was long and narrow and somehow anxious. He was dark-skinned and thin and had the kind of hands Sol had learned, over twelve years, to look at first: big hands, out of proportion with the rest of him, the knuckles cracked from cold. Farm hands. Somebody had put him to work outside, young.

"Is this Fourteen?" he said. "C-Fourteen? They said Gang Four was C-Fourteen."

"It says so over the door."

He looked up at the stencil, and under it at the scratching, and she watched him read it and decide not to say anything about it.

"I'm Okafor," he said. "Pell Okafor. They told me to report to the gang chief. Dace?"

"That's me."

"I'm assigned to you."

"I know."

"From Tamsin's Reach. The yards there. I was on the—I did pressure work at the grain silos before that, and then six months at the Tamsin yards in a rigging school, and I've done forty hours outside, suited, and I know—" He stopped. "They said there was an opening."

"There's a bunk," Sol said. "Middle on the left. It was emptied about six hours ago."

He looked past her, into the bunkroom, at the middle bunk on the left, which Juno had stripped and Bracken had wiped down, so that it was just a pale grey shelf with a thin pad on it and the net above it empty. He looked at it for long enough that she knew somebody on the shuttle had told him.

"I'm sorry," he said. "About your man."

"So's the Company. They put four days on all our bonds to show how sorry they were."

He did not know what to say to that, so he did not say anything, which she gave him a small mark for.

"Come in," she said. "Don't touch the hot plate. Don't touch Bracken. Shift's at the sixth hour, you'll be on the Ring with us, and if you do one thing on the Ring I don't tell you to do I will cut your line myself. You understand?"

"Yes, chief."

"Do you know what a Lindqvist clip is?"

"It's a—it's the standard harness clip, the—"

"In the morning I'll show you how to check one. You'll check yours every time you clip in. You'll check it if you unclip to scratch your nose. You'll check it in your sleep." She stood aside. "Go on."

He went in with his kit bag and stood by the empty bunk as though waiting for it to give him permission. Then he put the bag in the net and sat down on the edge of the pad and put his big cracked hands on his knees, and she saw that they were shaking a little, and that he was trying to stop them.

Juno looked at him from her bunk, up top on the right, with her chin on her arms.

"Tamsin's Reach," she said. "That's the agri-world, the one with the—what is it—"

"Orchards," said Pell. "Mostly orchards. Pears."

"Pears," said Juno. "God. What did you sign for?"

"Twelve years."

"No, I mean what for. What's the debt."

"Oh." He looked at his hands. "My father's land. There was a blight. The note came due."

"So it's twelve years on the Ring for a pear orchard."

"For my family's place in it," he said, and his chin came up a little, which Sol also gave him a mark for. "My sisters are still there. They get to stay."

"Good," Juno said, and meant it, and put her head down.

Sol lay down again with her face to the wall and her flask beside her on the pad. After a while the lights went down to the night setting, blue, and the platform made its night sounds: the far deep hum of the reactor, the ticking of the hull, the air plant cycling, and once, a long way off, the clang of a door. Bracken snored. Teague coughed, and spat, and coughed. Somebody was crying very quietly, and she thought it was Juno, and then she realized it was not; it was the new boy, in the middle bunk on the left, with his face pressed into the pad so that no one would hear.

She did not say anything to him. It would not have helped. The first night was the first night, and everybody had one.

After a while the crying stopped and was replaced by something else, so faint she thought for a moment it was the air plant. Then she realized the boy was singing. Under his breath, to himself, the way you sing to keep the dark off when you are small. It was a farm song, something about a cart and a river. She did not know it. It had a good tune.

She reached out in the blue dark and found the marks on the wall with her fingertips, and ran them along the rows, ten and ten and ten, the way she did every night, feeling the little ridges the scribe had raised in the polymer. Sixty-one. She found the four new ones at the end, rougher than the rest. She left them alone.

Sixty-one days. Then the hauler. The *Ostrava Mercy*, due on Day Three Hundred, to take the finished crews home before the Lighting. Then eleven weeks in the hauler's hold, and then the docks, and Pier Six under the old cranes, and a bowl of fish soup with the bones still in it.

She thought about it. For the first time in twelve years she let herself think about it all the way through, the way Kostya had asked her to. It did not make the days longer. It did not make them shorter either. It only made her very tired.

"Chief?" said the boy, in the dark.

"What."

"Sorry. I didn't know you were awake."

"I'm not."

"Is it true you can see the whole Ring from out on the face? All of it at once?"

She thought about Kostya, sixty meters past the end of the chain, saying *I never saw the whole thing.*

"Only if you're a long way out," she said. "Go to sleep, Okafor."

He went quiet. In the blue dark the marks were under her fingers. Somewhere above the platform, two hundred thousand kilometers over Kettle and turning slowly in the dark, there was a type nine hardsuit with its lamps still burning, and it would go on burning, the riggers said, for about a year, until the cells ran down.

# Chapter 2: The Lottery

*The Concord · 336 AL*

Every morning of his working life Idris Vey had begun in the same way, with his palm flat on the cold brass rail of the Spindle's outer gallery and the words of the Keepers' invocation in his mouth.

"One for the hook," he said, "and two for the line. Three for the dog and four for the time."

It was not a long prayer. Novices joked that it was the shortest in the Concord's whole liturgy and therefore the most efficient, which was the kind of joke Keepers made, being engineers first and priests a long way second whatever the Lightwardens liked to pretend. Nobody knew what it meant. The hook was the Keepers' sigil, worked in white thread on the breast of every grey habit in the order and cast in steel above the door of every Keeper chapel in the Reach; the line was the braided cord Idris wore round his neck and crossed over his chest, the stole of his office. The dog and the time were mysteries. Archkeeper Sallow said they were the oldest words in the Concord, older than the Book of the Lighting, older than the Founders' Roll, carried down from the hands that had raised the Ring, and that a Keeper did not need to understand a prayer to say it truly, any more than he needed to understand Kettle to orbit it.

Idris said it, and took his hand off the rail, and looked down.

From the outer gallery of the Spindle you could see the Eye. That was what the gallery was for; it had been built, three hundred years ago, so that the Keepers could stand where they stood now and look into the thing they tended. The Spindle rose nine hundred meters off the rim of the Ring like the gnomon of a sundial, and the gallery was a band of glass three-quarters of the way up, and below it and beyond it the Ring curved away on either hand, thirty-eight kilometers of dark metal crusted with three centuries of city—Tithe, nine million souls, spokes and wards and towers and docks grown along the rim like coral on a reef—until the two arms of the curve met again on the far side, small with distance, a bracelet of lights.

And inside the bracelet, the Eye.

People who had never seen it asked what it looked like, and people who had seen it all their lives could never tell them. Idris had tried, once, writing to a cousin on Brine. *Like a well*, he had written, *if you looked down a well and saw the sky at the bottom of it, a different sky, and the water were made of light.* That was not right either. It was a disk of light filling the Ring from rim to rim, blue-white at the edges and deepening toward the center into a color there was no word for, a color that was not quite violet and not quite black, and it moved: not as water moves, nor as fire, but as a held breath moves in a chest, a slow swell and settling that the Keepers called the Eye's respiration and measured to the ten-thousandth of a cycle. Through it, if you looked long enough, you could see other stars. Not Kettle's stars. The stars of the Inward, a thousand light-years off: the sky over Ostrava, where the Eye's sister burned in her own ring and the Houses kept their courts.

Ships went through it. That was the point of it, and you forgot that, looking. At this hour of the morning there was an ore-train going outward, nine linked barges from the Gall mines riding down the approach lane on their tug's plume, small and black against the light; and coming the other way, just surfacing from the Eye's center like a fish rising, a House courier, white and slender, its running lights winking gold. Idris watched it rise. He could feel it on the governor before he saw it on the gauges: a tiny change in the Spindle's voice, a deepening, the whole tower taking the mass of the courier the way a bridge takes the weight of a cart. Then the courier was clear and the Eye settled, and the tower's voice went back to its morning note.

He checked the gauges anyway. The gallery's inner wall was a long bank of them, brass-cased and glass-faced, polished every morning by novices who were not allowed to touch anything else: the phase meters, the respiration count, the mass-balance dials that told the Keepers how much the Eye had swallowed and spat out since the last bell. He read them all in order, as he had been taught, and wrote them in the day-book in his small square hand, and initialed the page. *Phase nominal. Respiration nominal. Mass-balance within limits. Light holds.* Then he turned, as every Keeper turned at the end of the morning rite, to face the grille.

It was set into the inner wall at the end of the gallery, where the gallery curved inward to meet the Inner Housing: a panel of brass lattice a little taller than a man, green at its edges, bright as gold in the middle where three centuries of Keepers' hands had touched it. Behind it, mounted in the steel face of the Housing, was the Knot.

It was not much to look at. Idris had thought that the first time he saw it, at fifteen, a novice in a habit too long for him; and he had thought it with a small private shock, because he had been expecting something like the Eye, something that would make his heart turn over. The Knot was a lump of old steel. The head of something, a cylinder as thick as a man's thigh, standing a hand's width out from the Housing's face and ending in a squared boss with a deep socket in it, the kind of socket a heavy wrench would fit. Its surface was pitted and dark. There were marks on it, small fine scratches, too regular to be accidental and too worn now to read. Beside it in the Housing face was a round plate the size of a cart wheel, held by a ring of ten bolts, painted over and over in the Keepers' grey until the bolt heads were soft lumps under the paint, and sealed across each bolt with a disk of lead stamped with the hook.

*The Knot is the Founders',* said the Rule of the Keepers, chapter one, verse one, in letters cut into the lintel above the grille, *and shall not be turned.*

Nobody knew what it did. Nobody had ever asked, in Idris's hearing, except himself at fifteen, and he had asked the Keeper who was training him, and the Keeper had looked at him with such quiet horror that he had never asked anybody again.

He put his palm flat on the grille where the brass was brightest, and bowed his head, and said the invocation once more, and took his hand away.

"You're early."

Idris turned. Archkeeper Benedek Sallow was coming down the gallery from the lift, slowly, as he did everything now. He was seventy-one and had been a Keeper since before Idris was born, and his habit hung on him like a sail on a windless day, and his line was the old kind, real braided tether cord, grey-white and furred with age, that he said had been his own master's and his master's before that. He walked with a stick of black Brine-ice wood. His face was long and pouched and sorrowful, like an old dog's, and he was the cleverest man Idris knew.

"Levy Day," Idris said. "I wanted to finish before the Square."

"Ah." Sallow came to the rail beside him and looked down at the Eye. "Yes. The girl's eligible this year?"

"She was eligible last year."

"So she was. And not drawn." Sallow nodded slowly. "The Light provides."

Idris said nothing. Sallow glanced at him sideways from under his heavy lids.

"Two thousand from nine million and the moons," the old man said. "Your sister's chances are better than the courier's of coming out of the Eye in pieces. And that has not happened in three hundred and thirty-six years."

"I know the figures."

"I know you know them. I am telling you that I know them too, so you needn't go on carrying them alone." He put his hand on Idris's shoulder, a dry light hand, all bones. "Go to the Square. Stand with her. Come back to me tomorrow and we'll laugh about how pale you looked."

* * *

Basilica Square lay on the old Founders' deck, at the heart of the Hub, where the first platform had been moored to the Ring before there was a city to moor it to. It was not, strictly speaking, a square. It was a vast oval floor of white stone—real stone, quarried on Gall and shipped up at a cost no one would ever name—open to a dome of glass so high that clouds formed under it in the dry season, and at its northern end the Basilica of the Light rose in tiers of white and gold toward the dome, and before the Basilica, on a plinth taller than three men, stood the Founders.

There were nine of them, cast in bronze gone black and green with three centuries of breath. Men and women in the long coats of the old Combine, standing shoulder to shoulder, their faces turned up and outward toward the glass, toward the Eye that you could see through the dome if you stood in the right place; and in the hands of the foremost of them, raised over all their heads, a lamp. Not a real lamp, not the kind anybody had used in a thousand years. A storybook lamp, a lantern, with a flame of gilt bronze. The Light.

Under them, cut deep in the plinth's face, the Founders' Roll: forty-one names, the directors and officers and engineers of the Combine who had raised the Ring and lit it. Schoolchildren in the Reach learned them by heart and recited them on Firstlight, the anniversary of the Lighting, which was the new year. Idris had learned them, and recited them, and forgotten most of them as adults did, but some stayed. There was a Castellane, whose name was first. There was a Haddad, whom the Book of the Lighting called *the Lightbringer's Hand*, near the bottom. There was a Lind. He could see them from where he stood, a long way off over the heads of the crowd, cut in letters the size of his forearm and gilded, catching the light.

And under the Roll, cut smaller, the words every child in the Reach knew before they knew their own name: *THEY LIT THE EYE, AND THE REACH WAS BORN INTO THE LIGHT. FOR THE LIGHT, WE OWE.*

The crowd filled the Square from wall to wall. Every soul in Tithe between sixteen and forty who was not a Lightwarden, a Keeper, or a House servant was required to stand in the Square or a ward chapel on Levy Day, and the Square was where the Third Spoke stood, and the Fourth, and half the Sump. They stood in their ward blocks between lines of white cord strung on brass posts, as though for a procession, with Wardens' Guard in their white-and-gold half-armor posted every twenty paces along the cords. They stood quietly. That was the thing that always struck Idris, every year, because he was not required to be there and came anyway: the quiet. Two hundred thousand people in one place, and you could hear the air plant breathing in the vents under the floor.

Lark stood beside him with her hands in her pockets.

She was seventeen and she was nearly as tall as he was now, which still surprised him every time he stood beside her; in his head she was six, small and furious in a black mourning smock that had been let down twice, refusing to hold his hand at their mother's pyre. She had their mother's long jaw and their father's black eyebrows, which met in the middle when she frowned, which was often. She had cut her hair herself the week before, with a kitchen knife, he suspected, short and ragged so that it stood up at the crown, and she had dyed one lock of it the red of a Levy cord, which in the Third Spoke on Levy Day was the kind of joke that got you looked at. People were looking at her. She was ignoring them, which was the thing Lark did best in the world.

"You don't have to stand with me," she said, without looking at him. "You're not eligible. You can go and stand with the Keepers on the steps."

"I want to stand here."

"You'll get your habit dirty."

"It's grey. It doesn't show."

She almost smiled, and didn't, and looked up at the Basilica steps instead.

The Prelate was there. Most Luminous Ottilie Marsh, head of the Concord of the Reach, Lightwarden of Lightwardens, a small round woman in white and gold vestments so heavy with thread that two acolytes walked behind her holding up their hem. She had a pleasant motherly face and a pleasant motherly voice and Idris had heard her, once, at a Keepers' council, order a man's pension stripped from his widow for a clerical error in his transit logs, in exactly that voice. She stood at the top of the steps, beside the Drum.

And a little behind her and to her left, in black, stood Factor Severs.

Idris had seen Lucan Severs perhaps a dozen times in the four years since House Ostrava had sent him through the Eye to sit in the Factor's House in the upper wards. He was a tall narrow man, younger than you expected, with a long pale face and pale hair cut close, and he wore the plain black of the Inward Houses, unornamented except for a small gold disk at his throat: the House's seal, a ring with a line through it. He did not stand like a man at a ceremony. He stood like a man watching an inventory. He had a slate in one hand, and once, while the Prelate was speaking, he looked down at it and touched something on its face, and then looked up again.

The Prelate spoke for a long time. She spoke about the Founders, and the Lighting, and the Debt. She spoke of the Founders' sacrifice, and of the great cost of raising the Ring, which the Founders had borne out of their own substance, out of love for the Reach that did not yet exist, so that it might be born into the Light; and of how the Reach, which owed its every breath to the Eye, owed also its share of that cost, the Founding Debt, which the Houses of the Inward held in trust; and of how the Levy was the Reach's honor, the means by which its children went Inward in service to that Debt, and returned—she said *returned*—enriched in skill and grace. Idris had heard it every year of his life. He watched Lark's face while the Prelate spoke. It did not move at all.

Then the acolytes turned the Drum.

The Drum was a sphere of Gall crystal two meters through, clear as water, hung in a gilt frame on a horizontal axle, and inside it were four million small brass disks, one for every soul in the Reach of Levy age, each stamped with a name and a ward-number. When it turned, the disks inside it rolled and slid over one another with a sound like surf, a long hissing roar that filled the Square, and that was the only loud sound on Levy Day, and every year Idris felt it in his teeth. They turned it nine times. Then they stopped it, and a Lightwarden reached into the opening at its base, where the disks fell one at a time through a gate the width of a single coin, and took out the first disk, and handed it to the crier.

"Ward Nine of the First Spoke," said the crier into the voice-horn. "Annora Weil."

Somewhere off to the left, in the First Spoke's block, there was a small sound, not a scream, a kind of gasp. Then a Guard was walking through the cord toward it.

It took a long time. Two thousand names. The crier read them in a level unhurried voice, and after each name there was the small sound somewhere in the Square, or sometimes no sound, and the Guard walking. Some of the drawn walked out to meet the Guard themselves, with their chins up; Idris saw one young man in a dock-loader's vest walk out and hold out his wrist for the cord before the Guard had reached him, as if to say, *there, I'm not afraid of you*, and saw his hand shaking as he held it out. Some had to be fetched. Twice there was shouting, and a scuffle, and the Guard closed in and the shouting stopped. Once, near the Sump block, someone ran: a girl, Lark's age, ducking under the cords and running for the eastern gate with her hair flying, and the whole Square seemed to lean toward her, holding its breath, and two Guards caught her twenty paces short of the gate and brought her down on the white stone, and the Square let its breath out, and the crier, who had paused, read the next name.

The red cords went on wrists. Idris watched them go on. Each Guard carried a hank of them on his belt, bright red braided silk, and he tied it round the drawn wrist in a particular knot, quick and neat, the way a Keeper ties off a line. It was not a binding. It came off with a sharp knife. But no one in the Reach would sell you food or rent you a bunk or let you onto a ship with a red cord on your wrist, and if they found you without it they would put your name back in the Drum for the rest of your life, and your family's names with it.

The Third Spoke's turn came in the second hour.

"Ward Two of the Third Spoke," said the crier. "Benedikt Aro."

A sound, on the far side of the block.

"Ward Six of the Third Spoke. Mallory Teng."

Lark had taken her hands out of her pockets. Idris did not know when she had done it. She stood with her arms loose at her sides and her chin a little raised, and looked at the Drum.

"Ward Four of the Third Spoke. Calloway Hesk."

Their ward. A man in front of them, three rows forward, went slowly to his knees, and the woman beside him put her arms round his shoulders and held on.

"Ward Four of the Third Spoke," said the crier, and Idris knew, he knew before the crier's mouth moved, the way he knew a ship's mass on the governor before the gauges showed it, a deepening, a weight arriving. "Lark Vey."

There was no sound from Lark at all.

He heard a sound, and realized after a moment it had come from him.

"Lark—"

"Don't." She did not look at him. "Don't, Idris."

The Guard was coming. A big man, older, with a grey moustache, the hank of red cord on his belt half used up. People got out of his way. He came to the cord in front of them and stopped and looked along the row until he found the girl with the red lock in her hair, and Idris watched his eyes go to it, and narrow, and something in his face go hard.

"Lark Vey," he said. "Ward Four."

"I heard."

"Your wrist."

Lark held it out over the cord. Her left wrist, thin and brown, with the little scar on the inside of it where she had cut herself on a tin at nine. Idris saw that her hand was perfectly steady, steadier than the dock-loader's, steadier than his own, and that she was looking the Guard straight in the face.

The Guard tied the cord. Quick and neat. He gave the knot a little tug to set it, and as he did he looked at the red lock of hair again, and said, low, not unkindly, "Bit early for that, girl."

"Not really," Lark said, "as it turns out," and laughed.

It was not a loud laugh. But in the silence of the Square it carried, and Idris saw heads turn. Saw, on the Basilica steps far off, a small pale face in black glance down from its slate, as though at a column of figures that did not quite balance, and then glance back.

* * *

Their rooms were on the forty-first level of the Third Spoke's middle wards, two rooms and a cook-niche with a window that looked out, not at the Eye, but at the back of the Fourth Spoke's water tower, a great rust-streaked cylinder sweating condensation, on which someone a generation ago had painted a Founder with a lamp, now so faded that only the lamp remained, a smear of gold on the rust. Idris had been born in these rooms. So had Lark. Their father had carried them up the stairs from the Keepers' clinic, both times, wrapped in his habit, their mother walking behind.

Lark sat on the window ledge with her knees drawn up and the red cord on her wrist and looked at the gold smear on the tower. She had not spoken on the long walk back from the Square, through the corridors full of quiet people, through the lift where an old woman had looked at the cord on Lark's wrist and then at Idris's habit and then very carefully at nothing. She had not spoken while he made tea, nor when he put it beside her on the ledge. It was going cold there now.

"I'm going to fix it," he said.

She didn't answer.

"I mean it. There are ways. You know there are ways. The Levy Office takes petitions for hardship, for dependents—"

"I'm not a dependent. I'm seventeen."

"For essential service, then. The Keepers can claim essential service. If you were a novice—"

"You want me to take the habit." She looked at him at last. "To get out of the Levy. You want me to take vows to the Light so I don't have to go and pay the Light's bill."

"I want you not to go Inward for twenty years."

"Twenty years and then I come back enriched in skill and grace." She said it in the Prelate's voice, the motherly voice, and it was so exact that in another hour he would have laughed. "Do you know anybody who came back, Idris? Anybody? You know everybody. You know all the Keepers and all the Keepers' families and half the clerks in the Hub. Name me one person who went in the Levy and came home."

He thought. He tried to think.

"Calloway Hesk's uncle," he said. "I think—"

"Calloway Hesk's uncle came back with no hands," said Lark. "He works the Fourth Spoke's water gate. He opens and closes it with his elbows. You've seen him."

He had seen him.

"That's one," she said. "In my whole life. One."

She turned back to the window. Outside, on the water tower, the gold smear of the Founder's lamp caught the light of someone's window across the shaft and gleamed for a moment and went dull.

"I'll find a way," he said. "I'll go to the Archkeeper tomorrow. And if he won't, I'll go to the Levy Office myself. There are people there who—there are ways it's done, Lark. Everybody knows there are."

"Everybody knows," Lark agreed.

"So I'll do it."

"Everybody's brother says that."

He did not know how to answer. He stood in the middle of the room in his grey habit with the line round his neck and his hands empty.

After a while she began to sing. Very low, to herself, with her forehead against the window glass. It took him a moment to realize what it was, because he had never heard it sung. He had only ever heard it said, in the gallery, in the dawn, with his hand on the brass.

*One for the hook and two for the line,*  
*three for the dog and four for the time—*

He stood still and listened. She did not stop where the invocation stopped. She went on, low and quick, in the sing-song of a child's game.

*—five for the air and six for the seal,*  
*seven the hand on the cold steel wheel,*  
*eight for the dark and nine for the drop,*  
*ten for the Comp'ny, ten and* stop.

She hit the last word hard, a little bounce on it, like a skipping rope coming down.

"Where did you learn that?"

She glanced at him over her shoulder, and for a moment she was six again, caught at something.

"What? It's a skipping song."

"That's the invocation. The first part. That's the Keepers' invocation."

"Is it?" She seemed genuinely surprised, and then not. "Well. Everybody knows it. Everybody below the Spokes. The kids skip to it in the Sump." She looked at him with an odd expression, half pity and half something he could not name. "There's more."

"More?"

"Lots more." She turned back to the glass. "You never asked."

"Where did it come from?"

"How should I know? It's a skipping song, Idris. It came from where skipping songs come from. Somebody's grandmother." She drew her knees up tighter. The red cord on her wrist was very bright against her skin, the brightest thing in the room. "You say it every morning and you don't know what it is. That's funny. Isn't that funny?"

"Lark—"

"I'm tired," she said. "I want to go to bed. You go to the Archkeeper in the morning. You go and fix it."

But she did not go to bed. She sat on the ledge with her forehead against the glass, and he sat at the table behind her with his cold tea, and neither of them moved, and the Third Spoke's lights went down for the night, level by level, until the only lights left were the little gold smear on the water tower and, very faint, reflected in the glass so that it seemed to be inside the room with them, the slow cold breathing of the Eye.

# Chapter 3: The Count for the Dead

*The Dark · Year 419*

We sent Pim out the lock on the ninth day of the cold month, and I sang him home.

That is the work. I am the Tally of Hold Fourteen, which is to say I am the one who remembers, and when a child dies of the rot in Hold Fourteen I am the one who stands at the inner door of the lock with my hand on the wheel and counts him down. That year I was not the Tally yet. I was nineteen and I was the Tally's girl, and the Tally was Quill, who was old and blind and whose voice had gone to a thread that winter, so that for two months now I had done the singing while he stood beside me with his hand on my shoulder and moved his lips along with mine, to keep me true.

Pim was six. His mother had wrapped him in the good cloth, the blue cloth with the gold edge that the Hold keeps for the dead, which is older than anyone and has been patched so many times that there is more patch than cloth. We are not wasteful in Hold Fourteen. The cloth comes back. Before we cycle the lock we unwrap them, at the last, and fold the cloth, and send them out in what they wore. Pim wore a jersey his mother had knitted from unpicked suit-liner, grey, with a red stripe she had got from somewhere, and his feet were bare. They were very small. I tried not to look at them, and then I made myself look at them, because Quill says that is also the work: to see what there is to see and carry it.

The lock at the end of the Fourteen corridor is the old kind, from before the Dark, a round inner door of steel as thick as my arm is long, with a wheel on it the size of a cart's. The outer door you cannot see. Between them is a little round room with a bench, where the dead wait. Pim's mother had carried him in there herself and laid him on the bench and come out again, and now she stood beside me with her hands pressed flat together and pressed against her mouth, and the Hold stood behind her down the corridor, all of us who could stand, which was eighty-one that morning. It had been eighty-two the morning before.

Through the little round port in the inner door you could see him on the bench, in his jersey with the red stripe.

Quill's hand was on my shoulder. It squeezed once. I touched the string at my throat, the Tally's girl's string, white, with no knots in it yet, the way you touch it before every Count so that it knows to listen. Then I put my hands on the wheel.

There is a right way to begin. You take a breath all the way down, into your belly, the way Quill taught me, and you let it out, and on the next breath you begin, and you do not begin loud. You begin as if you are speaking to someone just beside you. Because you are.

"Ten," I sang, "for the Company. Ten, and stop."

Behind me eighty voices came in under mine, low, a sound like the air plant when it is working well.

"Nine for the drop and eight for the dark."

I turned the wheel a quarter turn. That is also the way it is done. A quarter turn for each line, so the door seals as the Count goes down, and by the end it is shut fast and the lock is ready to cycle.

"Seven the hand on the cold steel wheel."

I had my hand on the cold steel wheel. You always do. You feel it every time.

"Six for the seal and five for the air.  
Four for the time and three for the dog.  
Two for the line and one for the hook."

The low numbers do not rhyme. Nobody knows why. It is the oldest part of the Count, the numbers, older than the Dark, older than the Light maybe, and Quill says that is how you know it is true: that the old true things never quite fit together, because they were made for some purpose we do not remember, and only the new false things are smooth. When I was small I used to try to make them rhyme. *Three for the dog and one for the log.* Quill hit me with his stick for that, not hard. He said a Tally keeps the Count. A Tally does not mend it.

Then the turning. The second part, which we call the Door.

"Once to the dark and twice to the light,  
pin it and seat it and hold it tight,  
dog to the notch where the old ones go,  
Kettle beside you and sun below."

The wheel was nearly home. Through the port Pim lay on the bench with his face turned a little toward the door, as if he were listening. His mother made a sound against her hands.

"That is the way that we close the door."

The wheel went home. I felt the dogs of the inner door slide into their seats all round the rim, a deep shudder through the steel and into my hands, into my arms, into my chest.

"Count it back down," I sang, and eighty-one voices sang it with me, and Pim's mother sang it too, because you do, because it is the last thing you can do, "we are home once more."

Then I pulled the lever and the lock cycled, and there was a long sigh behind the door as the air went out of the little room, the Hold's air, the air we make and clean and breathe and breathe again; and then the outer door opened, which you cannot see but you can feel, a kind of lightness, a drop; and then through the port the bench was empty, and beyond the port, if you put your eye close to the glass, there was the Dark.

* * *

The Tallies' room in Hold Fourteen is the oldest room in the Hold. Quill says it may be the oldest room in all of Tithe, older even than the Spindle, and I believe him, because he has never told me a thing that was not true, though he has refused to tell me a great many things. It is small, four long steps by six, with a round port at one end so scratched with age on the outside that it is white as milk and you can see nothing through it at all, only a glow when Kettle is on that side. There are shelves on the long walls, three high, deep, like bunks, which is what Quill says they were once: bunks, for sleeping in, before the Light, when people came out here to build things and slept six to a room. Now they are full of what a Tally keeps. Old slates that do not light. Books, real ones, paper, that I am allowed to touch only with my hands wrapped in cloth. Bundles of string with knots in them that are older than the books and that Quill is the last person in Tithe who can read, and was teaching me to. Bones, some of them. The finger bones of Tallies, which we keep, so that the Count has something to hold onto when we are gone.

Over the door, in yellow paint gone brown, there are some old letters: C-14. That is why we are Hold Fourteen. Under them, scratched into the wall so small you have to stand on the stool to read them, there are two more words in the old letters. Quill says they say *abandon hope*. When I asked him why, he said that the people who made the room were people who had a sense of humor, which he said was a kind of courage, and which he said I did not have yet but might grow into.

And on the wall by the bottom bunk on the right, nearest the port, are the marks.

We call them the First Count. They are rows of little lines scratched into the old grey wall, hundreds of them, in long rows and blocks, very neat, the way you would scratch them if you had time and a good point and nothing else to do. And some of them have a line through, and some do not, and down at the bottom there is a second set, smaller, finer, in rows of ten. Nobody knows who made them or what they count. The Lamplighters say they are the days until the Light comes back. The hullwalkers say they are some old prisoner's sentence. The Tallies say only that they are the First Count, and that every Tally's girl's first lesson is to put her fingers on them, one by one, row by row, and count them, out loud, until she can do it without missing, and then to do it in the dark.

I did that lesson at seven. I can do it now asleep.

After the lock we went back there, Quill and I, because after a lock there are things to write, and because Quill was tired. I helped him down onto the bottom bunk, where he sleeps, under the marks. His hand found them without looking, as it always did; he could not see anything any more, not even the glow of the port, but his fingers went to the wall the way a child's hand goes to its mother's sleeve. He was very thin that winter. I could feel his shoulder-bones through his coat.

"You were good," he said. His voice was all breath now. "The Door was good. You were a little fast on the dog."

"I know."

"You know. Good. Then you know." He lay back. "Get the string."

I got the string. It is Hold Fourteen's death-string, a long cord of braided wire and fiber, very old, wound on a bobbin of black wood, and every soul that goes out the lock from Hold Fourteen has a knot in it. A knot for a child is small. A knot for a grown person is bigger, and a knot for someone who has done something the Hold should remember has a twist in it, and Quill can tell you, from feeling a knot, who it was and how they died and what year of the Dark. He held his hand out, and I put the end of the string in it, and he ran his fingers back along the knots, the last ones, last month's and the month before, eight in the cold month alone, and I watched his lips move as he named them.

"Pim," he said, and held out his other hand for me to tie it, because his fingers would not do a knot any more. I tied it. A small knot. "Nine days into the cold month. Year four hundred and nineteen of the Dark. Six years. The rot."

"The rot," I said. I said it after him so the string would hear it twice.

"How many does that make? This year?"

He knew. He was asking so that I would say it.

"Thirty-one."

"And last year?"

"Nineteen."

"And the year before?"

"Twelve."

"Nineteen to thirty-one," Quill said, "and it is only the cold month." He lay with his blind eyes open, looking at nothing. "The lungs are dying, Tally's girl. The Hold's lungs. Veska knows it. She knows it better than I do, and she won't say it, because if she says it she will have to do something about it, and there is nothing to do."

The lungs are what we call the scrubbers, the great old machines in the deep levels of the Hold that clean the air. They are from before the Dark, like the lock, like the walls, like nearly everything in Tithe that works. Nobody alive knows how to make them. We know how to mend them, a little, with parts from other scrubbers in Holds that died, and the hullwalkers bring back what parts they can find from out on the hull and in the dead districts; but the parts are running out, and the lungs are running down, and when the lungs run down the black comes up out of them, a fine black mold that floats in the air too small to see, and gets into the chest. The children get it first. Then the old. Then everybody. That is the rot.

"There are lungs on Brine," I said.

"There are lungs on Brine," Quill agreed. "And water on Brine. And the Reeve of Brine sits on all of it like a hen on an egg." He turned his head toward me, a little. "Say the line for me."

He meant the lineage. He made me say it every day that winter, and I knew why, and I said it anyway.

"I am the Tally's girl of Hold Fourteen," I said, "taught by Quill, who was taught by Sorrel, who was taught by Abel the Lame, who was taught by Hanne, who was taught by the twins Ruth and Ruth, who were taught by Matthias Long-Arm," and on, and on, all of them, twenty-two names, back through four hundred years of the Dark, every Tally of Hold Fourteen, "who was taught by the First Tally," I finished, "who came up out of the Dark when the Dark was new, and would not give his name, and taught the children to count."

"Good," said Quill. "And what did he teach?"

"The Count. The Door. The marks."

"And?"

"Never sing the Count on the Halo."

"Why?"

"Nobody knows."

"Nobody knows," Quill said, "is not the same thing as there being no reason." He was quiet for a while. I thought he had gone to sleep. Then he said, "There is another rule."

I waited.

"Not today," he said. "I'm tired today. Ask me again."

* * *

There are three stories about how the Dark came, and every child in Tithe knows all three, and believes whichever one their Hold believes.

The Lamplighters say the Light was stolen. They say that once the Halo burned, and the Eye inside it was full of light, and the light poured down into Tithe like water into a cup and made everything go: the lungs, the lifts, the great lamps in the ceilings of the dead districts that are dark now and full of frost; and that the Founders who had made it lived among us, and were kind, and kept the Light. And then the Houses—which are some kind of devils in the Lamplighters' stories, I have never understood exactly what kind—the Houses were jealous of the Light and came and stole it, and the Founders went away after it to bring it back. And one day they will. One day the Halo will burn again, and the Founders will come back through it with the Light in their hands, and everything will go again, and the rot will be gone. That is why the Lamplighters keep lamps burning in their chapels in the Holds that have let them in, little oil lamps, wasting oil, so that the Founders will see them coming home.

The hullwalkers say the Light died. Just died, the way a star does, the way a lamp does when the oil's gone. They say the Halo was a machine like the lungs, and nobody looked after it, and it ran down. The hullwalkers do not have much patience for stories.

And the Tallies sing that the Light went home.

That is all. It went home. The Count says so: *count it back down, we are home once more*. Where home was, the Count does not say, and if Quill knew, he did not tell me. I asked him once if it meant that the Light was dead, like Pim. He said, *that is one way to hear it*, which is the kind of thing Quill said when he did not want to say anything.

I did not believe any of them, that winter. I was nineteen. I believed in the rot and the lungs and the water ration and the cold, which were what there was to believe in. The Light was before. Before was a story.

Ness came in off the hull that night.

You can hear the hullwalkers come in, all over the Hold, because the hullwalkers' lock is right at the top of the Hold under the old dome, and when it cycles the whole Hold rings with it like a bell. I was in the Tallies' room with a lamp, picking out a knotted string for Quill that he wanted to show me in the morning, and I heard the lock, and I put the string down so fast that I tangled it, and I had to sit and untangle it before I could go, because you do not leave a knotted string tangled. It is disrespectful to the knots. So by the time I got up to the dome they were out of their suits already, the three of them, Ness and old Barrow and Barrow's boy, and sitting on the bench by the lock with their hair stuck to their heads with sweat, steaming in the cold, eating the hot gruel that Veska keeps for walkers coming in.

The dome of Hold Fourteen was a garden once. You can tell. There are beds of old soil in long rows, gone grey as ash, and the ribs of the dome overhead are hung with the dead stems of something that climbed, and in the very top the glass is still whole, most of it, and that night Kettle was up and filling it, so the whole dome was full of Kettle-light, purple and brown and that sick slow yellow. It made everybody look dead. Ness looked up when I came in, her face purple and yellow in it, with the burn scar along her jaw gone black, and grinned.

"Tally's girl," she said. "You sang the boy out?"

"This morning."

"I heard you. I was at the top lock, suiting up. I heard you through the whole Hold." She held out her bowl. "Want some?"

I sat by her and ate some of her gruel. It is the best gruel in the Hold, walkers' gruel; Veska puts salt in it.

Ness was twenty-four. She had been walking the hull since she was twelve, when her father, who walked before her, did not come back one day, and the Hold needed somebody to bring in parts and she was the only one the right size for the suit. She was small and wiry and had arms like knotted cord and a laugh like something breaking. She had shaved her head that winter because of lice, and it made her look like a novice in an old picture, except for the scar and the grin. She had been my friend since I was five and she was ten and she had pulled me out of a vat of algae I had fallen into while trying to catch a fish that turned out to be a sock. She had been something else to me since the summer before, which we did not speak of in front of other people, though everybody knew. Quill knew. Quill said it was good for a Tally's girl to have something in her life that did not want to be remembered.

"What did you get?" I said.

"Not much." She nodded at the sack by the lock. "Two filter housings from the Ninth Spoke. A valve. Some cable. The Ninth's nearly picked out. We'll have to go further."

"Further where?"

"Out along the Halo." Barrow said it, the old walker, not looking up from his bowl. He was the oldest walker in Hold Fourteen, fifty, which is very old for a walker, and he had lost three fingers on his left hand to the cold, and he did not talk much. "Toward the Spindle. There's districts out that way nobody's walked since before my father."

"There's a reason nobody's walked them," I said.

"There's a reason for everything," Barrow said. "The reason is there's nothing left anywhere else."

He got up and went to put his suit away. His boy went after him. Ness and I sat on the bench under the dome with Kettle filling it, and she put her shoulder against my shoulder, and I put mine against hers.

"Tally's girl," she said, after a while, low. "Can I tell you a thing."

"You can tell me anything."

"Don't write it on a string."

"I won't."

She was quiet. Then she said: "The Halo's knocking."

I did not know what she meant, so I waited.

"I was out on the rim today," Ness said. "Way out, past the Ninth, right out where the city stops and it's just the Halo. Just the bare ring, you know, the old metal, with the gold lines on it. I was walking it to look for a way in to the dead districts on the other side. And I was standing there. On the Halo itself. With my boots locked down. And I felt it."

"Felt what?"

"Knocking." She put her hand on her own leg, just above the knee, and tapped it with her fingertips. "Up through my boots. Through the metal. Like somebody hitting it a long way off with a hammer. Not hard. Just—" she tapped. *Tap tap. Tap tap.* "A rhythm."

"It's the cold," I said. "Metal ticks in the cold. You know it does. The whole Hold ticks at night."

"Not like this. Ticking's ticking. This was—" She stopped. "It was *counting*, Tally's girl. It went in runs. A run of beats and then a stop, and then another run. Over and over. Like somebody saying something."

"Barrow felt it?"

"Barrow says he's felt it for years. On and off. He says all the old walkers have. He says his father used to call it the Halo singing, and that it means bad weather coming off Kettle." She shook her head. "But he says it's getting louder. He says when he was a boy you had to stand still for an hour to feel it. And today I felt it the second I locked down."

I did not say anything. Up in the top of the dome, through the old glass, Kettle turned. A long sheet of lightning went across its face, slow, the way it does, from one band to the next, and lit the whole dome for a moment white, and then it was gone.

"You think I'm making a story," Ness said.

"No."

"I'm not a Lamplighter."

"I know you're not."

"I just want somebody to know," she said. "In case. Somebody who remembers things."

"I'll remember," I said.

And I meant to say something else, something that would make her laugh, because she was frightened, I could feel it through her shoulder, and Ness was never frightened of anything. But before I could think what it was, the Hold rang.

Not the walkers' lock. The big lock. The dock.

Hold Fourteen has a dock, down at the bottom of the Hold, a great hangar of a place from before the Dark, where in the Light ships used to come in and tie up. It is mostly dark now and full of junk, and we use it for trading, when the water-boats come from Brine twice a year with ice, and when other Holds' walkers come round the hull to trade parts. It has a lock big enough to take a ship. When the big lock cycles, the whole Hold doesn't ring like a bell. It booms like a drum. You feel it in your feet and in your teeth and in the roots of your hair.

It was not the season for the water-boats. Nobody had sent word.

We went down. Everybody went down; you could hear the whole Hold going down, doors and feet and voices in the stair-wells. Veska was at the front with her big lamp and her iron bar. When we came out onto the gallery that runs round the top of the dock, there were already forty people at the rail, looking down.

The big lock had opened. Somebody had opened it from outside, which nobody in my lifetime had done, because the outer controls had been dead since before Quill. And through the lock, with frost smoking off its hull in the Hold's warm air and its lights burning so bright that we all put up our arms against them, a ship was coming in.

I had never seen a ship up close. Only the water-boats, which are tubs, ice-haulers, squat and ugly and slow. This was not a tub. It was long and lean and scarred all over, patched and repatched like the funeral cloth, black where it had been burned and silver where it had been mended, with a nose like a knife and gun-turrets on its flanks that were not for show, because one of them turned, slowly, as it came in, and looked up at the gallery where we stood. Its engines were low and loud and filled the dock with a growl you could feel in your stomach. It came in with a kind of contempt, as though the Hold were nothing, as though the dock had been built for it alone and it had only now, after four hundred years, got round to arriving.

On its flank, under the scorch and the patches, there were letters. Old letters, the kind Quill was teaching me, from before the Dark. Faded nearly to nothing and painted over and then painted again, by someone who had wanted them kept.

*PATIENCE*, they said.

"That's the Reeve's ship," Ness said beside me, very quietly. "That's the Reeve of Brine."

Down on the dock floor, a hatch opened in the ship's flank, and a ramp came down, and men in armor came down it, a lot of men, with guns. And after them, slowly, leaning on one of them, a big man in a long coat, coughing.

He stood at the foot of the ramp and looked up at the gallery. At all of us standing there in the lamplight with our mouths open. He looked along the rail as though he were counting us. Then his eyes came to the end of the rail where I was standing, beside Ness, and went to my throat, to the white string there, and stopped.

And I understood, I do not know how, the way you understand a thing in a dream: that he had not come for the Hold, or the walkers, or Veska, or our parts or our water or our dead. He had counted us, and he had found what he was counting for.

He was looking for the Tally.

# Chapter 4: The Skirt

*The Combine · FY 2291*

"Again," Sol said.

Pell held the clip up in front of his visor where she could see it, and turned it in his glove so the work light caught the throat. His hands were steadier than they had been a week ago. They were still too slow.

"Spring," he said, and pressed the gate with his thumb and let it snap back. "Gate. Throat." He ran his thumb round the inside curve where the line's eye would sit. "No burr. No crack. No flat. Eye." He took the eye of his line in his other glove and bent it back on itself, hard, the way she had shown him, looking for the little white stress lines in the braid where it went into the swage. "No fray. No white. Seat." He clipped it to the hardpoint, and pulled, and leaned his whole weight back on it, which in the Ring's gravity, such as it was, was not much, but was enough to tell. "Holds."

"Again."

"Chief, that's nine times."

"Kostya checked his clip four hundred times. That's how many shifts he'd done. He checked it every shift, and it held four hundred times, and on four hundred and one the eye pulled through the throat. You know why?"

Pell was quiet.

"Because a Lindqvist clip isn't rated for side-loading," Sol said. "Because when you swing out on it, like he did, to ride a cassette in, the line comes off the clip at an angle, and the throat flexes, a hair, every time. Four hundred times. And you can't see it flex. And you can't see it in the throat when you look, because the crack starts on the inside of the steel. So checking it doesn't save you. You check it anyway. Because the day you don't check it is the day it'll be the burr, and not the crack." She pointed at him. "Again."

He did it again.

They were at the Spindle that week, all of Gang Four, fitting the governor's outer collar. It was the best work on the Ring and the worst. The best because the Spindle was the heart of the thing, and the work was fine, and Gang Four had been trusted with it because Gang Four was the best gang on the Ring and the Company knew it, even if it would have docked them a day's pay for saying so. The worst because the Spindle stood nine hundred meters off the Ring's rim, straight up, a tower of black steel and gold phase-lines narrowing toward its top like a needle, and to work on it you had to work in the open, on the outside, with nothing at your back but your line and nothing below you but the Ring's face nine hundred meters down and Kettle a long way under that.

Sol had worked the Spindle for three years. She had helped raise the last third of it. Dov had died on it.

"Okay," she said. "Clip in. We're going inside."

The governor lived in the Spindle's belly, three-quarters of the way up, in a cavity the size of a cathedral that the riggers called the Barn. You went in through a rigging hatch in the tower's flank, which was open, because nothing on the Spindle was closed yet, and then you were inside, and the dark swallowed your lamps, and you hung on your line in the middle of a space so big your lamps did not reach its walls.

Pell said, "Oh," very softly, as everybody did.

The governor ring filled the Barn. It was forty meters across, a ring within the Ring, standing on its edge like a great wheel: a collar of dark steel two meters thick, ringed all round its outer edge with teeth the size of a man, and laced on every surface with phase-lines finer and denser than anywhere else on the structure, so that when your lamps touched it the whole thing lit gold and then went dark again as the light moved on. It was not turning. It could not turn. It was held. All round it, at intervals, great steel arms reached in from the Barn's walls, and at the end of each arm was a fixed collar, a cuff of steel through which the governor's teeth passed, and through each cuff, driven in through a bore and seated deep in a notch between two teeth, there was a dog.

"Three for the dog," Sol said. "There they are."

Pell moved his lamp. The nearest dog was ten meters off: a steel pin as thick as a man's thigh and four meters long, driven through the bore of its collar with only its head standing out, a squared boss with a socket for the big drivers.

"That's what we're doing?" he said. "Dogs?"

"That's what we did. Last year. Now we're doing the outer collar. But you need to know the dogs." She pushed off and floated toward the nearest one and caught herself on the arm. He followed, clumsy, his line paying out behind him. "When the Ring's done and all the phase-lines are seated, the governor has to be free to move. To breathe, Haddad calls it. It doesn't turn round, not like a wheel. It shifts. Tiny amounts. Fractions of a degree, back and forth, all the time, and that's what holds the phase steady. If the governor can't breathe the throat can't open. So while we're building, we dog it. Lock it. So it doesn't shift under us while we're seating lines. Twelve dogs, twelve notches."
