She put her glove on the dog's head. "And before they light it, we pull them. All twelve. And it breathes."

"How long does that take?"

"Six hours, if nothing seizes. Four of us on the big drivers, three rounds of the Count on each one to back it out even, and then you walk it out of the bore on the crane." She turned, so her lamp went round the Barn and touched the other arms, one by one, going away into the dark. "Twelve notches. One. Two. Three—"

"That one's different."

She stopped. "What?"

"The third one." Pell's lamp was on it, across the Barn. "The notch. It's a different shape. See? The others are square-bottomed. That one's deeper. And there's no dog in it."

He was right. She had stopped noticing it years ago. The third arm reached in from the Barn's wall like the others, and its cuff gripped the governor's teeth like the others, but the cuff was bigger, much bigger, a great boxy mass of steel plating that wrapped not just the collar but a whole section of the governor ring and the arm behind it; and the bore through it was empty, and the notch in the governor's teeth that the bore lined up on, which you could see if you got close, was cut deeper than the rest, with a stepped profile, like a key-slot.

"Design change," she said. "Year before last. Originally there were thirteen notches—that was one of them, the deep one, it was for something. Haddad's people had it in the plans. Then the plans came back from Ostrava with that one struck through, and they didn't take the notch out because it was already cut, and they didn't dog it because they didn't need to, because they've got twelve. So it's a leftover."

"What was it for?"

"I'm a rigger, Okafor. I hang off things and bolt them together. Ask an engineer."

"What's the box?"

"The Inner Housing." She looked at it. "That's the most steel on the whole Ring, in one place. Four meters thick on the outside face. There's a chamber in the middle where the governor's sensors are, and the old instruments. There's a plate on the front, bolted, for the instruments, and a crawl hatch round the back for people." She remembered. "If Kettle flares when you're working the Spindle, and you can't get down to the platform in time, you go in there. Behind the steel. Everybody does. It's the only place on the Spindle that'll keep a flare off you."

"Have you been in it?"

"Twice." The second time had been with Dov. They had sat in the dark in there for six hours with their suit lamps off to save the cells, back to back, and Dov had told her the plot of every serial he had ever watched on the platform screens, at length, wrong, until she had threatened to vent his suit. Three months after that his clip went. "It's not much. It's a steel room."

Pell was looking at the deep notch with his lamp. His face, behind the visor, had the expression it got sometimes, which she was beginning to recognize: a farm boy's expression, a look at a thing that did not make sense yet but was going to.

"Chief," he said. "Why's it called a dog?"

"Because it holds on and doesn't let go," said Teague, on the gang channel, and coughed. "Come on, children. Collar's waiting."

* * *

They worked the collar for six hours and ran out of air margin at the end of it, the way they always did now, because the shifts had been extended by forty minutes that week without anyone extending the suits. Sol called the Count for the bolts and the gang answered, and on the third round Pell came in on his own number for the first time without being prompted—*seven the hand on the cold steel wheel*—in a cracked tenor, a little sharp, and Juno said, on the tug channel, "Oh, he *sings*," in a voice so delighted and so cruel that he did not sing again all shift.

Halfway through, a white suit came up the tower.

You could see it from a long way off, coming up from the Ring's face on a little Company scooter, the kind the engineers used: clean, white, lit up all over with running lights like a party. Bracken saw it first and said "Company," and everybody stopped talking, and then the white suit came round the tower's flank and hung there off the end of the collar, and through its visor, under the lights, there was a woman's face. Fifty, maybe. Narrow and dark, with deep lines bracketing her mouth, and very black eyebrows, and eyes that did not look at anybody but at the collar, the bolts, the seam of gold where the phase-lines met.

"Dr. Haddad," Sol said.

"Chief Dace." The voice was dry and accented, a Halvard voice, educated. "Carry on."

They carried on. Haddad watched. She did not say anything for a full round of the Count. Then, while the drivers were being reset, she said, on the gang channel, so they all heard: "The dogs. In the Barn."

"Yes, doctor."

"How long to pull them? All twelve."

"Six hours. If nothing's seized."

"Could you do it in four?"

Sol looked at her. Haddad was still not looking at anybody.

"With two gangs," Sol said slowly. "Four of us on the drivers and four on the crane. And if nothing's seized. And if you don't mind us maybe scarring a notch."

"I would mind that very much."

"Then six."

Haddad said nothing. Then she nodded, once, inside her helmet, and wrote something on the tablet strapped to her forearm.

"Why four?" Sol said.

Haddad looked up from the tablet. For the first time she looked at Sol, directly, through two visors and a meter of vacuum and the glare of her own running lights; and Sol, who had spent twelve years reading the faces of Company people for the thing behind them, saw something in this one and could not tell what it was. It was there and gone.

"Contingency planning," said Haddad. "Carry on, Chief." And the scooter turned, its little thrusters puffing white, and went back down the tower toward the Ring, small and bright and getting smaller.

"What was that?" said Juno.

"Don't know."

"*Contingency planning*," said Juno in Haddad's accent, very precisely. "God. When's first light, doctor? When the board says, chief."

"Don't," said Teague.

"What?"

"Just don't," he said, and coughed, and coughed, and went on coughing for a long time, so long that they all stopped work and waited, and Sol watched his air drop on her visor, four percent, five, until finally it stopped, and he said, thickly, "Sorry. Count it, chief."

* * *

The *Patience* took them home at shift end, all five of them crammed into her little crew bubble with their helmets off and their faces grey with tiredness, Juno flying. She was an old tug, older than the Ring; she had been a dock tug at Ostrava before the Company bought her for the yard, and she still had her Ostrava registry plate bolted to the bulkhead over the pilot's couch, brass, with her name on it in the old Combine lettering, *PATIENCE*, and under it her number and the date of her keel. Juno said she was the best tug on the Ring and also that she was held together by spit and prayer. Both were true. She pulled forty tonnes with arms that had been rebuilt so many times there was nothing original left in them but the bolts. She had never once let them down.

From the *Patience*'s bubble, coming in, you saw the platform the way you never saw it from inside: the whole of it, small and alone, hung off the Ring's rim on its long mooring arm. The dock module, the spine, the admin stack with its lit windows, and the hab rings—three of them, A and B and C, wheels of grey metal turning slowly on the spine, six hundred meters across—where four hundred and twelve bonded workers and forty-odd Company staff and twenty Proctors ate and slept and coughed and played cards and waited to go home.

And round the hab rings, the skirt.

It was meant to be a shield. For the Lighting. Sol knew this the way she knew most things about the Lighting, which was secondhand and in pieces. When the Ring was lit, first light would come out of it—the riggers did not know exactly what first light was, only that it was a kind of flare, a big one, bigger than Kettle's worst, and that it would come out of the throat when the throat opened, and that you did not want to be in front of it. The platform would be moved off to a safe distance for the Lighting, they had always been told. The hab rings would be shielded. The skirt was the shielding. It was a great cupped shell of plates—steel, and between the steel, tanks of water, and between the water, something Haddad's people called *the mesh*, a lattice of the same gold lines that laced the Ring—built in sections, on frames, that would close round the three hab rings like a hand closing round an egg. Gang Nine had been building it for two years.

It was half done. Sol had seen it every shift from the *Patience* coming home, and she saw it now, and she had seen it so often she did not see it at all; and then she did.

"Juno," she said. "Go round the skirt."

"I'm on approach."

"Go round it. Slow."

Juno looked at her and then took the *Patience* off approach without a word, and brought her round in a slow arc past the platform's flank, past the hab rings, along the line of the skirt.

There was nobody on it.

The frames stood out from the rings like the ribs of a half-built ship. Some of them were plated. Most of them were not. On the plated sections, the water tanks were in, and you could see the gold mesh glinting between them where the outer plates were not yet on; on the bare sections, there was nothing but the frame. Floating tethered all along the line, waiting, were the plates: hundreds of them, great curved sheets of steel four meters on a side, each one moored to a frame by a single line, turning very slowly on its line in the light. And there were no gangs. No work lights. No tugs. Gang Nine's work lights should have been on, all along the line, as they had been on every shift for two years, and they were off. The plates turned slowly in the dark like leaves on a dead tree.

"Where's Nine?" said Bracken.

Nobody knew.

"Take us in," Sol said.

* * *

She found Oyelaran in the galley at the end of third shift, eating alone at the end of a long table with his back to the wall. He was a broad dark man with grey in his beard, and he had been chief of Gang Nine for six years, and he and Sol had drunk together a few times and fought once, over a crane, and had a kind of respect for each other that neither of them would have called friendship. He saw her coming and did not look surprised.

"Dace," he said.

"Where's your gang?"

"Here. In the galley. Eating. Like me." He gestured at the long tables with his spoon. Gang Nine was there, she saw, at the next table along, eight of them, talking low. "We're on the cassettes now. Segment Three-Forty. Started this morning."

"What about the skirt?"

He put the spoon down.

"Suspended," he said. "Pending revised specification. That's the order. Came down from the Director's office through the Chief Engineer, yesterday, end of shift. Stop work on the skirt, stow tools, moor the plates, transfer to cassettes. Revised specification to follow."

"What revised specification?"

"It'll follow," said Oyelaran. "That's what follow means. It hasn't come yet." He picked up his spoon again and looked at it. "I asked. I asked the shift engineer and he said he didn't know. I asked to see the Chief Engineer and they said she was busy. I put in a request to see the Director." He ate a spoonful. "They gave me a day's dock for the request. Wasting Company time."

"How long to finish it? The skirt. If you went back tomorrow."

"Full gang? Two of us, three, if they gave us Gang Six too? Sixty days. Maybe fifty. It's not just plating. The mesh has to be tuned to the Ring's phase. That's Haddad's people." He looked at her. "Why?"

"Because the *Mercy* comes Day Three Hundred," said Sol. "And Lighting's after. And if the skirt's not finished by Lighting it doesn't matter, because we'll all be gone. Unless."

"Unless," Oyelaran agreed. He did not finish it either.

At the next table along, a woman from Gang Nine with a shaved head had turned round on her bench and was listening. Sol knew her by sight; Ruzicka, a cutter. She said, "You heard about the *Mercy*?"

"What about it?"

"She's late," said Ruzicka. "Clerk in the comms stack told my bunkmate. She was supposed to signal from the outer system relay on Day Two Forty, when she crossed the heliopause. She didn't. Nothing. Clerk said it's been seven days."

"Signals get lost," said Oyelaran.

"Seven days," said Ruzicka. "Out of a hauler. You ever known a hauler miss its relay signal by seven days?"

Nobody had.

* * *

Teague took her to the ballast hold that night.

She had known he would, sooner or later. She had known about the ballast hold for years: everybody did, the way everybody knew about the purge valve. The platform's ballast hold was down at the bottom of the spine, below the dock, a long low cavern of a space packed with crates of Gall ore that the Company kept for trim and never moved, and it was cold and dark and the Proctors' cameras had not worked down there since before Sol came aboard, because the Company had not wanted to pay for new ones; and so it was where people went, when people wanted to talk about things that could not be talked about anywhere else.

"I'm not joining anything," she said, on the ladder.

"I know."

"I've got fifty-three days."

"I know how many days you've got, Sol. I can count." He was ahead of her, going down slowly, a rung at a time, stopping to breathe. "I'm not asking you to join anything. I'm asking you to come and listen. You can listen and not join."

"That's how it starts."

"That's how everything starts," said Teague.

There were perhaps forty of them, in the end, sitting on the ore crates in the light of two work lamps turned down low. People she knew. Oyelaran was there, and Ruzicka, and men and women from Gangs Two and Six and Eleven, and two of the galley crew, and an old woman from the air plant called Mother Szell who was supposed to have been on the Ostrava docks in the strike of '64 alongside Teague, and who had a voice like a rusty hinge. Juno was there. Sol had not known Juno came. Juno saw her and lifted a hand and looked away. Bracken was not there, and Pell was not there, because Sol had told Pell that if she ever caught him in the ballast hold she would cut his line.

They talked. It was not a meeting, exactly. There was no one in charge, and nobody stood up. They sat on the crates and said things, one after another, in low voices, and the cold came up off the ore. The skirt. The *Mercy*. The forty extra minutes on every shift, and no extra air. Kostya. Somebody from Gang Two whose bunkmate's bond had been extended three months for an infraction nobody could name. The Lindqvist clips. Dov.

"Sixteen times," said Teague, and looked at Sol, and she looked back at him until he looked away.

"So what do we do?" somebody said. A young man from Gang Eleven, not much older than Pell. "If they're not finishing the skirt. If the *Mercy*'s late. What do we do about it?"

"We ask," said Mother Szell.

"Oyelaran asked. They docked him."

"Then we ask together," said the old woman. "All of us. All at once. One person asks, they dock him a day. Four hundred ask, they've got four hundred people not working, and a Ring to light."

"That's a strike," said the boy from Gang Eleven.

"It's asking," said Mother Szell, "loud."

"You know what they do to strikers," said Juno, from her crate, suddenly. Her voice was very precise. "Under the bond schedule. You know what the penalty is. Withdrawal of labor is breach of bond. They can extend your bond to life. They can transfer you. They can put you on a penal hulk at the Halvard yards and you'll never see daylight again, never, and they can do it to your family, too, if your family's on the bond, and mine is." She looked round at them. "I've got a son on Ostrava. He's nine. He's on my bond. If I break it they can take him."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"I'm not saying don't," said Juno, in a smaller voice. "I'm just saying."

Sol stood up.

They all looked at her. She had not meant them to. She had meant to stand up and go up the ladder and back to Bay Fourteen and cross off today's mark and go to sleep. She stood there on the cold ore crates in the low light with forty faces turned toward her, and she found that she did not know what to say, because she had not meant to say anything.

"I've got fifty-three days," she said, finally. "I'm sorry. I've got fifty-three days, and then the *Mercy*, and I'm out. And I'm not—I can't. I'm sorry about the skirt. I'm sorry about Kostya, and Dov, and everybody. But I can't. Not now. Not when I'm this close." She looked at Teague. "You said I could listen and not join."

"I did," said Teague.

"Then I've listened."

She went to the ladder. Nobody stopped her. She heard someone say something behind her, low, not kindly, and she heard Teague say, "Leave her be," and then she was on the ladder and climbing, and the cold of the hold fell away under her, and she was in the spine corridor, with its harsh white lights and its clean grey walls, alone.

She had gone about twenty steps along it toward the hab ring transfer when the speakers came on.

They did not come on often. Shift changes, and drills, and a flare warning when Kettle was acting up. They made a soft double chime first, two notes, high and low, the Company's chime, and then a voice, a woman's, recorded, calm and pleasant and not belonging to anybody on the platform. Sol had heard it so many times she did not hear it. She heard it now.

"Attention all personnel. Attention all personnel. This is a notice from the office of the Site Director.

"Following a phase instability in the Ring's primary governor, identified and certified by the Chief Phase Engineer, Doctor Y. Haddad, the Lighting of Transit Ring TTH-9 is advanced to Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Eight of the current fiscal year.

"All bonded personnel will continue in their assigned duties. Revised work schedules will be posted to your terminals.

"The Combine thanks you for your service."

The chime again, high and low. Then the speakers went off, and there was only the hum of the corridor lights, and the air plant breathing in the walls.

Sol stood in the middle of the spine corridor.

She did the sum without meaning to. It did itself. Day Two Hundred and Forty-Seven today. Lighting on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Eight. Forty-one days. And the *Mercy* on Day Three Hundred, if the *Mercy* came at all, which would be twelve days after first light. Twelve days after.

The platform would be moved off to a safe distance for the Lighting. They had always been told that. The hab rings would be shielded. She thought of the plates on the dead skirt, turning slowly on their lines in the dark.

Behind her, at the bottom of the spine, she heard the ladder ring under somebody's feet, and then voices, a lot of voices, coming up out of the ballast hold. They had heard it too. The speakers went everywhere.

She did not turn round. She stood with her back to them in the white light, with her hands at her sides, and she found that her right hand had gone, by itself, to the thigh pocket of her liner, where she kept her scribe; and that she was holding it so hard, through the cloth, that the point had gone through and into her leg, and she could feel a small warm wetness there, spreading, and it did not hurt at all.

# Chapter 5: The Price of a Name

*The Concord · 336 AL*

"No," said Archkeeper Sallow.

He said it gently. He was sitting in the high-backed chair by the window of his study, at the foot of the Spindle, in the Keepers' chapter-house, with a rug over his knees and his stick across the rug and a cup of something steaming on the sill beside him, and he had listened to the whole of what Idris had come to say without interrupting once, his long sorrowful face turned toward the window and the Eye beyond it. When Idris had finished, he had let the silence go on for a while. Then he had said no, gently, the way you would say it to a child who has asked whether a dead dog might come back.

"She could take the habit," Idris said. "As a novice. A novice is essential service. It's in the Levy Statute—"

"I know what is in the Levy Statute. I helped the Prelate's clerks draft the last revision." Sallow turned from the window. "Essential service, Idris, is a privilege the Concord extends to the Keepers because the Concord needs the Eye kept, and the Eye cannot be kept by children dragged out of the Square with a red cord on their wrists and shoved into a habit to save their skins. If I let one Keeper's sister take the habit with a cord on her wrist, I will have forty Keepers' cousins at my door by nightfall, and the Square will know by morning, and the Square will say what it has always said under its breath and has never quite dared to say aloud: that the Light's servants do not pay the Light's debt. And then we will have riots in the Sump, and the Wardens' Guard will put them down, and people will die." He lifted his cup and did not drink from it. "Two thousand go every year. Every year, Idris. Some of them are someone's sister. Most of them, I'd imagine."

"She's my sister."

"I know." Sallow set the cup down. "And I am sorry. Believe that I am."

Idris stood in the middle of the study and did not know what to do with his hands. The room was lined with books, real ones, paper, the Keepers' library of three centuries—transit logs and phase tables and commentaries on the Rule, the old maintenance manuals in their cracked leather—and they went up to the ceiling on every side, and the window was the only gap in them, and the Eye filled the window, breathing.

"You could ask the Prelate," he said.

"I could. And she would say no, more pleasantly than I have. And she would remember that I had asked." Sallow looked at him under his heavy lids. "This is not the year to be remembered by the Prelate, Idris. Nor by the Factor."

"Why not this year?"

Sallow did not answer at once. He looked out at the Eye.

"The Factor's people have been asking questions," he said at last. "This month and last. About the Eye's tolerances. How much mass she'll take in a single transit. How many ships abreast. How quickly the governor recovers after a heavy load." He was quiet. "Engineering questions. Polite ones. Through the proper channels, to me, in writing, with the House's seal on them. I've answered them. Of course I've answered them. The Houses hold the Debt, and the Concord serves the Houses, and the Keepers serve the Light, which is to say all of them." He turned his cup on the sill, a quarter turn, the way a Keeper turns a dial. "But it's an odd thing, to be asked how many ships abreast. Nobody's ever needed to know that before."

"What does it mean?"

"I don't know what it means. That's what I'm telling you. When I don't know what a thing means, I keep my head down until I do, and I don't give anyone a reason to look at me, and I advise my Keepers to do the same." He reached out and touched Idris's hand, as he had on the gallery, dry and light. "There are things we don't touch, my boy. You know the first verse of the Rule."

"The Knot is the Founders', and shall not be turned."

"Just so. There are a great many Knots in the world. Most of them aren't made of steel. The Levy is one." He took his hand back. "Go home. Be with her. You have forty-eight days. Don't spend them in the Hub chasing clerks."

Idris bowed, as a Keeper bows to the Archkeeper, from the waist with his hand on his line. Then he went out, and down the long stair of the chapter-house, and out through the Keepers' Spoke into the Hub, and went directly to the Levy Office to chase clerks.

* * *

It took him four days to find the right one.

The Levy Office occupied six levels of the Hub's eastern quarter, a warren of counters and corridors and waiting halls with long benches where people sat with numbered slips in their hands, sometimes for days. Idris went from counter to counter in his grey habit. He filled out a hardship petition, and a dependent's petition, and an essential-service query, which a young clerk with an elaborate hairstyle took from him with an expression of such delicate pity that he wanted to put his fist through the counter's glass. He waited on the benches. He listened.

People talked, on the benches. Not loudly. Not to him, at first; the habit made them careful. But on the third day an old man sitting beside him with a slip in his hand and a red cord on his wrist—a man of forty, he must have been, the last year of eligibility, though he looked sixty—leaned over and said, without looking at him, "You're asking at the wrong windows, Keeper."

"Which are the right ones?"

"There's no right windows." The man looked at his slip. "There's a desk. Fourth level. Reconciliations. You go there and ask for Mister Oake, and you don't ask about anything at all, you just say you've come about a reconciliation, and he'll know." He turned the slip over in his fingers. "If you've got the money."

"How much money?"

The man laughed. It was not a happy sound. "More than me," he said, and a Guard at the end of the hall called a number, and the man got up and went.

The Reconciliations desk was at the end of a corridor on the fourth level that did not look as though it led anywhere, behind a frosted door with nothing on it but a number, and behind the door was a small warm room with a desk and two chairs and a window onto a light-well, and behind the desk was Ferrant Oake.

He was a large soft man of perhaps fifty, in the brown coat of a senior clerk of the Concord, with a round pink face and very small pale eyes and hair that had been arranged with great care across a skull it no longer covered. There was a bowl of candied kelp on his desk, the expensive kind from the Brine vats, dusted with sugar, and as Idris came in he was eating one, delicately, with two fingers, and he finished it before he looked up.

"Keeper," he said. "Sit, sit. You've come about a reconciliation."

Idris sat.

"I'll tell you how it works," said Oake. He had a light, pleasant, wheezing voice, like a small bellows. "So there's no confusion. People come in here confused, and it makes everything harder, and I don't like things to be hard. The Levy is two thousand. Not one thousand nine hundred and ninety-nine. Two thousand. The House counts them as they go through the Eye, and if there's one short, the Concord answers for it, and then the Office answers to the Concord, and then I answer to the Office, and I don't like answering. So a name can't come out of the Levy. Never. Not for love or money." He took another piece of kelp. "But a name can be *reconciled*. Do you see? There's an error. A disk was miscast, or misread, or two disks stuck together in the Drum, it happens, it's a very old Drum. The wrong name was called. The Office corrects the error. The right name is substituted." He ate the kelp. "And the wrong name goes home."

"The right name," said Idris.

"There's a list. The reserve. Don't concern yourself with it."

"Who's on it?"

Oake looked at him with his small pale eyes. "Keeper," he said, gently, "you came in here to buy your sister's way out of the Levy. Did you think it came out of nowhere? Did you think the House would take nineteen ninety-nine and send a nice letter?" He shook his head. "Don't concern yourself with the list. That's my advice. That's free."

Idris said nothing.

"Normally," said Oake, "the fee is forty thousand."

It was more than Idris earned in eight years. It was more than his father's pension and his mother's and the rooms on the forty-first level and every book he owned and the line round his neck, all together, sold.

"I don't have that," he said.

"No," Oake agreed. "Keepers don't. It's a vocation, not a living. I've always admired that." He brushed sugar off his fingers onto a cloth. "But a Keeper has other things than money. A Keeper has a rota. You're on the Transit Register, Keeper Vey, aren't you? The Third Spoke's register desk. Two days in seven."

Idris went cold.

"How do you know that?"

"I know a great many things. It's the only way to get anything done." Oake folded the cloth. "A ship is coming through the Eye in eleven days. Inbound. Her name's the *Clemency*. She's a private vessel, chartered out of Ostrava, and she'll be coming through on your shift, at the fourth bell of second watch, on the Third Spoke's lane. And when she comes through, the governor will tell you her mass, and you'll write it in the Register, as you write every mass." He smiled. "And you'll write forty thousand tonnes. Ore barge, empty, returning. Forty thousand tonnes. That's all."

"What will the governor tell me?"

"I've no idea," said Oake. "I'm not a Keeper. I'm a clerk. I'm only telling you what you'll write."

The Transit Register was the oldest book in the Concord. Older than the Book of the Lighting. The first entries in it were in a hand nobody could read any more, in the first year of the Light, when the Founders were still alive, and every ship that had come through the Eye since was in it, its name and its lane and its mass to the tonne, written by a Keeper and initialed and sealed, in three hundred and thirty-six years of unbroken entries in a library of volumes that filled a hall under the Spindle. A Keeper could be stripped of his line for a blot. For a false entry—there had never been a false entry. Not that anyone knew of. It was not a thing anybody had thought to make a rule against, because it was not a thing anybody had thought could happen. It would be like falsifying the tide.

"It's a number in a book," said Oake gently, watching his face. "That's all. It's a number in a book, Keeper, and your sister comes home."

Idris thought of Lark on the window ledge, with her forehead against the glass. *Everybody's brother says that.*

"Yes," he said.

* * *

He had not meant to follow her.

That was what he told himself afterward, and it was nearly true. He had come home from the Hub that evening sick and light-headed, as though he had been fasting, and found the rooms dark and Lark's coat gone from its hook; and he had stood in the cook-niche for a while not doing anything; and then he had gone out again and down the stairs and across the Third Spoke's main gallery, toward the lifts, with no idea where he was going. And at the lifts he had seen her, a long way ahead, the ragged dark head with the red lock in it, going not into the lifts but past them, to the old service stair at the gallery's end, the one with the rusted gate that nobody used, that went down.

He followed her down.

The service stair went down a long way. Past the middle wards, past the lower wards, past the water levels where the air was thick and warm and smelled of algae, through a door with a broken lock and a sign on it that said NO ACCESS—SUMP in Concord letters, and below the sign in older letters, half scraped off, something else he could not read. Then the stair ended, and he was in the Sump.

He had been born in Tithe and lived all his life in it and he had never been to the Sump. Nobody he knew had. You knew it was there the way you knew your own bones were there: underneath, holding everything up, not to be looked at. The Sump was the old platform. Everybody knew that, too. The first thing the Founders had built, before the city, before the Spokes, when Tithe was a single station moored to the Ring—its hab rings and its spine and its docks were still there, under everything, the whole city grown over them and round them the way a tree grows round a nail, and the poor lived in them, and the bond-families, and the families of Levy-dodgers who had lost their ward-numbers, and anyone else the city did not have room for higher up.

The first thing he noticed was the floor. It curved. Not much, but you felt it, after the flat decks of the Spokes: you were walking along the inside of a great wheel, and the floor ahead rose gently and went up out of sight round the curve, and the ceiling came down to meet it. The ceilings were low. The corridors were narrow. The walls were grey, under paint—under many paints, under three centuries of paint, flaking, so that in places you could see layer under layer of color, ochre and green and white and blue, like the strata in a cliff—and in some places the paint had flaked right down to the bottom, and there on the grey were letters, old letters, stenciled in a yellow gone brown. *B-9*. *B-11*. *SPINE TRANSFER*. *NO HELMETS PAST THIS POINT*.

It was crowded. It was warm. Every corridor had people in it, sitting in doorways, cooking on little plates, washing clothes in buckets, arguing, sleeping. There were stalls selling fried krill and kelp cakes and something grey in bowls, and stalls selling salvage, wire and bolts and old circuit boards, and one stall selling nothing but buttons, thousands of them, in heaps, of every color. Lamps hung from hooks in the ceiling, little oil lamps and salvaged ship's lamps and in one corridor a string of tiny lights that flickered on and off in sequence, red and green and gold, for no reason that he could see except that somebody had thought it would be beautiful. People looked at him as he passed. At the habit. Some of them stopped talking. One woman spat, not at him, just near him, on the curving floor.

He lost Lark at a junction where four corridors met under a dead light fixture. He stood there turning round, looking down each of them, his habit drawing eyes. Then he heard the singing.

It came from the left-hand corridor, round the curve. Children's voices, and a rope: a slap, and a slap, and a slap, steady, on the deck. He went toward it because he did not know where else to go.

There were six of them in the corridor beyond the curve, small, ragged, the eldest perhaps ten. Two at either end of a long rope made of knotted wire, turning it, and one in the middle, skipping, a little girl with her hair in a hundred tiny braids that flew up and came down every time she jumped, and three more lined up against the wall waiting their turn, clapping. They were singing. They did not see him. He stood at the bend of the corridor and listened.

*One for the hook and two for the line,*  
*three for the dog and four for the time,*  
*five for the air and six for the seal,*  
*seven the hand on the cold steel wheel,*  
*eight for the dark and nine for the drop,*  
*ten for the Comp'ny, ten and*—STOP!

The rope stopped dead on the deck, and the little girl stopped dead on top of it, with both feet, exactly on the beat, and the others shrieked. Then the rope went round again, and she skipped again, and they sang on, faster, a new verse he did not know, the rope slapping, the braids flying.

*Sun on your left and the kettle below,*  
*dog in the box where the old men go,*  
*twice to the dark and once to the light,*  
*seat it and pin it and kiss it goodnight!*

On *goodnight* the girl jumped out of the rope sideways and the next child jumped in, without breaking the beat, and they all shouted the last lines together, very loud, delighted, the way children shout the part of a song they know best:

*That is the way that you lock the door—*  
*count it back down and you're HOME once more!*

The rope came down. The child in the middle crouched, and the rope-turners swung it low, and the child hopped it backward, and they all chanted together, slower, in a kind of sing-song countdown as she hopped—*ten, nine, eight, seven*—back down to one, and on *one* she jumped clear and everyone cheered.

Idris stood in the corridor with his hand on the cold grey wall.

He had said the first two lines of that every morning of his life. He had said them with his palm on the brass, in the gallery, with the Eye breathing below him. He had been told that they were the oldest words in the Concord, carried down from the hands that had raised the Ring, and that a Keeper did not need to understand them to say them truly. And here they were, in the Sump, being skipped to by children, with a whole song wrapped round them like a body round a heart, a song nobody had ever taught him.

"What are you doing here?"

He turned. Lark was standing behind him at the bend, with her arms folded, and her face was so white with anger that the red lock in her hair looked like a wound.

"I followed you," he said. There did not seem much point in saying anything else.

"I can see that. I asked what you're doing here."

"I don't know."

"You don't *know*." She came up to him. She came right up, until her face was a hand's width from his, and he saw that she was shaking. "You don't come here. Nobody like you comes here. Do you know what they'll think? A Keeper, in the Sump, in Levy season, following people? Do you know what they'll think you are?" Her voice dropped. "Do you know what they'll *do*?"

The children had stopped skipping. They were standing in a row along the corridor wall, staring at him, the little girl with the braids at the front with her thumb in her mouth.

"I'm sorry," Idris said.

Lark looked at him for a long moment. Then something went out of her shoulders, and she shut her eyes, and opened them.

"Well, you're here," she said. "Somebody's seen you. Half the Sump will know by now. If I send you back up on your own you'll get a knife in the back on the stair." She turned. "Come on. You'd better meet Rook."

* * *

Mother Rook lived at the end of a short dead-end corridor off a bigger one, in a room that had once been something else—a store, maybe, or a pump room; there were pipes running across its ceiling, painted the same grey as everything, and a big valve-wheel on one wall, rusted solid—and was now so full of things that there was barely space for the three of them to sit. There were shelves on every wall, made of salvaged plating, and on the shelves there was everything: jars of buttons, coils of wire, old slates with cracked faces, a ship's compass, a box of keys, a stuffed bird with one glass eye, a child's shoe, a tangle of red cords—Levy cords, Idris realized, dozens of them, cut through—a lamp, a bell, a model ship carved out of packing foam. There was a narrow cot, and a hot plate, and a stool, and a chair; and in the chair, small as a child, wrapped in shawls, Mother Rook.

She was the oldest person Idris had ever seen. She might have been eighty. She might have been a hundred. Her face was folded in on itself like a dried fruit, and her eyes were nearly lost in the folds, but they were black and bright and missed nothing. Her hands, on the arms of the chair, were all knuckle. She watched him come in and duck under the pipes and sit, at Lark's nod, on the stool; and she did not say anything at all until he was sitting. Then she said, in a voice like gravel shaken in a tin:

"So this is the brother."

"This is the brother," said Lark.

"The Keeper."

"Yes."

Mother Rook looked at him for a long time. At his habit, at the white hook on his breast, at the line round his neck.

"Pretty," she said at last. "Your rope. Real cord, is it?"

"It's my father's."

"Is it now." She did not explain what she meant by that. "You'll have tea."

It was not a question. Lark got up and went to the hot plate. There was a kettle on it already, and she took it off and reached up to a shelf over the hot plate and took down a flask, and unscrewed it, and poured something dark into it from the kettle, and screwed it shut again, and shook it, and then unscrewed the cup off its top and poured from the flask into the cup, and handed it to Idris. All of it quick and practiced, as though she had done it a hundred times. He understood, watching her, that she had.

"Drink," said Mother Rook.

He drank. It was hot and black and very bitter, and it was not tea. It tasted of something burnt, and of earth, and of metal.

"Chicory," said Rook, watching his face. "Grown in the vats down on Nine. Sump drinks chicory. Always has. Don't know why. Tea's for the Spokes."

"It's—" He did not know what it was. "Thank you."

"It's foul," said Lark, sitting down on the cot. "You can say it's foul. Everybody does, the first time."

He drank some more. It was foul. It was also, after a while, not, in some way he could not have explained: it was warm and it was strong and it tasted of a place. He held the cup in both hands. The flask Lark had poured it from was standing on the edge of the hot plate shelf, and he looked at it because it was in front of him, and because it was not like anything in the Spokes. It was steel, old, heavy, dull grey, worn smooth and bright at the shoulders where hands had held it, with a dent in one side.

"Your sister says you heard the children," said Mother Rook.

"Yes."

"And you knew the words."

"The first two lines," Idris said. "We say them. The Keepers. In the morning, at the rite. It's the invocation."

"I know what you say." Rook's black eyes did not move from his face. "Everybody knows what you say. You think the Sump doesn't hear what goes on in the gallery? Half your novices come from down here, Keeper, they just don't tell you." She lifted one knuckled hand an inch off the chair arm and set it down again. "You say two lines. We say all of it."

"Where does it come from?"

"From before."

"Before what?"

"Before me. Before my mother. Before hers." The old woman's face did not change. "It's old, Keeper. It's older than the Sump. It's older than anybody's name. That's all anybody knows, and anybody who tells you more is selling something." She paused. "My gran used to say it was a working song. A song you'd sing to keep the time, doing a job of work. But she didn't know what job. Nobody does."

"*Lock the door*," Idris said. "What door?"

Rook shrugged, a tiny movement under the shawls. "What door is ever locked against the Sump?" she said. "Pick one."

He did not have an answer to that. He drank the chicory. It was nearly gone. He tipped the cup to get the last of it, and the bottom of the cup came up, and he looked at it without meaning to; and then he looked past it, at the flask on the shelf, and leaned forward, and turned the flask a little with one finger so that its base caught the light from the lamp.

There were letters stamped in the steel of the base. Old letters, worn nearly flat, in a square plain face like the stencils on the corridor walls. He had to tilt it twice before he could make them out.

*TTH-9 · 0417*

"What's this?" he said.

"Lucky," said Mother Rook.

"I mean the letters. What do they mean?"

"Nobody knows that either." Rook watched him turn the flask in the lamplight. "It's been passed hand to hand down here longer than anybody can say. Never stays with one family. It goes where it's wanted. When somebody's in trouble, it goes to them for a while, and when the trouble's done they pass it on. That's the custom." Her black eyes went to Lark, and stayed there. "I've had it a while now. Longer than I ought. I'm waiting to see who wants it next."

Lark looked at the floor.

Idris set the flask back on the shelf, carefully, the way he would have set down an instrument in the gallery. He did not know why he did it so carefully. It was only an old flask, dented, with numbers on the bottom. His hand did not want to let go of it.

"More?" said Mother Rook.

He held out his cup, and Lark took the flask down, and poured.

# Chapter 6: Tribute

*The Dark · Year 419*

Veska went down the stair to the dock floor alone, with her lamp in one hand and her iron bar in the other, and the rest of us stayed at the rail and watched her go.

Aunt Veska had been the boss of Hold Fourteen since before I was born. She was not anybody's aunt that I knew of. She was a broad short woman with grey hair cropped close and forearms like a walker's, though she had never walked; she had worked the lungs, as a girl, down in the deep levels, and had come up out of them at thirty with a cough she never lost and a temper she never lost either, and the Hold had made her boss because nobody else wanted to be and because she was the only one who could make the lungs go when they stopped. She was not kind. She was fair, most of the time, which in the Dark is better. She walked across the dock floor toward the Reeve of Brine's ship with the iron bar resting on her shoulder as though she were going to mend a pipe, and stopped ten paces from the foot of the ramp, and set the end of the bar down on the deck with a clang that rang all round the dock.

"This is Hold Fourteen," she said. "I'm the boss of it. Who opened my lock?"

The big man in the long coat stopped coughing.

"I did," he said. "I've got the old words for it. The Keepers' words. I'm sorry for the fright." He did not sound sorry. He did not sound anything; his voice was deep and slow and a little hoarse, as though he had worn it out shouting a long time ago and had not bothered to get it mended. "I'm Kine. Absalom Kine. Reeve of Brine."

"I know who you are. Everybody knows who you are." Veska did not move. "You're not the water-boat. The water-boat's not due till the thaw month."

"No. I'm not the water-boat." He looked round the dock, slowly, at the junk piled in its corners, at the dead cranes, at the old letters on the walls. "But I brought water."

He lifted his hand, and two of his armored men went up the ramp into the ship and came down again a moment later rolling a tank between them, a big one, the kind the water-boats carry, frosted over and smoking in the air of the dock. They rolled it to the foot of the ramp and stood it up, and it rang like a bell when it settled, full. Then they went back up and brought another. And another. Six, in the end, in a row at the foot of the ramp, smoking.

At the rail beside me, somebody let out a breath.

Six tanks was a month's water for Hold Fourteen. More than a month. We had been on half-ration since the hot month, because the last water-boat had come in short, and the one before that. Six tanks was baths for the children. Six tanks was the algae vats topped up and the lungs' cooling loops refilled and nobody having to choose, for a while, whether to drink or to wash a wound.

"That's a gift," said the Reeve of Brine. "No price. That's to say I'm sorry for the fright."

"Nothing from Brine comes without a price," said Veska.

"No," the Reeve agreed. "But that does. Those six are a gift. What I've come to talk about is the rest."

He coughed again, then, a long rattling cough that bent him over and made the man he was leaning on take his weight, and the sound of it went round the dock and came back off the walls, and every one of us on the gallery knew what it was. You cannot live in Tithe and not know it. It was the rot. The Reeve of Brine had the rot in his chest, deep, the way Quill had it, the way Pim had had it at the end.

When it was done, he straightened, and wiped his mouth on the back of his glove, and looked up at the gallery again, along the rail, to where I was standing.

"I want to talk to your Tally," he said.

* * *

Quill would not be carried.

I had gone up for him, running, the whole way up the stair from the dock to the Tallies' room, with Ness behind me; and I had found him already sitting up on his bunk under the marks with his coat on and his stick in his hand, as though he had heard the big lock and known exactly what it meant. He was breathing very badly. When I tried to tell him what had happened he held up his hand, and I stopped.

"The Reeve of Brine," he said. "With his ship and his men and a present."

"Six tanks."

"Six." He nodded slowly. "He's not stupid, then. Good. I'd rather a clever one."

"He wants you."

"He wants the Tally," said Quill. "Help me up."

Ness and I got him up between us. He was so light. He stood swaying, with his hand gripping my arm through my sleeve, and his blind eyes turned toward the door, and he got his breath, and he said: "We walk. Down. All the way."

"Quill, it's eleven flights—"

"Then it'll take us a long time," he said, "and he'll have to wait for us. And everybody on that dock will see the Tally of Hold Fourteen come down the stair on his own feet, and not be carried down like a sack of old filters. And so will he." He squeezed my arm. "You think that doesn't matter. It matters. Walk."

We walked. It took nearly an hour. We had to stop on every landing, and on some of them twice, while he leaned on the wall and breathed with a sound like a lung's bellows full of grit, and I held him up and Ness held him up and neither of us said anything. The Hold had come out onto the stair. They stood in the doorways on every landing and watched us pass, and nobody said anything either, and some of them touched his coat as he went by, very lightly, the way you touch the funeral cloth.

When we came out onto the dock floor the Reeve was sitting on one of his own water tanks, with his long coat spread round him, waiting.

He had not gone back aboard. He had not sent anyone to hurry us. He had sat there on the cold tank for an hour, with the rot in his chest, and his men standing around him in their armor, and Veska standing across from him with her iron bar, and the Hold watching from the gallery, and he had waited. I did not know whether that was a good thing or a bad thing. When he saw us he stood up, slowly, and I saw that it cost him nearly as much as the stair had cost Quill.

He was big. Bigger than he had looked from the rail. Not fat; broad, made on a large frame, with a great head of grey hair going white and a grey beard cut close, and a face that had been handsome once and had been hit a great deal since. His coat was old. Older than him, much older. It was dark green, or had been, and it had brass at the cuffs, and the remains of some kind of insignia on the shoulders that had been picked off long ago and left darker shapes behind. Under it he wore armor like his men's, plates over padding. He had grey eyes, very pale. He looked at Quill, and then he looked at me, and then at the white string at my throat, and then back at Quill.

"Tally," he said. "Thank you for coming down."

"Reeve," said Quill. "Thank you for the water."

"You heard about that?"

"I'm blind," said Quill. "Not deaf. You could hear those tanks ring in Hold Ninety."

The Reeve's face moved, a little. It might have been a smile.

"Hold Ninety," he said. "Yes. I've been to Hold Ninety. I've been to all of them that still answer a lock. Ninety, and the Spokeholds, and the Ninth, and the Tens. I've been to their Tallies." He paused. "Ninety's Tally was a woman named Anselm. Did you know her?"

"I knew her."

"She died. In the hot month. On Brine." He said it flatly, like a man reporting the weather. "She was old, and she had the rot like you, and the cold of the crossing was too much for her, and she died. I'm sorry for it. She was a good woman. She sang me the Count before she went." He coughed, briefly. "But she didn't sing it all. She said she didn't know it all. She said Hold Ninety's line had broken in her grandmother's time, a Tally who died before she'd finished teaching her girl, and since then they'd had only the numbers and half the Door, and they'd been singing it that way for a hundred years. She said if I wanted the whole of it I should come here. To Fourteen. She said yours was the oldest line, and the only one that had never broken." He looked at Quill. "So I've come. And I'd like to hear it."

"The Count is for the dead," said Quill.

"Then sing it for mine. I've sent enough of them out the lock. More than you, Tally, I'd guess. A lot more." The pale grey eyes did not move. "Sing it for them."

Quill was quiet for a long time. His hand was on my arm. I could feel it shaking, and I did not know whether it was the stair or something else.

"Why?" he said at last.

"Because I'm going to wake the Halo," said the Reeve of Brine.

The dock was very quiet.

"Brine is dying," said the Reeve. He said it the same way he had said that the Tally of Ninety was dead: flatly, without weight, as if he had said it so many times that the words had worn smooth. "Not quickly. Not this year. But the ice is going. The roof. Brine's city is under the ice, you know that, it was cut into it in the Light, and the ice is the roof, and the ice is going rotten from underneath, because the old heat-pumps that kept it frozen are dying like your lungs are dying. Every year we lose a district. Last year it was two. In ten years it'll be the whole city, and then there won't be any more water-boats, Boss," he said to Veska, "because there won't be any Brine to send them. And the rot's in the city now. In me. You can hear it." He did not cough, as if to show that he would not. "Everything in the Reach that works was made in the Light. Everything. The lungs. The pumps. The ships. And everything that was made in the Light ran on the Light. The old records say so. They ran on it the way a lamp runs on oil, and when the Light went, they started to run down. And they've been running down for four hundred years, and now they're nearly stopped." He looked up, past the gallery, past the dock's dead lights, as though he could see through the hull to the Halo. "So I'm going to light it again."

Somebody on the gallery made a sound. Not a word. A kind of moan. I looked up and saw that it had come from old Geller, who kept the Lamplighters' little chapel on the ninth level, and that he had both hands pressed over his mouth, and that his eyes were full of tears.

"You can't light it," Veska said. "Nobody can. Nobody's even been to the Spindle in a hundred years. The ones who tried didn't come back."

"I know how many tried. I know how many didn't come back. I've got their names." The Reeve did not look away from Quill. "And I know that the Keepers left words behind them. A Rule. Most of it's lost. Some of it isn't. I've got a piece of it on Brine, an old book, burned, and the piece I've got says this." He spoke the words slowly, as if he had learned them by heart: "*The Knot is the Founders', and shall not be turned.*" He let the dock hear it. "Nobody knows what the Knot is. Nobody knows anything. But your Tallies have a rule too, don't they? Never sing the Count on the Halo. Ninety's Tally told me that one. She told me it was the First Tally's rule, the very first one, older than any of the knots on any of your strings." He leaned forward a little. "Why would you have a rule about where you could sing a funeral song, Tally? Unless it was about more than funerals?"

Quill said nothing.

"Sing it for me," said the Reeve. "That's all I'm asking, today. Sing it for my dead. All of it."

And Quill did.

He let go of my arm. He stood by himself on the dock floor with his stick in both hands, swaying a little, his blind face lifted toward the big man; and he took the breath all the way down, the way he had taught me, though I could hear it catch and rattle as it went; and he began. Not loud. As though he were speaking to someone just beside him.

His voice was a thread. But the dock was so quiet that the thread carried, all the way up to the gallery and round the walls.

"Ten for the Company," Quill sang. "Ten, and stop. Nine for the drop and eight for the dark."

I stood behind him and sang it with him in my head, the way I always did, keeping him true. Seven. Six and five. Four and three, two and one. All the numbers, all right. Then the Door.

"Twice to the dark," Quill sang, "and once to the light."

I stopped singing in my head.

"Seat it and pin it and turn it tight," Quill sang, "sun on your right and the Kettle above, dog to the place where the old ones go—"

It was wrong.

It was all wrong. Every line of the Door. Not much—a word here, a turn there—but wrong, every one, and in the wrong order, and in a voice that did not waver at all, so that anyone who did not know it would have thought he had sung it a thousand times exactly that way. I stood behind him with my mouth open. I had heard him sing the Count for the dead more times than I could count, and I had sung it with him, and I had been hit with his stick when I was seven for trying to make the low numbers rhyme, *a Tally keeps the Count, a Tally does not mend it*, and he was standing on the dock floor in front of the whole Hold and the Reeve of Brine and singing it false.

"That is the way that we close the door," Quill sang. "Count it back down, we are home once more."

He stopped. The thread of his voice went out across the dock and up the walls and was gone.

The Reeve of Brine looked at him for a long time.

"That's not what Ninety's Tally sang," he said at last.

"Ninety's line broke," said Quill. "You said so yourself. A hundred years of half a Door. Things drift."

"Do they." The Reeve stood very still. Then he smiled, a real smile this time, slow and tired, that showed a broken tooth at the side of his mouth. "You're a liar, old man. You're a good one. But I listened to that woman sing for nine days before she died, and I learned the Door the way she had it, the half of it, and I'd bet my ship your line goes the other way round from what you've just given me." He turned his head and looked at me. "And I'd bet my ship your girl knows it."

I did not say anything. I did not do anything. I kept my face as still as I could and looked at him. But I think he saw something in it anyway.

"Twenty days," the Reeve said. He turned back to Quill. "I'll come back in twenty days. Think about it, Tally. Talk to your boss. Talk to your Hold. And tell them this, so they understand." He looked up at the gallery, at all of us. "The water-boats from Brine are mine. When I come back, if I go away with the Count, the right one, the whole one, then Hold Fourteen gets water from Brine for as long as there's a Brine to send it. A full ration. And lungs. I've got scrubbers in Brine, three of them, whole, from the old districts that went under the ice. I'll send you two. That's the price. Two lungs and a full ration of water for a song." He looked back at Quill. "And if I go away without it, the water-boats don't stop here any more. Not ever. Not for anything."

He turned, slowly, leaning on his man, and went up the ramp into his ship.

And as he went, I saw for the first time that there was someone else at the top of the ramp, in the shadow of the hatch, who had been there the whole time: a thin man, very thin, gaunt, in a long grey robe, with his hands in front of him in iron cuffs joined by a chain. His head was shaved. On his forehead, in black ink, there was a mark I knew, because I had seen it on old Geller's forehead and on the doors of the Lamplighters' chapel on the ninth level: a little flame, inside a ring.

He was looking at me. He had been looking at me, I understood, all through the Count. His eyes were dark and deep-set and very bright in his starved face, and they did not leave me as the Reeve went past him into the ship, and they did not leave me as the ramp began to rise, and the last thing I saw before the hatch closed was the little black flame on his forehead, and his eyes, burning.

* * *

"Why did you lie?"

We were back in the Tallies' room. It was the middle of the night. Quill had not been able to walk back up the stair, and in the end, at the third landing, he had let Ness carry him, and she had carried him up the rest of the way in her arms as easily as a bundle of cable, and laid him on his bunk, and gone. Now he was lying under the marks with his fingers on them and his eyes open, and his breath came and went with that grinding bellows sound, and between breaths, sometimes, there was a long time.

"Sit down," he said.

I sat on the floor by the bunk, with my back against the wall, where I had sat since I was seven.

"Why did you lie?" I said again. "You told me a Tally doesn't mend the Count. You hit me. You said—"

"I said a Tally keeps the Count." He turned his head toward my voice. "Keeping is not singing it to everyone who brings you water. Keeping is keeping." He breathed. "Never sing the Count on the Halo. You know that rule."

"Yes."

"The Reeve of Brine wants to take the Count to the Halo. To the Spindle. And sing it there." He breathed. "That's all he wants. He's a clever man. He's put it together better than anybody has in four hundred years. A rule about where you can sing a song is a rule about what the song does. He's right about that. He's right."

"What does it do?"

"I don't know," said Quill. "I told you. Nobody knows is not the same as no reason. I don't know what the Count does on the Halo. Sorrel didn't know. Abel didn't know. None of us knew. The First Tally knew, and he didn't tell, and he made a rule instead. And for four hundred years we've kept it, and we've kept the song, and we haven't asked." He was quiet for a long time. "That's what keeping means. You keep a thing until somebody who knows what it's for comes to use it."

"And the Reeve?"

"The Reeve doesn't know what it's for. He only knows it's for something." Quill's fingers moved on the marks. "That's not the same."

I sat with my back to the wall and my knees drawn up.

"He'll stop the water," I said.

"Yes."

"If he stops the water—"

"I know what happens if he stops the water." Quill's voice was very quiet. "Veska will know too. And the Hold. They'll all know, by tomorrow. And they'll look at you. I'm sorry for that. I'm sorry I won't be here for it." He breathed. "Ask me again."

"What?"

"The other rule. You asked me, after Pim, and I said not today. Ask me again."

"What's the other rule?"

"Never let one Tally be the only one who knows it." He turned his face toward me. His blind eyes were wet. "That's the First Tally's other rule. The one we don't say in the lineage. The Count must always be kept by more than one. That's why every Tally takes a girl, or a boy, young, and teaches them, all of it, before the old one goes. So that it's never in only one head. Because a head can stop." He breathed. "Ninety's Tally broke it. Not on purpose. Her grandmother died too soon. And then there was half a Door in Hold Ninety for a hundred years, and now there's none, because she's dead on Brine. The Spokeholds' line broke when I was a boy. The Tens' Tally drowned in a vat. There were nine lines when Sorrel was young. There are three now that I know of, and two of them are broken, and the third is in this room."

I understood then what he was telling me, and I did not want to.

"You're the only one," I said.

"I was the only one," said Quill, "until I taught you. Now there are two." His hand found my shoulder in the dark. "And very soon there will be one again. And that one will be you, Tally's girl. And you will have broken the First Tally's rule, through no fault of your own, the moment I'm gone. Do you see?"

"Yes."

"So you'll have to teach someone. Quickly. Whatever else happens. Before he comes back." His hand tightened. "Promise me."

"I promise."

"Good." His hand loosened. He lay back. His breathing was very bad now, long grinding pulls and then a gap, and then another. "What's your name?"

"What?"

"Your name. Your mother's name for you. Before you were the Tally's girl. A Tally gives it up, when she takes the string. You know that. You'll give it up tomorrow, or whenever it is. What was it?"

I had not said it aloud since I was seven.

"Mira," I said.

"Mira." He said it as though he were tasting it. "That's a good name. It means something, in one of the old languages. Something about looking." He was quiet. "Say it once more. Then put it away."

"Mira," I said.

"Good," said Quill. "Now put it away."

I put it away.

He died a little before the morning bell. I was asleep against the wall by his bunk when he went, and I did not hear it, and when I woke his breathing had stopped and his fingers were still on the marks, on the wall above his head, resting there, on the last row, as if he had been counting them in the dark and had lost his place.

* * *

I sent him out alone.

That is not the custom. The custom is that the Hold comes, and stands down the corridor, and sings under the Tally's voice. But the Hold was not speaking to me that morning, or to Quill, or to anybody; the Hold had heard the Reeve, as Quill had said it would, and had gone quiet, and was in its rooms with the doors shut, thinking about water. Veska came. She stood a little way down the corridor with her arms folded, and did not sing. Ness came, and stood beside me, and held my hand while I turned the wheel, and did not sing either, because Ness cannot sing at all and has never pretended to, and I loved her for not pretending then.

I washed him and folded the good cloth back from him at the last, the blue cloth with the gold edge, and laid him on the bench in the little round room in his old coat, with his stick beside him. I put my hand on the wheel. I touched the string at my throat. It was still white. It had his knot on it now, the first knot, a big one with a twist in it, that I had tied myself that morning with my hands shaking, because there was no one else to tie it.

"Ten for the Company," I sang. "Ten, and stop."

I sang it the right way. All of it. The numbers and the Door, every word, in the order he had taught me, going down. My voice did not shake. I thought it would and it did not. I turned the wheel a quarter turn on every line, and through the round port he lay on the bench with his blind face turned up toward the ceiling, and I sang him home.

When it was done and the lock had cycled and the bench was empty, Veska unfolded her arms.

"Twenty days," she said.

"I know."

"He meant it. About the water."

"I know he meant it."

"Two lungs," said Veska. "And a full ration. For as long as there's a Brine." She looked at me, and her face was hard, and tired, and not unkind. "You think about that, Tally. That's all I'm going to say. You think about what a song's worth."

She went away down the corridor.

Ness and I stood by the lock. After a while Ness let go of my hand and wiped her face with her sleeve and said, roughly, "So you're it now."

"I'm it."

"Tally of Hold Fourteen."

"Yes."

"What happens if you die?"

"Then there isn't one." I looked at the empty bench through the port. "Then there's nobody. Then it's gone."

Ness was quiet for a while.

"Teach me, then," she said.

I looked at her. She was looking at the lock, not at me, with her jaw set so that the burn scar stood out on it black.

"You can't sing," I said.

"I can learn."

"Ness, you can't sing *at all*. You sang at the thaw feast once and Barrow's boy cried."

"Barrow's boy cries at everything." She turned and looked at me then, and she was not laughing. "I mean it. You heard him. The old man. It's not supposed to be in only one head. So put it in mine. I'm not a Tally, I don't care about the string, I don't need the lineage. Just put it in me. In case." She held my eyes. "In case something happens. On a walk. Or with the Reeve. Or anything."

I wanted to say no. I wanted to say that it was not hers to have, that it was the Tallies', that it had been kept in Hold Fourteen's line for four hundred years from the First Tally down and that Quill had hit me with his stick for mending a rhyme. I had been the Tally of Hold Fourteen for half a morning.

"All right," I said.

So we sat on the floor of the Tallies' room, under the marks, with Quill's bunk empty beside us, and I put my fingers on hers and put her fingers on the First Count, row by row, the way Quill had put mine when I was seven; and then I began to teach her the Count, from the top, from ten, a line at a time; and she was terrible at it. She could not hold a note to save her life. She got the words wrong and the numbers wrong and the turns of the Door wrong, and swore, and started again, and got them wrong again. We did it until the evening bell. By then she had the numbers, all of them, and the first two lines of the Door.

"That's enough for one day," I said.

"No it's not," said Ness. "Again."

# Chapter 7: Insured Losses

*The Combine · FY 2291*

The Director saw the gang chiefs on Day Two Hundred and Fifty-Eight, in the long room on the top deck of the admin stack where the Company held its inquiries.

There were thirty-one of them. Thirty-one gangs on the Ring, thirty-one chiefs, and every one of them had put in a request to see the Director in the eleven days since the speakers came on, and every one of them had been docked a day for it, and then on the morning of the twelfth day a notice had appeared on their terminals: *The Site Director will meet with gang chiefs at the second hour of first shift. Attendance is compulsory. Time will be charged as Company time.* As though, Juno said, he were doing them a favor by not docking them for coming to be lied to.

They stood. There were chairs, but nobody had told them to sit, and nobody did. The room had a carpet, real carpet, blue, worn grey in a path from the door to the long table at the far end, and the walls were paneled in something that looked like wood and might even have been, and there were windows, big ones, double-glazed and clean, looking out over the Ring. Sol had never been in it before. She had been in the inquiry room on the deck below twice, once for Dov and once for a cracked cassette that had not been her gang's fault and that she had been docked for anyway, and that room had had no carpet and no windows. She stood at the back with her hands behind her and looked out at the Ring curving away in the work lights, and at the Spindle standing up off the rim nine kilometers round, like a needle, and at Kettle filling everything, and she waited.

Imre Castellane came in by a side door with two aides and a Proctor, and the room went quiet.

He was not what she had expected, the first time she had seen him, at Dov's inquiry. She had expected someone harder. He was a tall soft-shouldered man of fifty-five or so with a big handsome tired face, grey at the temples, and he wore the Company's dark blue undress coat with the collar open, as though he had been working late and had not had time to button it, and perhaps he had. He had kind eyes. She had noticed that at Dov's inquiry, too, and had hated him for it, and had gone on hating him for it for three years; and she found now, seeing him come in and look round the room at all of them, really look, one face after another, that she could not quite remember how.

"Sit down," he said. "Please. All of you. I'm sorry nobody told you to sit."

They sat. He did not. He stood at the head of the long table and put his hands flat on it and looked down the room.

"I know why you're here," he said. "I know you've all asked to see me, and I know you were docked for asking, and I want to say first that I've reversed those docks, all of them, as of this morning. It shouldn't have happened. That's not how I want this platform run, and I'm sorry." There was a murmur. He waited for it to pass. "I also know what you want to ask me. You want to ask me about the Lighting. You want to know why it's been brought forward, and what it means for you. And you deserve answers. So I'm going to give you as many as I can."

He gave them a great many. He talked for most of an hour, without notes, in a low, clear, reasonable voice, and Sol, at the back, listened to every word.

There was a phase instability in the governor. It had been identified by Dr. Haddad's team in the course of routine modeling. It was not dangerous now, but it would become dangerous if the Ring were left unlit for too long in its present state, because the phase-lines were already seated and partly coupled, and Kettle's field was working on them every day, and an unlit Ring with coupled lines was like—he looked for the image—like a bow drawn and held. You could hold it for a while. Not forever. The longer you held it the worse it would be when it went. So the board, on Dr. Haddad's advice, had authorized an early Lighting. It was the safest course. It was, in fact, the only safe course.

The skirt work was suspended because the skirt was no longer needed. Dr. Haddad's revised models showed that the hab rings, at the platform's present distance from the Ring, would receive a dose from first light well within the Company's safety limits. The skirt had been designed under a conservative specification, at a time when first light was less well understood. Engineers, he said, and smiled a little, loved redundancy; they would wear a belt and braces to bed if you let them. But the platform would not be relying on the hab rings' rating alone. In the days before the Lighting, the mooring arm would be released, and the platform would be stood off, under tow, to a distance of four hundred kilometers, behind the shoulder of Gall, where it would be entirely shadowed. The *Assessor* would tow her. The tugs would assist.

As for the *Mercy*—he spread his hands. Yes, she was late. Haulers were late. The outer relay was old and had failed before. He had no reason to think anything was wrong with her, and every reason to think she would arrive on Day Three Hundred as scheduled, and that every bonded worker on the platform whose term was complete would be aboard her when she left. He would be on her himself. He was going home too.

"I have a daughter," he said. "On Ostrava. Annick. She's eleven. She was four when I came out here. I've seen her on a screen twice a year, when the courier comes. I want to go home and see her as much as any of you want to see your families. More, maybe." He looked down the table. "We've been out here a long time. Some of you longer than me. We've built something here that no one has ever built before. In thirty days, we're going to light it, and the Reach will be open, and everything that comes after—every ship, every colony, every child born out here for a thousand years—will come through a door that you built. Your hands. I want you to remember that. I want you to be proud of it, because I am. And then I want us all to go home."

There was a silence when he stopped. Not an angry one. Sol could feel the room. They wanted to believe him. She wanted to believe him. It came off the thirty other chiefs like heat off a hull.

Oyelaran put his hand up.

"The *Mercy*," he said. "If she's late. If she's not here on Day Three Hundred. What then?"

"Then she'll be here on Day Three Ten," said Castellane, "and we'll all have had ten more days to admire the view." There was a laugh, a small one, and Oyelaran sat back.

Sol did not put her hand up. She waited until it was over and the chiefs were filing out past the aides, talking low, some of them almost cheerful, and she went the other way, up the room along the grey path in the carpet, to where the Director stood by the head of the table talking quietly to one of the aides, and she said, "Director."

He turned. The Proctor by the side door turned too, and took a step, and Castellane lifted a hand without looking at him, and the Proctor stopped.

"Chief Dace," said Castellane. "Gang Four."

She had not expected him to know her name. Then she remembered Dov's inquiry, and thought he probably did not, that the aide had a list. Then she saw his face, and thought he probably did.

"I'd like to ask you something," she said. "In private. If that's allowed."

He looked at her for a moment, and something moved behind the kind eyes; and then he smiled and said, "Of course. Come through," and opened the side door himself, and held it.

* * *

His office was small and warm, and it smelled of coffee.

Real coffee. Sol had not smelled it in twelve years except on the clothes of Company staff passing in the spine, and it hit her in the doorway like a hand on the chest. There was a pot of it on a hot plate on a shelf behind his desk. The desk was wood, real, dark and old and scarred, and it was too big for the room, and on it there was a tablet and a stack of paper—paper—and a model of the Ring, a meter across, in brass and black enamel, with the Spindle standing up off its rim in silver and a tiny platform moored beside it, and Kettle, in the middle, a ball of dark glass with bands of color in it. And a picture, in a frame. A girl, in a blue school coat, laughing at something off to the side.

There was someone else in the room. Sol did not see him until she was inside, because he was sitting in the corner behind the door in an armchair, very still, reading. A thin man in grey, with thin grey hair combed straight back from a high forehead, and rimless spectacles, and a paper book open on his knee. He did not look up.

"This is Mr. Lind," said Castellane. "From the board. He's observing. Don't mind him." He went round the desk. "Coffee?"

"No," said Sol. "Thank you."

He poured himself one. His hands, she noticed, were not quite steady, either. He sat down and gestured at the chair in front of the desk, and she sat, on the edge of it.

"You wanted to ask me something," he said.

"The mooring arm," Sol said.

He waited.

"You said the platform would be stood off. Released, and towed to Gall." She kept her voice level. "The mooring arm was welded. Last year. Day One Hundred and Ninety, last fiscal. Kettle's tides were working the coupling loose, and Gangs Four and Seven welded the coupling solid, all the way round. Forty meters of weld. I ran the bead myself on the north face." She looked at him. "It's not a coupling any more. It's not designed to release. To stand the platform off you'd have to cut it. That's two gangs and nine days, if nothing goes wrong. And nobody's been tasked to cut it."

Castellane looked at her over his coffee.

"And the *Assessor*," Sol said, "is a command ship. She's not a tug. She's got maybe a tenth the pull she'd need to shift the platform's mass. With all our tugs together on it, the *Patience* and the other six, you'd still need a month to move her four hundred kilometers, and you'd need to start now. Today. And you haven't." She stopped. "That's what I wanted to ask. Sir."

The room was quiet. In the corner, the thin man turned a page.

"You're very well informed, Chief," said Castellane at last.

"I'm a rigger. I built it."

"Yes." He set the coffee down. He looked at the model of the Ring on his desk, at the tiny platform beside it, and touched it with one finger, the way a man touches a sore tooth with his tongue. "The engineers have a plan for the coupling. It may be that they intend to cut it closer to the date. It may be that the tow is staged. I'm not an engineer, Chief Dace. I'm an administrator. I take the plans the engineers give me and I make sure they're carried out." He looked up. "If there's a problem with the plan, I'll take it up with Dr. Haddad. I promise you that."

"And if the plan is that there isn't a plan?"

"Then I'll take that up with her, too."

She looked at him. He looked back at her. And she saw it again, the thing that moved behind his eyes; and this time she could name it. It was not cruelty. She had seen cruelty on Company faces, on Lusk's, on Fenn's, sometimes, when he was in the mood. It was not contempt. It was something quieter and much worse. It was the look of a man who had already made his peace with something, and who was very tired, and who wanted, more than anything, for her to stop asking him to make it again.

"What's your bond number, Chief?" said the thin man in the corner.

She turned. He had not looked up from his book.

"What?"

"Your bond number," said Mr. Lind. His voice was soft and dry and precise, like paper being folded. "For the record. Of this meeting."

"Zero four one seven."

"Thank you." He took a pencil out of his breast pocket, a real pencil, and wrote something in the margin of his book, and put the pencil back, and turned a page.

Castellane stood up. "Thank you for coming, Chief Dace," he said. "I mean that. I'll look into the coupling."
