He came round the desk and opened the door for her, and as she went past him into the long room with the blue carpet, he said, very quietly, so quietly that afterward she was not sure he had said it at all: "Look after your gang."

* * *

The revised work schedules went up on the terminals that night. The Ring was to be finished in thirty days instead of fifty. Shifts went to eleven hours. Rest cycles were cut from three to two. Suit air was not increased. Nobody was docked for anything that week, which frightened people more than the docks had.

On Day Two Hundred and Sixty-One, in the ninth hour of a shift that should have ended in the eighth, Pell Okafor put a plasma cutter through the sleeve of his own liner.

It was not his fault. Sol said so to the medic, and to the shift engineer, and to Fenn, who came round with his tablet. It was the schedule's fault. Gang Four had been out for nine hours and on two rest cycles for three days running, and the boy had been working one-handed at the end of his line on a seam the cutter would not reach, and his other hand had gone numb with cold in a glove whose heater was failing, and he had not felt the cutter's nozzle drift. The flame did not go through the suit. It went through the outer layer of the sleeve and the insulation and stopped at the inner pressure layer, which held, as it was built to; and so instead of venting his arm to vacuum, it only cooked it, from the elbow to the wrist, through the liner, for the two seconds it took him to notice.

He did not scream. Sol had been waiting for him to. He said, "Chief," in an odd, interested voice, as Kostya had said *oh*. And then he said, "Chief, I think I've done something," and she looked over and saw the white smoke coming off his sleeve in the vacuum, boiling away in a thin bright plume.

They got him in. Juno flew the *Patience* back faster than Sol had ever seen her fly, and Bracken carried him from the dock to the infirmary in his arms like a child, in his suit, because they could not get the suit off him without taking the arm with it; and in the infirmary they cut the suit away while he lay on the table with his eyes open and his teeth clenched, and the smell came up, and Sol held his other hand and did not look.

The medic was a Company man named Amsel, thin and fair and exhausted, who had been on the platform two years and was owed, he said once, more sleep than anybody alive. He worked on Pell's arm for an hour. When he was done the arm was wrapped from elbow to fingers in a gel sleeve that glowed faintly blue, and Pell was asleep, because Amsel had put him to sleep, and Amsel sat down heavily on a stool by the instrument cabinet and put his face in his hands.

"He'll keep the arm," he said, through his fingers. "He won't have much of the use of it. Not for a long time. Not for work."

"How long?"

"A year. Two. With grafts. On Ostrava." He took his hands away from his face and looked at her. "Not here. I don't have the grafts here. He needs to be on the *Mercy*."

"The *Mercy*'s late."

"I know," said Amsel. "I've heard." He got up, slowly, like an old man. "I'm going to get some water. You can stay with him. Don't touch anything."

He went out. Sol sat on the stool by the table and held Pell's good hand, which was big and cracked and cold, and looked round the infirmary because there was nothing else to look at.

It was not a big room. Four beds, three of them empty. Cabinets. A terminal. A wall screen showing the platform's air and power figures, green, green, green. And on the counter by the terminal, under Amsel's elbow, where he had pushed it aside to make room for the burn kit, a stack of paper.

She would not have looked at it if it had been on a screen. Screens were Company; you did not look at a Company screen any more than you looked at a Proctor. But it was paper, and paper was rare enough on the platform that it drew the eye, and she looked, the way you look at anything strange, and saw the top sheet, and saw what was printed across the top of it in the Company's blue letterhead, and read it without meaning to, upside down.

*OSTRAVA MUTUAL ASSURANCE SOCIETY*  
*CONSTRUCTION LOSS POLICY OM/2291/0044*  
*SCHEDULE OF INSURED BONDED LABOUR — TRANSIT RING TTH-9*

She let go of Pell's hand. She stood up and went to the counter, and she did not touch anything, and she read.

It was a form. It had the look of forms, boxes and lines and small print, and most of it she did not understand. There was a Policy Period, which began on a day four years ago and ended on Day Two Hundred and Ninety of the current year. There was a Claimant, which was the Ostrava-Halvard Combine. There was a box labeled *Per Capita Indemnity (Remaining Bond Value + Completion Liability Avoided)*, with a figure in it so large that she read it three times. There was a box labeled *Medical Certification of Insured Status*, with a line for a signature, and under the line, *Site Medical Officer*, and on the line, in blue ink, still a little wet, *R. Amsel*. And there was a box labeled *Projected Loss Event*, and in it, typed, three letters and three numbers.

*D288.*

Under the first sheet there was a second. And a third. She turned the corner of the first one up with her fingernail, not touching the rest, and saw them: lists. Names and numbers, in columns, in small type, page after page. *Schedule A.* She ran her eye down the top one. *ABARA J. 0012. ADEYEMI T. 0388.* Down the next. She turned them, carefully, by the corners, one after another, until she came to the D's.

*DACE M. 0417. GANG 4. REMAINING BOND VALUE: 42 DAYS. COMPLETION LIABILITY: 1. INSURED STATUS: CERTIFIED.*

Under her name, *LEM K. 0233*, with a line through it, and in the margin in blue ink, *deceased D239, removed*. And *OKAFOR P. 0901*, typed in at the bottom of the page in a different face, new. *REMAINING BOND VALUE: 4,372 DAYS. INSURED STATUS:* and a blank, waiting for Amsel.

She heard his steps in the corridor. She did not decide to do it. She found the page with her name on it, and slid it out from the stack, gently, by its edges, the way you slide a cassette into a cradle, and folded it in four, and put it inside her liner against her skin, and was back on the stool holding Pell's hand when Amsel came in with two cups of water and gave her one.

He did not look at the counter. He sat down on the other stool and drank his water.

"Thank you," Sol said. "For his arm."

"It's my job," said Amsel. He looked at the boy on the table, at the blue sleeve glowing on his arm in the dim light. "God knows it's my job."

* * *

"What's indemnity?" she said.

Teague was sitting on his bunk with his back to the wall and the folded sheet open on his knees, holding it close to the work lamp, because his eyes were going. He did not answer her. He read it all the way through, slowly, his lips moving. Then he read it again. Bay Fourteen was dark except for the lamp. Bracken and Juno were asleep, or pretending to be. Pell's bunk was empty.

"Teague."

"It means insured," he said. He did not look up. "Indemnity's what they pay you if you're insured and you lose the thing you insured. A ship. A cargo. You insure the cargo, the ship goes down, the underwriters indemnify you. Pay you what it was worth."

"So we're insured."

"We're insured." He put one finger on a line of the sheet. "Not *us*. You see? *Insured bonded labour*. Not insured bonded laborers. The labor. Our bonds. What's left of them. What we're still worth to the Company, in days." He moved his finger. "And completion liability. That's the bonus. The completion bonus they owe every bond that finishes its term. And the passage home. Fifteen thousand a head, the passage on the *Mercy*. That's liability. If you don't finish your term, they don't owe it." He moved his finger again. "*Avoided.*"

"Insured against what?"

Teague looked up at her for the first time. In the light of the work lamp his face was the color of old paper, and his lips were blue.

"Against us dying, Sol," he said. "Construction loss. If the bonded labor is lost in an accident during construction, inside the policy period, the underwriters pay the Company everything we'd have been worth to them if we'd lived. Plus everything they'd have had to pay us if we'd lived." He laid the sheet down flat on his knees and smoothed it with both hands. "That's the policy. That's an ordinary policy. Every yard has one. The Combine's had them since the docks; it's how they could afford to build in the first place. That's not the thing."

"Then what's the thing."

He touched the box with his fingertip.

"There's a date on it," he said. "Policies don't have a loss date. You don't know when a loss is going to happen. That's why you insure. That's what insurance is. You pay so that if something happens, someday, you're covered." He tapped the box. "*Projected loss event.* That's a date they know. Two days before the policy runs out. And the policy runs out two days after the Lighting, and the Lighting was moved up to fit." He looked at her. "You see it? Day Two Ninety the policy ends, and after that we're just bonds, and they owe us. Day Two Eighty-Eight we're insured. The Lighting got moved to the last day we're worth more dead."

Sol stood in the middle of Bay Fourteen in the dark.

"Maybe it's a mistake," she said. "Maybe it's—that's the Lighting date, so they wrote it in, that's all. Because that's the riskiest day. Because something might go wrong on the day. Maybe it's routine."

"Maybe," Teague said. He folded the sheet up again along its creases and held it out to her. "You can ask."

"Ask who?"

"Whoever certified the phase instability," said Teague, "that the Lighting got moved up for."

* * *

There was one place on the platform where you were allowed to smoke, and it was the observation blister on top of the admin stack, a bubble of thick glass the size of a bunkroom stuck on the stack's roof like a blister on a thumb, with a scrubber all of its own and a bench around the inside of it. Bonded labor was not permitted. Sol had never been up there. She went up there at the fourth hour of third shift, when the admin stack was dark, by the maintenance ladder in the stack's core, which she knew about because Gang Four had replaced its rungs two years ago; and she came up through the hatch in the blister's floor, quietly, and the air in the blister smelled of smoke.

Haddad was alone on the bench, with her back to the hatch and her knees drawn up, looking out at the Ring.

She did not turn round. From the blister you could see all of it, the whole Ring at once, the way Kostya had seen it from out past the chain: the band of dark metal going round, and the gold lines on its face, and the gangs' work lights crawling on it, more of them than there had ever been, because the shifts were long now and the gangs never stopped; and the Spindle standing up off the rim, small and sharp; and Kettle filling it, turning, sending a long slow sheet of lightning across its face as Sol watched.

There was a thin white stick in Haddad's fingers, burning. She lifted it to her mouth and drew on it and the tip glowed.

"You're not allowed up here, Chief Dace," she said, without turning.

"No."

"I could call Lusk."

"You could."

Haddad drew on the stick again. Then she turned on the bench, slowly, and looked at Sol over her shoulder; and Sol saw her face without a visor for the first time, narrow and dark and lined, with the black eyebrows, and the eyes that had not looked at anybody on the Spindle. They were looking at her now. They were red-rimmed, as though from smoke, or from something else.

Sol took the folded sheet out of her liner and unfolded it and held it up so the doctor could see the blue letterhead.

Haddad looked at it for a long moment. She did not reach for it. She did not look surprised. She looked, Sol thought, like someone who has been waiting for a long time for a ship that is overdue, and has just seen its lights.

"Is it routine?" Sol said. "Teague says it might be routine. That they put the date on because it's the Lighting, and that's the dangerous day. Tell me it's routine, Doctor, and I'll go back down the ladder and you'll never see me again."

Haddad put the white stick out against the glass, carefully, until it was quite dead. Then she stood up. She was shorter than Sol had thought; the white suit had made her bigger.

"Not here," she said.

# Chapter 8: Substitution

*The Concord · 336 AL*

The Register desks of the Third Spoke lane were in the Spindle's lower gallery, a long low room cut into the tower's flank two hundred meters above the rim, where the Keepers on the Register rota sat in a row behind a sloping counter of brass and glass and watched the ships come and go. There were six desks. Each had its own bank of gauges, and its own great book, open on a lectern of black Brine wood, and its own lamp; and each faced a window, a tall narrow slot of armored glass through which the Keeper could see his lane, a lit corridor of beacons running down from the Spindle's shoulder into the Eye.

Idris had sat at the third desk two days in seven for nine years. He knew it the way he knew his own rooms. He knew the scratch on the brass by his left hand where some Keeper before him had dropped a stylus, and the slight sticky drag of the mass-balance dial's bezel, and the smell of the lamp oil, and the sound the book's pages made when he turned them, a dry whisper like a breath. He knew the lane's beacons by their colors, and the governor's voice in every weather, and he could feel a ship arriving in his teeth before it broke the surface.

He felt the *Clemency* coming a full minute before she surfaced, and the hair stood up on his arms.

It was the fourth bell of second watch, the dead hour, when the lanes were quiet. The novice at the second desk, a long-necked boy from the Sump named Tobiah who had been with the Keepers six months and was still afraid of the gauges, was copying out a phase table by lamplight and did not look up. The Eye was breathing slow and even, far below the window, its color at the center deepening toward that violet-black that had no name. And under the floor, under the brass, under Idris's feet, the Spindle's voice began to change.

A ship coming through the Eye was a weight arriving. You felt it in the governor the way you felt a man step onto the far end of a plank you were standing on: the faintest shift, a settling, as the Eye took the ship's mass from its sister on the far side and the governor breathed to hold the phase. An ore barge, forty thousand tonnes, was a step. A House courier was a cat landing. Idris had felt big ships, too, in nine years: the colony haulers that came through twice a year from the Inward with their holds full of settlers bound for Brine and Gall, a hundred and fifty thousand tonnes, that made the gallery's lamps tremble on their chains.

This was not a step. This was the whole plank going down.

The mass-balance dial began to move. He watched it. It went past the forty-thousand mark without slowing, past the hundred, past the hundred and fifty where the colony haulers stopped. The lamp on its chain above his desk began to swing, very slightly. At the second desk Tobiah looked up from his phase table with his mouth open.

"Keeper—"

"I know."

The dial went past two hundred thousand. Two hundred and fifty. The governor's voice was a deep hum now, in the floor and the walls and his teeth, a note he had never heard it make. The Eye, far below the window, swelled; its color at the center went darker, and the blue-white at its rim brightened, and the slow breathing of it caught, for a moment, the way a man's breath catches when he lifts something heavy.

Then the *Clemency* surfaced.

She came up out of the Eye's center slow and black and very long, and as she cleared the light he saw her shape, and she was a freighter. That was what she looked like. A big one, ungainly, with a blunt nose and a long hull of cargo frames and a cluster of engines at her stern, painted in a dull House grey with nothing on her flank but a registry number and her name in white. She looked like a hundred freighters he had watched come through his lane. Except that she rode deep, so deep in the light that she seemed to drag it up with her as she rose, a long bright wake of it peeling off her hull; and except for the dial, which had stopped, finally, trembling, at three hundred and eighty-eight thousand tonnes.

He sat and looked at it. The lamp swung above him on its chain and slowed and was still.

Three hundred and eighty-eight thousand tonnes was not a freighter. A freighter of that size, loaded to her frames with Gall ore, would come through at a hundred and twenty. Three hundred and eighty-eight was ten freighters. It was a hull of solid armor plate and engines and reactors and something else besides, something very heavy, packed into a frame built to look as though it held nothing at all.

"Keeper Vey," said Tobiah, in a small voice. "That's—"

"The dial stuck," said Idris.

He did not know he was going to say it until it was out. He heard his own voice say it, quite calmly, and turned on his stool and looked at the boy, and saw the boy's long earnest face, and his eyes going from Idris to the dial and back.

"It stuck," Idris said. "On the bezel. It does that. You've seen it. It drags." He reached out and put his finger on the dial's bezel, the bezel he knew so well, the one with the slight sticky drag, and turned it a little, and the needle jumped and swung and settled at zero. "It hangs up when a big one comes through. Then you have to knock it. Old brass." He made himself smile. "You'll learn. I'll show you how to knock it."

Tobiah looked at the dial. He looked at Idris. He was sixteen, and from the Sump, and he had been a Keeper six months; and Idris was a Keeper-Adjunct with nine years on the Register and a white hook on his breast and his father's line round his neck. Idris watched the boy decide to believe him. It took about a breath.

"Yes, Keeper," said Tobiah, and went back to his phase table.

Idris turned to the book.

It lay open on its lectern in the lamplight, the right-hand page half full, in his own small square hand. *Second watch, fourth bell. Lane Three. Inbound.* He dipped the stylus. He had written ten thousand entries in this book. He wrote the name. *Clemency.* He wrote the registry number, from the slate the lane-beacon had sent up. He wrote *Ore barge, returning, empty.* And then he wrote the mass.

*40,000 t.*

He looked at it for a while. It looked like every other entry on the page. Then he took the seal from its box on the desk, and the stick of grey wax, and held the wax to the lamp until it dripped, and let three drops fall on the page beside the entry, and pressed the seal into them, the hook, and lifted it, and initialed beneath. *I.V.*

His hand did not shake. He watched it not shake. Of everything he did that night, that was what he remembered afterward, for the rest of his life: that he had watched his own hand write a lie into the oldest book in the Reach, and seal it, and initial it, and that his hand had been perfectly steady.

* * *

The reconciliation came through two days later.

He was not there for it. Oake had said he need not be. A Guard came to the rooms on the forty-first level in the middle of the afternoon with a slate and a sealed paper, while Idris was at the Spindle and Lark was at home, and read the paper aloud in the doorway, and then took a short sharp knife from his belt and cut the red cord from her wrist, and went away. Lark told him this in the evening, flatly, when he came home and found her sitting at the table with the cut cord in front of her on the wood, a little red loop of silk, frayed at the ends.

"He said there'd been an error," she said. "In the Drum. Two disks stuck together. Mine and somebody else's. The wrong one was read out." She did not look up from the cord. "He said the Levy Office apologizes for the inconvenience."

"Lark—"

"Who was it?"

He stood in the doorway in his habit. He had thought about this moment for two days and he had not thought of what he would say.

"I don't know," he said.

It was true. He did not know. Oake had told him not to concern himself with the list, and he had not, and he had not asked, and he had told himself that not asking was the same as not knowing, and he had told himself that so often in two days that it had worn smooth.

Lark looked up at him.

"You don't know," she said.

"No."

"You went to the Levy Office. And you did something. And you don't know what." She picked up the cord and turned it in her fingers. "You know it's two thousand. Everybody knows it's two thousand. You know that if it's not me it's somebody else. And you didn't ask who."

"It was an error. In the Drum—"

"*Don't*." She stood up so fast her chair went over behind her. "Don't you dare say that to me. Don't you *dare* say it was the Drum." She held the cord up between them. "Who was it, Idris? Who did you give them?"

He did not answer. He could not.

She looked at him for a long moment. Then she put the red cord in her pocket and went past him, out of the door, and down the stairs, and did not come back.

He waited until the middle of the night. Then he went down after her.

He knew the way now. The service stair with the rusted gate, the door with NO ACCESS—SUMP and the older letters beneath, the curving floors and low ceilings and the paint flaking off the old grey walls in strata. He went quickly, with his head down and his hands in his sleeves and the hood of his habit up, which did not help. People still looked. He went to Mother Rook's, at the end of the short dead-end corridor, and knocked, and the old woman's gravel voice said "Come," and he went in, ducking under the pipes, and she was sitting in her chair in her shawls, exactly as she had been, as though she had not moved in thirteen days.

"She's not here," Rook said, before he could ask.

"Where is she?"

The black eyes looked at him from their folds for a long time.

"Fourteen," said Mother Rook. "Go out, left, down the curve, past the button stall, second stair down, first door on the right. The one with the writing over it." She paused. "Knock."

* * *

The door had writing over it.

Old letters, stenciled in the yellow gone brown, on the grey under the flaking paint: *C-14*. And under them, smaller, scratched into the wall by hand, so small that he had to lift his lamp to read them, two more words in the same old square letters. *ABANDON HOPE.*

He stood looking at them for a moment. Then he knocked.

The door opened a hand's width. A face looked out at him, a young man's, hard and narrow, with a shaved head and a scar through one eyebrow. It looked at the habit, and at Idris's face, and its expression did not change at all.

"She's here," Idris said. "Lark Vey. My sister."

The face looked at him a moment longer. Then it turned and said something over its shoulder into the room, low, and listened, and turned back, and the door opened.

It was a small room. Four long steps by six. It had once been something—you could see the brackets on the long walls, three high, where shelves had been, or beds—and now it was bare, and full of people. Fifteen, perhaps, crammed in, sitting on the floor and on crates and on each other, young, most of them, Lark's age or a little older, with lamps on the floor among them throwing their shadows huge up the walls; and they had all stopped talking when the door opened, and they were all looking at him. There was a round port at the far end, so scratched on the outside that it was milk-white. And on the wall by the port, at the height of a sitting man's head, there were marks.

Lark was sitting under them, on the floor, with her back against the wall and her knees drawn up and the red cord in her hand.

"Let him in, Teodor," she said. "He's my brother. He's harmless."

"He's a Keeper," said the young man with the scar.

"He's harmless," said Lark. "Aren't you, Idris? Completely harmless." She looked up at him across the room. Her eyes were red and dry. "Come in. Shut the door. Since you're here. Since you can't keep away."

He came in. The door shut behind him. Fifteen faces watched him pick his way between them to the far wall, and he did not know any of them, and they all knew him. He could see it. They knew who he was and what he was and, he thought, what he had done.

He stood in front of Lark.

"Benno Kask," she said.

He did not understand at first.

"That's the name." She did not get up. "Benno Kask. Ward Nine of the Sump. Sixteen. He sells krill cakes at the foot of the Nine stair. Fried, with salt. They're good. His mother sits with him and does the money because she can't stand at a stall any more, because she's only got one leg, because she lost the other one in a vat accident on the Fourth Spoke when he was four. His father's dead. His sister's dead. There's just him." She looked at the cord in her hand. "His name was on the reserve. Did you know there was a reserve? I didn't. Teodor did. Teodor knows everything." She looked up. "You know how you get on the reserve? You get on it if you lost your ward-number. If your family was Levy-dodgers, once, two generations back. If you're Sump. If you owe the Office money. If somebody up in the Spokes didn't like your face. Nobody knows they're on it. You just are. And then when somebody's brother goes to the Office and pays, they reach into the reserve and pull out a name, and a Guard goes down the Nine stair and ties a cord on a boy selling krill cakes." She held up the cut cord. "This was on my wrist two days ago. It's on his now."

Idris could not speak.

"What did it cost?" said Lark. "What did you pay? You haven't got any money."

"It doesn't matter."

"It matters to me."

"A number," Idris said. "In a book."

Lark looked at him. She did not understand, he could see. He did not want her to. Then something moved in her face, and he saw that she did, or part of it, enough.

"I'm going to put it back," she said. "I'm going to the Office in the morning, and I'm going to tell them there was another error, and put my name back, and take his out."

"They won't," Idris said. "It's sealed. A reconciliation's sealed. They can't undo it, even if they wanted to, which they don't. If you go in there and say there was a false reconciliation they'll arrest Oake and they'll arrest me and they'll put your name back in the Drum for the rest of your life, and his name will stay where it is. That's all that will happen. You can't put it back, Lark. Nobody can."

She looked at him for a long time.

Then she bent her head and put her face in her knees, and her shoulders began to shake; and he understood after a moment that she was crying, without any sound at all, the way she had cried at six, in the black smock, at the pyre, when she would not hold his hand.

He knelt down in front of her. He did not touch her. He did not think she would let him.

The room was silent around them. Fifteen people, and none of them moved. On the wall above Lark's bent head, in the light of the lamps on the floor, the marks went up in their rows, hundreds of them, thin lines scratched into the old grey with a point, very neat, in blocks; and some had a line through them, and most had, and down at the bottom, smaller, finer, a second set, in rows of ten.

"What are they?" he said. He did not know why he asked. Because he could not ask anything else.

It was the young man with the scar, Teodor, who answered, from behind him.

"Nobody knows," he said. "They've always been here. Sump kids touch them for luck. Before something hard. Before Levy Day." His voice was flat and cold. "The old ones say somebody was counting the days they owed. Somebody who lived in this room. Counting down. The ones with lines through are the days they paid." He paused. "You can see they didn't finish."

Idris looked at the bottom rows. Some of the small marks had lines through them. Some did not.

Lark lifted her head. Her face was wet. She looked at him, kneeling in front of her in his grey habit with the hook on his breast and his father's line round his neck, and then she reached up behind her, without looking, and put her fingertips on the marks on the wall, the way you would put your hand on someone's shoulder.

"Go home, Idris," she said.

* * *

He did not go home.

He went to the foot of the Nine stair in the Sump, in the early morning, when the stalls were opening and the lamps were being lit along the curving corridor and the smell of hot oil and frying was coming up off the hot plates. He did not know why he went. He had not decided to. He found his feet had taken him there, and he stood at the foot of the stair with his hood up and his hands in his sleeves, and looked.

There were four stalls at the foot of the Nine stair. One sold wire. One sold the grey stuff in bowls. One sold nothing he could identify. The fourth was a sheet of old hull plate on two crates, with a hot plate on it and a pan of oil on the hot plate, spitting; and behind it, on a stool, a woman with one leg and a crutch leaning against the wall beside her, counting coins into a tin; and in front of it, turning cakes in the oil with a long fork, a boy.

He was small for sixteen. Thin, quick, with a mop of black hair and big ears and a grin that took up most of his face, which he was turning on an old man in front of the stall, haggling over a cake. He had a red cord on his left wrist. Idris could see it from the stair, bright against his skin. He did not seem to be ashamed of it. He had pushed his sleeve up past the elbow so that it showed.

Idris went to the stall.

"Krill cake, Keeper?" said the boy. He had a quick light voice. "Two bits. Fresh. Salt's extra. Salt's worth it."

"With salt," said Idris.

The boy forked one out of the oil and shook it and dropped it on a square of old paper and sprinkled salt on it out of a twist of cloth and handed it over, all in one movement, grinning. Idris gave him three bits. The boy looked at the third bit, and then at Idris, and then shrugged and pocketed it.

"Don't see many Keepers down the Nine," he said.

"No."

"You lost?"

"A little."

"Where you headed? I'll tell you the way. No charge." The grin. "Well. One bit."

Idris did not answer. He stood with the hot cake in his hand on its square of paper, grease soaking through to his fingers, and looked at the red cord on the boy's wrist.

The boy saw him looking. He looked down at it himself, and the grin changed, a little, went sideways.

"Got called," he said. "Two days ago. Guard came right down the stair. Funny, that—they said there was a mix-up in the Drum, somebody else's name got read out by mistake, and it was mine all along." He shrugged. "So. Firstlight, I go."

"I'm sorry," said Idris.

"What for? Wasn't you that read it." The boy turned a cake. "Ma's took it hard. Haven't you, Ma?" The woman on the stool did not look up from her coins. "But it's not so bad. I've been thinking. They say if you go in the Levy and you're good with machines, they put you on the ships. Inward. On the big ships, the House ships." His eyes went bright. "I'm good with machines. I fixed this plate. I fixed half the stalls on the Nine. I could fly. I always wanted to fly. Nobody flies from the Sump. You've got to be Spoke-born to get on a ship, or have money, and I never had either. But in the Levy they don't care where you're from, they say. They just want hands."

"Who says?"

"Everybody." The boy's grin came back. "So maybe it's lucky. Maybe I'll fly a House ship back through the Eye one day and you'll be up there in your tower, Keeper, writing me down in your book." He laughed. "Benno Kask, pilot. You write that. Big letters."

Idris stood with the cake in his hand.

He could have told him. He thought about it. He stood at the foot of the Nine stair with the grease soaking through the paper into his fingers and the boy grinning at him over the pan, and he thought about saying: *It was my sister. It was my sister's name that was read out, and it was the right name, and I paid to have it changed, and they reached into a list and pulled out yours.* He thought about what the boy's face would do.

"I'll write it," he said. "Big letters."

"Good man." Benno turned another cake. "Eat that while it's hot, Keeper. It's no good cold."

Idris ate it. It was very good. He ate the whole thing standing there at the foot of the stair, and then he thanked the boy and nodded to the woman on the stool, who did not look up, and went away up the curving corridor toward the service stair, and was sick, quietly, against the old grey wall at the first bend, where nobody could see.

* * *

He did not sleep that night either.

At the twelfth bell he gave up trying and got up and dressed and went out. He did not go down. He went up, through the Third Spoke's empty galleries, past the dark chapels, to the Keepers' high walk: a narrow path along the top of the Spoke, under the outer hull, that the Keepers used to inspect the phase-taps where the city drew its power off the Ring. It was cold up there. It was always cold. You walked on a grating of iron with the dark metal of the hull a meter over your head, and every forty paces there was a window in the hull, a small round port of thick glass, and through it you could see out.

He walked it end to end, past the phase-taps humming in their housings, past window after window. Out, below and beyond, the city: the other Spokes, the towers, the lights. The Eye, breathing. He did not look at the Eye. He looked at the city. It was very beautiful at night. He had always thought so.

At the end of the walk, where the Third Spoke met the Hub, the last window looked down over the upper wards; and below the upper wards, at the edge of the Hub, on the Ring-side, the Factor's private berth.

It was a dock. A big one, cut deep into the Hub's flank, with its own lock and its own lights and its own wall round it, where House ships tied up when they came through, couriers and the Factor's own yacht; and it was not usually lit at this hour. It was lit now. Floodlights, white, very bright, shining down into the berth from its walls.

There was a ship in the berth. A long dull grey freighter with a blunt nose and a registry number on her flank and her name in white.

He stood at the window and watched.

The *Clemency*'s cargo doors were open. All of them, all along her flank, great square hatches swung back on their hinges like the lids of boxes. And out of them, down ramps onto the berth's floor, in the white light, figures were walking.

They walked in lines. That was the first thing he saw. Not in a crowd, as passengers walk off a hauler, or dockers off a shift. In lines, four abreast, evenly spaced, at the same pace, as though a single foot were coming down for all of them. They were in armor. Not the white-and-gold half-armor of the Wardens' Guard, which was for show; full armor, dark, faceless, helmeted, with long weapons slung across their backs. They came down the ramps out of the *Clemency*'s holds in lines of four and crossed the berth's floor and went into the Hub through a great door at the berth's far end that he had never seen open, and the door swallowed them, and more came.

He watched for a long time. He tried to count them. He lost count at six hundred. The lines did not stop.

Down at the berth's edge, under the floodlights, a small group stood watching too. He could just make them out. Three or four figures, not in armor. One of them in black, tall and narrow. One in white and gold, round and small, with two smaller figures behind her holding up the hem of something.

Idris put his hand on the cold glass of the window.

*Ore barge, returning,* he had written, *empty.*

The lines came on. Four abreast, out of the light, down into the city.

# Chapter 9: Water

*The Dark · Year 419*

The second lung died on the eleventh day.

I heard it go. Everybody in Hold Fourteen heard it go, because you hear the lungs all your life without hearing them, the way you hear your own heart, and when one stops the silence comes up through the floor like cold water. I was in the Tallies' room with Ness, on the floor under the First Count, taking her through the Door for the hundredth time. She had it now. Not well. She sang like a cracked pipe and she still put the dog before the pin when she was tired. But she had it, all of it, the numbers and the Door, and I had made her sing it to me forward and backward and out of order, and she had sworn at me in every way she knew, and I had felt, for the first time since Quill went, that I could breathe. And then the floor went quiet under us.

Ness stopped in the middle of a line.

"That's Number Two," she said.

There had been four lungs in Hold Fourteen when I was born. One had died when I was nine. Number Two was the big one, the oldest, down in the deepest level of the Hold under the old reactor shielding, the one Veska had worked as a girl. It was the one that kept the children's levels clear. We heard it start to cough and grind, two floors down, a sound like a giant gargling grit, and then a long rising whine, and then a bang that went up through the walls, and then nothing.

Then the smell.

You cannot describe the smell of the black to anybody who has not smelled it. It is not a bad smell, exactly. It is sweet, a little. Like bread gone over, like the inside of an old boot. It came up the stairwells and along the corridors and in under the door of the Tallies' room, faint at first and then not faint, and Ness and I looked at each other, and then we were both up and running, out and down, toward the children's levels, with our sleeves over our mouths.

Veska was already there. She had come up out of the lung level with her face black to the eyes and her iron bar in her hand and blood on her knuckles where she had beaten on something, and she was standing in the corridor outside the children's dormitory shouting at people to get the children out, up, into the dome, where the third lung still pushed air; and people were running past her with children in their arms, wrapped in blankets, coughing. I took one. A little girl, four, Hesta's youngest, so light. She coughed on my shoulder all the way up eleven flights. When we got to the dome there were already thirty children in it, lying on the old grey garden beds under the glass with Kettle turning overhead, and their mothers kneeling beside them, and the sound of all of them coughing together under the dome was like rain.

Two of them died that night. A baby, and a boy of three. I sang them both out in the morning, in the cold corridor, with the Hold behind me singing under my voice; and the Hold was singing again by then. But it was not singing for them. It was singing at me.

* * *

Veska did not call a meeting. She did not need to. She came to the Tallies' room on the evening of the twelfth day, alone, and knocked, and came in when I said to, and sat down on Quill's bunk, which was empty, under the marks, as though it were hers to sit on.

"He's called," she said.

"Who has?"

"Kine." She held up something in her hand: a little black box, the size of a fist, with a red light on its face. "He left this with me. On the dock. Before he went. It talks to his ship. I didn't tell you." She turned it over. "He called it this morning. He knew about Number Two."

"How could he know?"

"I don't know. Maybe he's got somebody here. Maybe he just knows how old our lungs are, and how long they've got. He's been to every Hold in Tithe. He knows how old everybody's lungs are." She set the box down on the bunk beside her. "He said he's sorry for our loss. He said it like he meant it. He said he's changed the terms."

I waited.

"He doesn't want the song any more," said Veska. "Not just the song. He's learned the hard way that old Tallies lie, he said. He said no offense to the dead. He wants you." She looked at me. "On Brine. Till he's ready to go to the Halo. And then at the Halo, to sing it there, wherever it is he thinks it needs singing. And he says if you'll come, his ship will be here in two days, and it'll bring four tanks and the two lungs, and you'll walk up the ramp and the lungs'll be rolled down it, the same hour."

"And if I don't come."

"Then he doesn't come. Nor the lungs. Nor the water-boats." She did not look away. "We've got one lung, Tally. One. Number Three. And it's not well. If Number Three goes the whole Hold breathes the black. All of us. In a month there's nobody left to sing out."

I sat on the floor with my back against the wall.

"What do you want me to say?" I said.

"I don't want you to say anything," said Veska. "I'm not here to ask you. I'm the boss of this Hold, and this Hold is going to die if he doesn't send those lungs, and I'd put you on that ship in a sack if I had to. I'm telling you that so you know where I stand." She stood up. She was a short broad woman and the room was small and she filled it. "But I'd rather you walked."

She went to the door. At the door she stopped and turned round.

"Quill lied to him," she said. "On the dock. Didn't he? I'm not a Tally, I don't know the Count, I only know it at funerals the way everybody does. But I've heard it at enough funerals. That wasn't how it goes."

"No," I said.

"Why'd he do that?"

"Because of the rule. Never sing the Count on the Halo."

Veska looked at me for a long moment.

"There's a lot of rules in the Dark," she said, "that were made by people who didn't have to breathe the black." And she went out.

* * *

I told Ness at the top of the Hold, under the dome, at the evening bell, among the coughing children. She did not take it well.

She did not shout. Ness shouts at everything that doesn't matter and goes quiet at everything that does. She sat beside me on the edge of an old garden bed with her elbows on her knees and her hands hanging between them, and listened, and did not say anything until I had finished; and then she said, very quietly: "No."

"It's not up to you."

"It's not up to Veska either. It's not up to the Hold. It's yours. It's your Count. You don't have to go."

"Look round you."

She did not look round. She did not have to. The children were all around us on the grey soil, wrapped in blankets, and the mothers kneeling, and the sound of the coughing under the glass.

"He'll take you to Brine," said Ness. "And he'll keep you there. And then he'll take you to the Halo, to the Spindle, and you'll sing it, whatever it does, and then what? What happens to you after? What does a man like that do with the only person who knows a thing he wants, once he's got it?"

"I'm not the only person who knows it," I said.

She turned her head and looked at me.

"I'm not," I said. "Not now. That's what the old man made me promise. Never let one Tally be the only one who knows it." I took her hand where it hung between her knees. It was cold and hard and scarred across the knuckles. "You've got it."

"Badly."

"You've got it. You can sing it forward and backward. You can sing it out of order. You put the dog before the pin when you're tired, and I'll tell you this now, so you remember: don't. The pin's first going down. And you've got the turns right every time, and that's the hard part." I held on to her hand. "So if I don't come back, it's not gone. It's not in only one head. That's what matters. That's what the First Tally said matters."

Ness looked at me for a long time. Her face in the Kettle-light was purple and yellow and the scar was black along her jaw.

"I don't want to be a Tally," she said.

"I know."

"I don't want to have to sing out kids."

"I know."

"I want you to come back."

"I know," I said, and I put my face against her shoulder, and she put her arm round me, and we sat there among the coughing children under Kettle until the night bell.

* * *

The *Patience* came on the morning of the fourteenth day, and they rolled the tanks down the ramp, four, smoking, and set them in a row on the dock floor; and then they rolled down one lung, a great dull grey machine on a sled with its intake mouths sealed in cloth, and set it beside the tanks.

"One," said Veska. She stood at the foot of the ramp with her iron bar. "He said two."

"The other's on the next boat." It was a woman who said it, at the top of the ramp: tall and lean, with grey-blond hair cut short and a long white scar from the corner of her eye down into her collar, in the same armor as the men. She had a slate in her hand and the air of someone who did all the talking that mattered on the *Patience* so that the Reeve did not have to. "It's on Brine, in the old Ninth district. It's under the ice, half dug out. We'll have it out in a month. It'll come on the first boat after."

"He said two in the same hour."

"He said two," the woman agreed. "And here's one, Boss, and you can see it's whole, and you can see we brought it. The other one comes after. You can take that or not." She looked past Veska, at me, standing at the edge of the dock floor with my bag over my shoulder. "Is that her?"

"That's her," said Veska, without turning round.

"Then she can come up."

I did not look back at the gallery. I had said goodbye to the Hold already, in the corridor that morning, after I had sung out the boy of three; and I had said goodbye to Ness, in the Tallies' room, with the door shut, and she was not on the gallery, because I had asked her not to be. I did not look back at the lung on its sled, either, or at the tanks, or at Veska. I walked across the dock floor to the foot of the ramp and up it, and at the top the scarred woman looked at me with flat grey eyes and said, "I'm Marrow. Come on," and I went past her into the ship.

* * *

I had thought a ship would be like a Hold. Corridors and rooms. It was not. It was smaller, and much more crowded, and it smelled of oil and old sweat and something sharp and metal that I learned later was the engines, and everything in it was close: the ceilings low, the corridors so narrow two people could not pass without turning sideways, the walls covered in pipes and cables and boxes and handholds, and lights, small hard lights, everywhere, so that there was no dark anywhere at all. It was very loud. The engines were a growl under everything that never stopped. And it moved. That was the thing nobody had told me, and that I had never imagined: that when the *Patience* left the dock, the floor would tilt, and press up under my feet, and then go light, and that I would have to hold on to a handhold on the wall while my stomach went somewhere it had never been.

Marrow took me forward. Up a short ladder and along a corridor and through a hatch into a room at the very front of the ship, where there were chairs, and screens, and a long window of thick glass across the front, through which—I did not look through it at first. I did not want to.

There was a brass plate on the wall above the front chair.

It was old. Older than anything on the ship, I thought; older than the Dark. It was bolted to the bulkhead with four bolts whose heads had been polished flat, and it was dull and green at its edges and bright in the middle, the way old brass goes when hands touch it. There were letters on it, raised, in the old square writing Quill had been teaching me. I read them because I could, and because it was the first thing on the *Patience* that I had understood.

*PATIENCE*, it said. And under that, smaller, a number, and *OSTRAVA*, and some words I did not know—*TUG CLASS III*—and at the very bottom, so worn I had to put my face close: *COMBINE*.

"Old, isn't it," said Marrow.

"What's the Combine?"

Marrow shrugged. "Who knows. Something from before. Kine says she's the oldest hull in the Reach. Older than the Dark, older than the Light, maybe. He says she's been rebuilt so many times there's nothing original left but that plate and the bolts that hold it." She looked at the plate without much interest. "He won't let anyone take it down. Sit there."

I sat where she pointed, in a chair along the side wall, and she strapped me into it with a belt across my chest, and went out.

And then I looked out of the window, because I could not help it.

We were outside.

I had never been outside. I was a Tally's girl; I had lived my whole life in Hold Fourteen, and the only outside I had ever seen was through the dome's old glass, and the milk-white port in the Tallies' room, and the port in the inner lock door, at the dead. Outside was where the dead went. It was the Dark. And here I was in it, with a window in front of me.

Tithe was behind us. I could see it in a screen on the wall, if I turned my head: a great dark tangle of a thing, broken towers and dead spokes and districts black with frost, with only here and there a light, very small, where a Hold still burned. I had not known it was so big, or so dead. I had not known it was so beautiful, either, in the way a broken thing can be. I looked at it for a long time, trying to find Fourteen, and could not.

And ahead of us, filling the window, the Halo.

I had seen it all my life through the dome glass, a dark band crossing Kettle. That is not seeing it. From the *Patience*, from outside, I saw it whole. It was a ring. A great dark ring, going round, so big that its far side was a thin line, a thread, small as a hair across Kettle's face; and on its near side, close, it was a wall of black metal going up and up out of the window's top and down and down out of its bottom, laced all over with lines of gold. Dull gold. Dead gold. The gold of something that has been cold for a very long time. And standing up off its rim, a long way round, small and sharp against Kettle's bands, a needle. A tower.

"That's the Spindle," said a voice behind me.

It was not Marrow. It was a man's voice, thin and soft and a little hoarse, and when I turned my head as far as the belt would let me I saw him in the hatch: the gaunt man in grey with the iron cuffs on his wrists and the chain between them, and the flame-in-a-ring on his forehead, and the deep bright eyes.

"I've been told to look after you," he said. "I'm sorry. I'm sure you'd rather not be looked after by me. But there's no one else on this ship who's not a soldier, and the Reeve thought you might want someone to talk to who wasn't carrying a gun." He came in, slowly, with his chain held in his hands so it would not clink. "I'm Cinder. Brother Cinder. Of the Wick, on Brine."

"You're a Lamplighter."

"I'm a Lamplighter." He sat down in the chair beside mine, carefully, as though he were not sure it would hold him. He was so thin that I could see the bones of his wrists move under the cuffs. "And you're the Tally of Fourteen. And neither of us chose to be on this ship, and here we both are." He looked out of the window at the Halo, and his face changed. It went soft. It went, I thought, the way the faces of the mothers went in the dome, looking at their children. "Isn't it beautiful?"

"It's dead."

"It's sleeping," said Brother Cinder. "That's what the Wick teaches. The Light didn't die. The Light was taken, and it went away, and those who loved it went after it, to bring it home. And one day it will come home. And when it does, the Halo will wake, and the Eye will open, and they'll come back through it. All of them." He did not take his eyes off the Halo. "The Founders. And everyone who went with them. Everyone we've lost."

"Everyone we've lost," I said. "You mean the dead."

"I mean everyone."

I thought of Pim on the bench in his jersey with the red stripe. Of Quill, with his fingers on the marks. Of the baby and the boy of three, two mornings ago.

"The dead don't come back," I said. "I send them out. I know where they go."

"Do you?" Cinder turned and looked at me. His eyes were so bright. "*Count it back down*," he said softly. "*We are home once more*. Your own song, Tally. What do you think that means?"

I did not answer.

"I've read the Rule," said Cinder. "The piece the Reeve has. I read it for him. That's why he keeps me—I can read the Keepers' hand, the old script, my order's kept it since the Light. *The Knot is the Founders', and shall not be turned.* The Founders' Knot. In the Spindle." He leaned toward me, and the chain clinked despite him. "The Founders tied it, Tally. Before they went. They tied it to hold the Light asleep until they came home. And your song is how it's untied. I've always known it. The Wick has always known it. And now he's got you, and he's going to take you to the Spindle, and you're going to sing it, and the Light is going to come home." His voice shook. "I've waited my whole life."

"You're in chains," I said.

"Yes."

"Why?"

He looked down at his wrists as though he had forgotten.

"Because I told him," said Brother Cinder, "that when the Light comes home, it won't be his."

* * *

The Reeve sent for me at the evening bell, or what I thought of as the evening bell; there were no bells on the *Patience*, only the engines, and the hard lights that never went down.

His cabin was at the back of the ship, behind the engines, where the growl was loudest. It was small, like everything else, and full of paper. Not slates. Paper. Old charts, pinned to every wall, so many that you could not see the walls under them: charts of Tithe, of Brine, of the Halo, of Kettle and its moons, charts of things I did not recognize at all, drawn in old ink and new, with notes on them in a big rough hand. There was a narrow bunk, and a table, and on the table a lamp, and a book, burned at its edges, open, under a sheet of glass.

He was sitting at the table. He did not stand up when Marrow brought me in. He was coughing, and when he had finished coughing he wiped his mouth with a cloth that he then folded and put in his pocket so that I would not see what was on it; and I saw anyway.

"Sit down, Tally," he said.

I sat on the end of the bunk.

"Marrow says you're angry about the lung."

"You said two."

"I said two. You'll get two." He looked at me with his pale grey eyes. "I don't lie about lungs. I've buried too many people to lie about lungs."

"How many?"

He seemed surprised. Then he thought about it, seriously, as though it were a question that deserved an answer.

"In Brine," he said, "since I've been Reeve, which is nineteen years—four thousand and something. I've got the number written down. Four thousand two hundred and eleven, last I looked. Not all the rot. Some the ice. Some the cold. Some each other." He paused. "One of them was my daughter. She was nine. That was eleven years ago. The rot. It came up out of the lungs in the Fifth district where we lived, and she breathed it, and I didn't have a lung to give her, because there wasn't one in the whole of Brine that was spare." He was quiet for a while. "So I don't lie about lungs, Tally. You'll get two."

I did not say anything.

He turned the book on the table round, under its glass, so that I could see it. The pages were brown and burned at the edges, curling. The writing on them was not like any writing I knew; not the old square letters, not the new, but a fine slanting hand, very close, with little marks above the letters.

"The Keepers' hand," said the Reeve. "Cinder reads it. This is the only piece of the Rule that's left anywhere, as far as I know. I found it in the Ninth district of Brine, in a dead Keeper's house under the ice. It's burned. Most of it's gone. But the first page is here." He put his finger on the glass, over a line. "*The Knot is the Founders', and shall not be turned.* That's what Cinder says it says. I believe him. He's a fanatic, but he's an honest one." He took his finger away. "Your Tallies have a rule too. Never sing the Count on the Halo. The First Tally made it. You know what I think?"

"No."

"I think whoever made your rule and whoever made that one knew each other," said the Reeve. "Or knew the same thing. I think the Knot holds the Light down. And I think your song lets it up." He looked at me. "Am I wrong?"

"I don't know."

"No. You don't. Nobody does." He sat back. "That's the thing about the Dark. Nobody knows anything any more. Everything that knew things is dead. All we've got is scraps. A page of a book. A brass plate with a word on it nobody understands. A funeral song." He coughed, briefly. "I'm sick, Tally. I've got a year, maybe. Maybe two. Brine's got ten. I don't have time to wait for somebody to come along who knows. So I'm going to try it with the scraps."

"And if it doesn't work?"

"Then I'll have tried."

"And if it does?" I said. "What then? What happens when the Light comes back? Brother Cinder says it won't be yours."

The Reeve looked at me for a long time.

"Brother Cinder," he said, "thinks dead people are going to come out of the Eye. He thinks his Founders are going to come back with lamps in their hands and make everything well." He shook his head slowly. "I don't think anybody's coming back, Tally. I think the Light's a machine, like the lungs, like the heat-pumps under the ice. I think it ran everything once and it can run everything again. And I think when it does, somebody's got to be in charge of it. Somebody's got to hold the door." He spread his big scarred hands on the table. "Would you rather it was me? Or would you rather it was nobody, and every Hold in Tithe and every district in Brine fighting over it with knives? Because that's the choice. That's the only choice there is."

I did not have an answer to that. I sat on the end of his bunk with my hands in my lap.

"The second lung," I said at last. "Before I sing. Not after. Before. I want to see it go down the ramp at Fourteen with my own eyes."

The Reeve of Brine looked at me. Then he smiled, his tired slow smile, with the broken tooth at the side.

"You have my word," he said.

Behind him, through a small round port in the back wall of the cabin, I could see the Halo going by, dark and vast, its gold lines dull, and far off on its rim, the needle of the Spindle, standing up black against Kettle's turning face. I looked at it for a long time. And it seemed to me, though I knew it could not be true, through the hull and the glass and the growl of the engines, that the floor under my feet was very faintly, very far off, beating, in runs, and stops, and runs again; like something counting.

# Chapter 10: First Light Is a Wave

*The Combine · FY 2291*

Haddad's quarters were on the fourth deck of the admin stack, at the end of a corridor of identical doors, and inside they were not identical to anything. Sol stood just inside the door while the doctor locked it behind her and looked round, because she could not help it, at the first room she had been in for twelve years that somebody had made for themselves.

There were books. Paper books, dozens of them, on shelves screwed to every wall and in stacks on the floor and on the narrow bunk, with strips of paper sticking out of them to mark places. There was a samovar, an old one, brass, on a stand by the bunk, with a little glass cup upside down on its top. There were pictures on the wall above the desk: a city on a hillside, white houses going up in terraces under a pale sky, and a woman in a scarf standing at a gate, and two small boys on a wall, squinting into the sun. There was a rug on the floor, red and blue, worn through in the middle. And there was a smell, under the smoke—something sweet and dry, like dust and flowers.

"Halvard," said Haddad, seeing her look at the city. "Kesra. Where I was born. That's my mother. Those are my brothers. They're old men now. One of them's dead." She went past Sol to the samovar and turned something on it, and it began to tick. "Sit down. Anywhere. Move the books."

Sol moved a stack off a chair and sat. Haddad did not. She stood by the samovar with her arms folded, looking at it, as though it might tell her where to begin.

"Is it routine?" Sol said again.

"No," said Haddad.

The samovar ticked.

"I'll tell you all of it," the doctor said. "Once. I won't tell it twice, I don't think I can. Then you can do what you want. Go to Lusk, if you like. Go to the Director. Go to the ballast hold, where I imagine you've been." She glanced at Sol. "Yes. We know about the ballast hold. Lusk has known for years. He lets you have it because it's cheaper than finding out what you'd do without it."

Sol said nothing.

"First light," said Haddad, "is a wave."

She took the little glass cup down from the samovar's top and turned it over in her fingers.

"When the Ring couples to Kettle's field, the throat forms. It takes about forty seconds. In those forty seconds, space inside the Ring—stops being one thing and starts being two. Here and Ostrava. It tears, if you like. A very precise tear, along a line we've drawn very carefully with ten thousand kilometers of phase-line. But it tears. And as it tears it throws off a front. A shockwave, in the geometry, moving outward at the speed of light from the throat's edge, across the plane of the Ring and up and down its axis." She set the cup on the samovar's spout. "It carries radiation. Hard. Harder than anything Kettle throws. And a gravitic shear, which is—it doesn't matter what it is. It goes through things. It goes through people. Within fifty kilometers of the throat, anything not behind four meters of steel, or the equivalent, takes a dose that kills it in between an hour and three days, depending on the dose and the person. Beyond fifty kilometers it falls off fast. Beyond two hundred it's nothing."

"The platform's six kilometers off the rim."

"Six point two."

"The hab rings."

"Have a centimeter and a half of hull plate and the water in the air plant." Haddad turned the tap on the samovar, and dark tea ran into the glass cup. "The skirt was the shielding. The skirt would have done it, just. Steel and water and a phase mesh tuned to bleed the front off sideways. We designed it four years ago. Gang Nine had sixty days of work left on it." She turned the tap off. "It isn't going to be finished."

"Castellane said the rings were rated. Your revised models."

"There are no revised models." Haddad held out the cup of tea to her. "Drink that. You're very white."

Sol took it. It was hot. She did not drink it.

"There's no instability, either," said Haddad. "In the governor. I made it up. Well. I didn't make it up, I was told to make it up, and then I made it up, which is worse. There's no such thing as a bow drawn and held. That was Imre's phrase. He likes a phrase. The Ring could sit unlit for a hundred years and Kettle wouldn't hurt it. But the board needed a reason to light it early, a reason that would go in the record and look like prudence, and the only person on this platform who could certify a phase instability that would satisfy the underwriters at Ostrava Mutual was me." She looked at the wall, at the white city on its hill. "So I certified it."

"Why early?"

"You know why. You've seen the sheet."

"I want you to say it."

Haddad looked at her.

"Because the construction loss policy expires on Day Two Hundred and Ninety," she said. "And after that the bonded labor on this platform isn't insured, and the Combine owes four hundred and twelve completion bonuses and four hundred and twelve passages home, and it can't pay them. It's overextended, Chief. It spent twice what it meant to on this Ring, and Halvard-Lyons is building one of its own at Corvo, two years behind us and catching up, and if Corvo lights first the Combine's paper isn't worth the ink. The board needs the Ring lit and it needs money. And there is a way to get both on the same day." She sat down, finally, on the edge of the bunk, among the books. "That's all. That's the whole of it. It's arithmetic."

"And the *Mercy*?"

"Reassigned. Day Two Thirty. To the Halvard yards, by board order, on Mr. Lind's authority. She was never coming. The relay signal didn't fail. There wasn't one to send."

"Standing the platform off. The tow."

"Never planned. You were right about the coupling." Haddad almost smiled. "Imre told me you'd been to see him. He was very shaken. He said, *she ran the bead on the north face herself.* As though that settled something."

"Does he know?"

"Imre knows." Haddad looked down at her hands. "Imre chose not to know the details, which is what Imre does. He is a good man in most of the ways that don't matter. He knows there's a policy and he knows there's a date and he knows the skirt was stopped, and he knows what those three things add up to, and he has decided that it is above his station to add them." She was quiet. "He'll be on the *Assessor* when she goes. He'll stand at the window and watch. And he'll be sick, afterward, I imagine. And then he'll go home and see his daughter."

"When does she go?"

"The *Assessor*? Day Two Eighty-Five. First shift. All Company staff, all Proctors, Mr. Lind, the Director. Three days out to Gall's shadow, which is two hundred and twenty thousand kilometers off, where the front is nothing. The story will be that the instability worsened unexpectedly on Day Two Eighty-Seven, and the governor coupled early, and the throat formed before the platform could be evacuated, and the staff were saved only because they had gone ahead to prepare the tow." She lifted her hands and let them drop. "There'll be an inquiry. I'll give evidence. The underwriters will pay."

"And the Lighting? If you're all gone. Who lights it?"

"The Ring lights itself." Haddad looked up. "That's the beauty of the design. My design. There's a clock in the Spindle. In the governor housing. Hardened, sealed, three redundant power feeds off the phase-lines. When the dogs come out of the governor, I arm it, and it counts down, and at the end of the count it couples the governor to the field, and the throat forms. Nobody has to be there. Nobody *can* be there; you couldn't be within fifty kilometers. The *Assessor* can stop it by signal, if something goes wrong. Nothing else can."

"When do the dogs come out?"

"Day Two Eighty-Three. Gang Four and Gang Nine. Four hours." Haddad met her eyes. "You remember I asked."

Sol sat with the cup of tea in her hands and did not drink it. She found that she was very cold, all through, the way she had been cold at the end of the chain with Kostya sixty meters past her fingers. The samovar ticked. On the wall the white city went up its hill in the pale sun.

"Why are you telling me?"

"Because you came up the ladder." Haddad's voice did not change. "That's all. Because I have been doing the arithmetic for thirty-one days, Chief Dace, four hundred and twelve people against a door that will carry ten million in the first century and God knows how many after, and I am very good at arithmetic, it's the only thing I've ever been good at, and every time I do it it comes out the same. Lind showed it to me on a single page. Four hundred and twelve. Against all of that. He said, *you're an engineer, doctor, you understand loads.* And I did. I do." She was looking at her hands again. "And then I go to bed and I can't stop doing it. I lie there and do the sum. Over and over. All night. And it always comes out the same, and it doesn't help." She looked up. "And then you came up the ladder with the sheet. And I thought, well. There's someone else who can do the sum now. At least I won't be doing it alone."

"That's it?"

"That's it. I'm not a brave woman, Chief. I'm not going to stand up in the galley and tell the platform. I'm not going to march on the command deck. I'm a coward with a samovar." She lifted her hand as though to drink from a cup of her own, and found she had not poured one, and looked at the empty hand, and put it down. "But you can. If you want. Now you know."

Sol put the tea down on the floor beside the chair, carefully, so it would not spill.

"If the *Assessor* can stop the clock by signal," she said, "then they can stop it if they're not on the *Assessor*."

Haddad looked at her.

"If they're still here," said Sol. "On the platform. On Day Two Eighty-Eight. Lind and the Director and Lusk and all of you. If they're still here when the clock runs out, then they're under the front with us." She stood up. "And then I think somebody will find a way to stop it."

* * *

There were two hundred people in the ballast hold on the night of Day Two Hundred and Sixty-Three. Sol had not known there were so many on the platform who would come. They came down the ladder all night, in twos and threes, through the cold, past the Proctors' dead cameras, and sat on the ore crates and stood along the walls and crowded into the aisles between the stacks until there was no more room, and the air went warm and close with their breath, and the two work lamps lit their faces from below.

She told them.

She had not meant to be the one. She had gone to Teague with it, and Teague had listened, sitting on his bunk with his hands on his knees, and then he had said, *you tell them.* She had said she couldn't. He had said, *they won't believe it from me. I've been telling them the Company's going to kill them for forty years, and the Company hasn't, mostly, and so they think I'm an old man with a cough. They'll believe it from you, because you never told them anything.*

So she stood on a crate in the middle of the ballast hold with two hundred faces turned up at her, and she unfolded the sheet with the blue letterhead, and she told them. All of it. The policy. The date. The skirt. The *Mercy*. The *Assessor*, on Day Two Eighty-Five. The clock in the Spindle. First light, which was a wave. She did not say where she had got it from. She said *I've had it confirmed by someone who'd know*, and people looked at each other, and nobody asked.

When she stopped there was no sound at all.

Then somebody began to cry. She did not see who. And somebody else said, quite loudly, "That's not true," and somebody else said, "Shut up, Danek, it's on the paper," and then everybody was talking at once.

It went on a long time. It went the way such things go: people shouting, and people crying, and people saying it could not be true and people saying of course it was true, what did you think, and somebody wanting to go to the Director, and somebody wanting to go to the galley and tell everybody, now, tonight, and somebody else saying they'd be shot in the spine corridor before they'd got halfway; and old Mother Szell, in her voice like a rusty hinge, saying over and over, *I told you, I told you, I said in '64 they'd do it one day, I told you,* until someone hushed her. Teague did not speak. Sol had thought he would, and he sat on his crate by the ladder with his cloth in his hand and coughed and watched her.

Oyelaran stood up.

"What do we do?" he said. He did not shout it. He said it in his ordinary voice, and the ballast hold went quiet to hear him. "Dace. You brought it. What do we do?"

They all looked at her again.

She had thought about it for two days. She had lain on her bunk with her face to the marks and her fingers on them and thought about nothing else. She had gone out on the Ring for two eleven-hour shifts and called the Count on the collar and thought about it with every bolt.

"They leave on Two Eighty-Five," she said. "First shift. On the *Assessor*. All of them." She looked round. "So they don't leave."

"How do we stop them leaving?"

"The *Assessor* docks at the head of the spine. On the mooring arm. There's one way onto her from the platform: the dock lock at the top of the spine, past the command deck. If we hold the command deck and the dock lock, nobody gets on her. And if nobody gets on her, they're still here on Two Eighty-Eight. Lind. Castellane. Lusk. Haddad. All forty of them. And the clock's still running." She paused. "And then they'll stop it. Because they'll have to. Because if they don't, they're under the front with us."

"And the Proctors?" said Ruzicka. "Twenty Proctors. With guns."

"Twenty Proctors. Four hundred of us." Sol looked at her. "And they don't want to die either. If it comes to it, they'll do the sum the same as anybody."

"You want to take the command deck," said the boy from Gang Eleven, slowly, as though he were tasting it. "You want to—that's mutiny. That's not a strike. That's mutiny."

"I don't want anything," said Sol. "I want a bowl of fish soup on Pier Six. I don't care what it's called. I care that on Day Two Eighty-Eight the people who decided this are standing in the same place as the people they decided it about. That's all. Then we'll see what they decide."

The hold was quiet.

Teague stood up then. It took him a while. He stood by the ladder, holding the rung, and when he spoke his voice was hoarse and thin, and the hold went so still to hear him that Sol could hear the ore ticking in the cold.

"We did it on the Ostrava docks in '64," he said. "Thirty thousand of us. We shut the docks for eleven days. And they brought in the Proctor school, the whole school, cadets and all, and they broke it, and they hanged nineteen men on the cranes at Pier Six for a week where the ships coming in could see them." He coughed. "I was one of the ones they didn't hang. I was nineteen. I've thought about those cranes every day of my life." He looked round at them. "Here's what I learned. You don't kill anybody. Not one. Not a Proctor, not a clerk, not a Director. You take, and you hold, and you don't kill, because the minute you kill somebody they'll stop talking about what they did and start talking about what you did. And you don't break. Once you've started you don't break, not for promises, not for threats, not for anything, because they'll promise you anything to make you break, and then they'll hang you." He sat down again, slowly. "That's all. Dace has the rest."

There was a long silence.

"Day Two Eighty-Four," said Sol. "Third shift. After the clock's armed and before they board. We go at shift change, when the spine's full and nobody's looking twice at anybody. Gang chiefs, come to me tomorrow, one at a time, in the galley. I'll tell you where." She looked round at all of them. "Anybody who doesn't want to be part of it, leave now. Go up the ladder. Nobody will say anything. Nobody will think anything."

Nobody went up the ladder.

At the edge of the lamplight, sitting on a crate against the ore stacks, with her knees drawn up and her arms round them, Juno Szabo was looking at Sol with an expression Sol could not read at all. When their eyes met Juno looked down.

* * *

Pell came back from the infirmary that night with his arm in its blue gel sleeve strapped across his chest, and sat on his bunk in the middle on the left, and looked at the rest of them, and said, "What's happened?"

They told him. Sol told him. She sat on the end of her bunk and told him all of it, while Bracken made stew on the hot plate and Juno lay on her bunk with her face to the wall and Teague coughed. When she had finished, Pell did not say anything for a long time.

"What does it feel like?" he said at last. "The front. First light. If it—if it comes. What's it like?"

"I don't know."

"Does it hurt?"

"I don't know, Okafor."

He looked down at his arm in its blue sleeve.

"My sisters think I'm going to come home," he said. "In twelve years. They've got a—there's a date. On the kitchen wall. My little sister drew it. A calendar. They cross off a day every night after supper." He laughed, a little. "Like you."

"Like me," said Sol.

"I don't want them to get to the end of it," he said, "and nobody comes."

"They won't," said Sol. She did not know whether it was true. She said it anyway. "Eat Bracken's stew and go to sleep."

He ate Bracken's stew. He did not go to sleep; none of them did. After a while the lights went down to the night setting, blue, and the platform made its night sounds, and Sol lay on her bunk with her face to the wall and her fingers on the marks.

"Sol," said Teague, in the dark.

"What."

"Can I tell you something about Dov."

She did not answer. He took it for yes; or he would have told her anyway.

"He reported the clips sixteen times," Teague said. "You know that. You know that because it came out at the inquiry, and Fenn read the sixteen dates into the record, and the Director said the reports had been received and logged and were under review. That's what you know." He coughed. "What you don't know is the seventeenth."

She lay very still.

"He wrote a seventeenth," said Teague. "The day before. Not to the Company. To the yards. To the factory on Halvard Four that made them. He found their address in the stores manifest and he wrote them a letter, on paper, by hand, and he gave it to the courier pilot on the shuttle with a bottle of shine and asked him to post it on Ostrava. He told me. He came down to the ballast hold that night—he'd been coming to the ballast hold for a year, Sol, did you know that? No. He didn't want you to know. He said you'd be angry. He said you were counting your days, and you'd think he was going to cost you some." Teague was quiet. "He came down and told me he'd sent it. He was pleased with himself. He said the Company could ignore a rigger but it couldn't ignore its own supplier telling it the clips weren't rated for side-loading. He said, *they'll have to change them now, Teague, they'll have to.*"

"And?"

"And the next day his clip went, at the Spindle, swinging out," said Teague. "And the courier pilot came back on the next run and found me in the galley and gave me the letter back. Still sealed. He said a Proctor met him on the dock at Ostrava and took his bag and went through it and found the letter and read the address and gave it back to him and told him to return it to sender." Teague coughed. "So he returned it to me. Because the sender was dead."

Sol said nothing.

"I've still got it," said Teague. "In my kit. I never opened it. I didn't think it was mine to open." He was quiet for a long time. "I thought you'd want to know. Now. Before."

She lay in the blue dark with her face to the wall. Her fingers were on the marks. She ran them along the bottom rows, the days, ten and ten and ten, feeling the ridges the scribe had raised; and she counted them, as she counted them every night, the ones without lines through, to the end.

Thirty-eight. Thirty-eight days, still, to Day Three Hundred. To the *Mercy*, which had been reassigned to the Halvard yards on Day Two Thirty and was never coming.

She took the scribe out of the thigh pocket of her liner. In the dark, by feel, she found the place in the rows where Day Two Eighty-Eight would be—twenty-five marks along from the last one she had crossed—and with the point of the scribe, carefully, she drew a line under it. A short hard line, across the bottom of that mark and the ones on either side, cut deep into the polymer, deeper than anything else on the wall, so deep that the scribe's point squealed and she felt it through her teeth.

Then she put the scribe away and lay with her fingers on the line she had cut and did not sleep at all.

# Chapter 11: The Instrument

*The Concord · 336 AL*

For eleven days Idris told no one, and went to the Register hall under the Spindle every evening after his shift, and read.

The Register hall was the Keepers' oldest room after the gallery. It ran the whole width of the Spindle's base, a long vaulted cellar lit by oil lamps on chains, and its walls were shelves, floor to vault, and on the shelves were the volumes of the Transit Register, three hundred and thirty-six years of them, bound in grey leather gone black, each with its year stamped on the spine in silver. The oldest were at the far end, in a locked case with a brass grille, in a hand nobody could read.
