The newest were at the near end, on a long table, open, where the six lanes' current books were brought down at the end of every watch and laid side by side so that the hall's archivist could copy their entries into the master. The archivist was an old Keeper named Brannoch who was nearly deaf and who liked Idris, and who let him sit at the end of the long table with a lamp after the hall had closed, because he thought Idris was doing research for a commentary on the transit tables, and Idris let him think so.

He read the last three months of every lane. He read them slowly, entry by entry, his finger moving down the columns. He did not know what he was looking for until he found it; and then he found it twice more.

Lane Five, forty-one days before Firstlight: *Constancy. Ore barge, returning, empty. 40,000 t.* Initialed *H.D.*, a Keeper of the Fifth Spoke he knew slightly, a careful man with a stammer.

Lane One, thirty-three days before Firstlight: *Benevolence. Ore barge, returning, empty. 40,000 t.* Initialed *A.S.M.*, a Keeper-Adjunct like himself, a woman he had trained with as a novice.

He sat for a long time at the end of the long table with the lamp on its chain swinging slightly above him, though nothing had come through, and looked at those three entries. His own, and two others, in two other hands. Forty thousand tonnes each, ore barges, empty. The same figure, exactly. As though someone had told all three of them what to write.

He thought about Keeper H.D., with his stammer, sitting at the Fifth desk at the dead hour with the lamp swinging over him, watching the dial climb past everything he had ever seen. He wondered what Keeper H.D.'s price had been. Whose name. He thought about Keeper A.S.M., who had once, when they were both fifteen, helped him copy out a phase table he had blotted so that their master would not see. He wondered if she had watched her own hand not shake.

Then he closed the books, and put out the lamp, and went up the long stair to the Archkeeper's study.

* * *

Sallow had sent for him anyway. There was a note on Idris's desk in the gallery that morning, in the Archkeeper's beautiful old Keeper's hand, asking him to call at the end of his watch; and he had read it, and folded it, and put it inside his habit, and gone to the Register hall first, because he had known what he would find and had wanted to be sure.

The old man was at his window with the rug over his knees. He did not turn round when Idris came in. The Eye filled the window, breathing.

"Sit down," Sallow said.

Idris did not sit.

"I've put you on the Firstlight rota," said Sallow. "You, and Marra from the First Spoke, and old Holloway, and Desmond Vane from the Fourth. The four best hands on the governor I've got." He still did not turn. "There's to be a great transit at Firstlight. A convoy of the House, inbound. Very heavy. Many ships at once. The governor will have to be tuned by hand all through it, by four Keepers at once, at the four quarters, as it was in the old days for the colony fleets. I want it done well." He turned his cup on the sill, a quarter turn. "You'll be at the north quarter. By the Inner Housing. Where the grille is."

"How many ships?"

Sallow was quiet.

"How many, Archkeeper? Forty? Fifty? How many abreast?"

The old man turned his head, then, slowly, and looked at him under his heavy lids. Idris saw him see whatever was on his face. Saw him understand it, and decide something.

"Forty-one," said Sallow. "Nine abreast, in the van. Then the rest. Over six hours."

"And what are they carrying?"

"They're House ships, Idris. They're carrying what House ships carry."

"Marines," said Idris. "In armor. In lines of four." He heard his voice shaking and could not stop it. "I've seen them. I saw six hundred of them come off the *Clemency* at the Factor's berth at the twelfth bell, and I stopped counting at six hundred, and they were still coming. And the Prelate was there. Watching. And the Factor." He took the folded note out of his habit and put it down on the sill beside the old man's cup. "And the *Clemency* came through my lane on my watch at three hundred and eighty-eight thousand tonnes, and I wrote her in the Register at forty. Ore barge, returning, empty. In my own hand. With my own seal."

The study was very quiet. Out in the window, the Eye breathed.

Sallow looked at him for a long time. His long sorrowful face did not move at all. Then he closed his eyes.

"Oh, my boy," he said. "Oh, Idris."

"So did Keeper Dunmore on Lane Five. The *Constancy*. And Keeper Sallis-Mead on Lane One. The *Benevolence*. Forty thousand, both of them. Exactly. It's in the books. Brannoch's copied all three into the master and never noticed, because why would he notice, nobody's ever written a false entry in that book in three hundred years." Idris's voice cracked. "Who told them what to write? Who told *me*? A clerk in the Levy Office who eats candied kelp. How did a clerk in the Levy Office know there was a warship coming through my lane on my watch?"

"Because someone told him," said Sallow, with his eyes closed, "who had it from someone in the Factor's House, who had it from the Factor."

"And you knew."

"I knew there would be a great transit at Firstlight. I've known for two months. I didn't know about the *Clemency*." He opened his eyes. "I didn't know they'd been coming through already. I didn't know they'd made my Keepers—" He stopped. He looked, for a moment, very old indeed. "I should have. I should have guessed. Ships abreast. The governor's recovery time. I answered all their questions, Idris, every one, in writing, under seal."

He reached down beside his chair, slowly, and lifted a flat case of black leather from the floor onto his knees, and opened it, and took out a document.

It was a single sheet. Heavy paper, cream, folded in three, with a great seal at its foot in black wax: a ring with a line through it, the House's seal, the same as the small gold disk at Factor Severs's throat. Sallow unfolded it and held it out. Idris took it. His hands were shaking so badly that the paper rattled.

At the top, in the long elegant letters of the Inward:

*AN INSTRUMENT OF CONSOLIDATION*  
*between the Most Noble House of Ostrava, holder in trust of the Founding Debt of the Reach,*  
*and the Concord of the Reach, debtor,*  
*to take effect at Firstlight in the three hundred and thirty-seventh year of the Light.*

He read it. It was not long. It did not need to be.

*Whereas the Concord of the Reach has for three hundred and thirty-six years serviced the Founding Debt by the annual Levy, and whereas the said Levy has proved insufficient to the said Debt, which by the accrual of interest has increased in every year of its service and now stands at a sum greater than the assessed value of the entire Reach—*

*Whereas the Concord, by its Most Luminous Prelate, acknowledges itself unable to discharge the said Debt—*

*Now therefore the House of Ostrava, as holder in trust, calls the Debt, and the Concord, as debtor, consents—*

*That at Firstlight in the three hundred and thirty-seventh year the Concord of the Reach shall be dissolved, and its offices and properties and the Eye and all its works pass into the direct administration of the House, under a Steward of the House's appointment, who shall be the present Prelate—*

*That the annual Levy shall be abolished—*

*That in its stead every person born in the Reach, or resident in the Reach at Firstlight, of whatever age or condition, shall be assessed, and entered upon the rolls of the House as a bond of the House, to serve the said Debt in such place and at such labor as the House shall direct, together with their issue, until the said Debt shall be discharged—*

*That a Squadron of the House shall transit the Eye at Firstlight to secure the said administration and the said assessment, and that the Concord shall render it every assistance—*

*Signed and sealed, for the House: Lucan Aurel Severs, Factor.*  
*Signed and sealed, for the Concord: Ottilie Marsh, Most Luminous.*

He read it twice. Then he stood holding it, with the Eye breathing in the window behind the old man's head, and he found that he could not feel his hands.

"Every person," he said.

"Every person."

"*Together with their issue.* Children. Children not born yet."

"Until the Debt is discharged," said Sallow quietly. "Which it never will be. You've read the clause. It's grown every year for three hundred years. It's larger than the Reach. It will always be larger than the Reach, because it has interest and the Reach has only people." He held out his hand for the paper. "Total Assessment, they call it in the Factor's House. I've heard the phrase."

Idris did not give it back.

"Nine million people," he said. "And the moons. Every one of them a bond. Forever. And the Prelate *signed* it."

"The Prelate had very little choice. The Concord's broke, Idris. It's been broke for fifty years. It's lived on the Levy and the tolls and the House's patience, and the House's patience has run out. If she hadn't signed, the Squadron would have come anyway, and she'd have been hanged in the Square, and somebody else would have been made Steward. This way she keeps her rooms in the Basilica." Sallow's hand was still out. "And we keep the Eye. The Keepers. That's in the Instrument too, if you read the back. The Keepers continue in their office, under the House. The Light holds."

"You agreed to this."

"I serve the Light." Sallow's voice did not rise. "So do you. The Light doesn't care who owns it. It doesn't care who comes through it, or what they're carrying, or what they've signed. It breathes. That's all. We keep it breathing. That's all we've ever done."

"Then why did they need me to lie about the *Clemency*?"

"Because the Concord's own Guard doesn't know. The Wardens' Guard. Because the Prelate doesn't want riots in the Square before Firstlight, and if the Guard knew they'd talk, and if the Sump knew there'd be fire in the Spokes for a month. So the House has been bringing its garrison in quietly, a ship at a time, to hold the Hub and the docks and the Basilica when the Squadron comes. So that there's no trouble." Sallow lowered his hand at last. "And for that they needed three Keepers to write three wrong numbers in a book. And they found three. And I didn't know." He looked down at his knees, at the rug, at the black stick lying across it. "I'll tell you something, Idris. I've been a Keeper for fifty-six years. I'd have said, before this morning, that there wasn't a Keeper in the order who could be bought. Not one. I'd have staked my line on it." He was quiet. "What did they give you?"

Idris could not answer.

"It was the girl," Sallow said. "Wasn't it. Your sister."

"Yes."

"Of course it was." The old man nodded slowly. "Of course."

They stayed like that for a long time, the old man in his chair with his hands on the rug, and Idris standing in the middle of the study with the Instrument in his hand. Out in the window the Eye breathed, and once, very far off and very small, a ship came up out of its center like a fish rising, and the governor took the weight of it under their feet, and let it go.

"What will you do?" Sallow said at last.

"I don't know."

"Don't do anything," said Sallow. "Please. I'm asking you. As your master. As the man who taught you the invocation. Don't do anything. There's nothing to be done. It's signed. It's sealed. The Squadron's waiting at Ostrava for Firstlight, and the garrison's in the Hub, and in twenty-four days it will all be over, and then it will just be how things are. The Light will hold. It always holds." He looked up. "You've already done one thing you can't take back. Don't do another."

Idris folded the Instrument along its creases, very carefully, and handed it back to him. Sallow took it, and held it on his knee, and did not put it away.

"Who else knows?" Idris said.

"The Prelate. The Factor. Some of the Factor's people. Me." Sallow paused. "And now you."

"Not the Square."

"No. Not the Square."

"Not the Sump."

Sallow looked at him for a long time.

"No," he said. "Not the Sump."

Idris bowed, from the waist, with his hand on his father's line, as a Keeper bows to the Archkeeper. Then he turned and went out of the study, and the old man did not call him back.

* * *

He knocked on the door under *ABANDON HOPE* at the second bell of third watch, and Teodor Gaunt opened it a hand's width and looked at him, and this time did not turn to ask anyone. He opened the door wide.

"She said you'd come back," he said.

The room was full again. More of them, this time. Twenty, twenty-five, sitting on the floor in the lamplight, young and not so young; there was an old man with a docker's hook for a left hand, and a woman with a baby asleep inside her coat, and two girls in the green smocks of the Fourth Spoke's water levels who could not have been more than fifteen. They all looked at him as he came in. They did not look away this time. He picked his way between them toward the far wall, toward the marks, and Lark was there, sitting under them where she had sat before, with her back against the wall; and she looked up at him, and her face was still and very tired, and she did not say *go home*.

"You found something," she said.

"Yes."

"Tell them."

He told them.

He stood in the middle of the old bunkroom with the lamps on the floor round his feet and the marks on the wall behind him and the milk-white port at the end, and he told them everything. The *Clemency* and her mass and what he had written. The *Constancy* and the *Benevolence*, and the two Keepers' initials. The lines of armored men coming down the ramps at the twelfth bell, four abreast, and the small figures in black and in white-and-gold watching from the berth's edge. The Firstlight rota. Forty-one ships, nine abreast in the van. And the Instrument: he had read it twice and he found he could say it nearly word for word, and he did, standing there, in Sallow's beautiful old study voice without meaning to, *whereas*, and *whereas*, and *now therefore*, down to *every person born in the Reach, or resident in the Reach at Firstlight, of whatever age or condition*, down to *together with their issue*.

When he stopped, nobody spoke.

The woman with the baby had put her hand over its head inside her coat.

"Together with their issue," said Teodor Gaunt, at last. He said it slowly, as though he were reading it off a wall. "That's a nice way to put it."

"It's the House's way," said Idris.

"I know whose way it is." Teodor looked at him. In the lamplight the scar through his eyebrow was white. "My grandmother's grandmother went Inward in the Levy, Keeper. In the year two hundred and something. So did her brother. They didn't come back. We've been dodging the Drum in my family for four generations. That's what we are, all of us in this room—you know that? You know what this is? This isn't a—" he waved a hand at the room, the lamps, the people. "This isn't a *club*. We're Levy-dodgers. The Unlevied. Some of us were drawn and ran. Some of us have families who ran so long ago they've got no ward-numbers, nothing, they're not in the Drum because the Concord doesn't know they're alive. We move people. We get them off the cord and down to the docks and onto ore barges and out to the mines on Gall, or the ice on Brine, where the Concord doesn't look. We've been doing it a hundred years." He looked round at the room. "We knew something was coming. Since the summer. Ships in the Factor's berth at night. Guard rotations changed, the Hub locked down in places nobody could say why. We knew. We didn't know what."

"Now you know," said Idris.

"Now we know." Teodor turned back to him. "And why do *you* want us to know, Keeper? You wrote the ship down wrong for your sister's name. You've got her name back. You're done. Why come down here and tell us?"

Idris did not have an answer that would fit in words. He stood in the lamplight with all of them looking at him.

"Because there isn't any getting her name back," he said finally. "Not now. Not from that. *Every person.* There isn't a reconciliation for every person. There isn't a reserve list long enough." He looked at Lark. "I paid to take her name out of the Drum. In twenty-four days they're going to throw the Drum away and write her name on a roll she'll never come off, and her children's, and theirs. Everybody's. Benno Kask's mother's. Yours." He looked back at Teodor. "I didn't come down here to help you. I came down here because there isn't anywhere else to go."

Teodor looked at him a long time.

Then he looked at Lark.

And Lark got up, slowly, off the floor under the marks, and came and stood beside her brother in the middle of the room, close enough that her shoulder touched his arm through his habit; and she looked at Teodor and at the rest of them, and said: "He's on the Firstlight rota. At the governor. North quarter. By the Housing." Her voice was steady. "He can get into the Spindle. Nobody else in this room can do that. Nobody else in the Sump. Nobody else in Tithe who'll talk to us." She paused. "So he's ours now. Aren't you, Idris?"

He looked down at her.

"Yes," he said.

* * *

They talked until the fifth bell. Plans, and half-plans, and plans that were nothing but anger with a shape on it. Idris listened to most of it without speaking. He did not know how to talk about such things. He knew the governor, and the gauges, and the Register, and the Rule; he did not know how many Guards stood at the Basilica's east gate or which ore barges' captains could be bought or what the Wardens' Guard would do if the Sump came up the service stairs with knives, and they did, and they argued about it in low voices, round and round, and he stood in the middle of it with his hands in his sleeves and watched their faces in the lamplight, and thought about Sallow's hands on the rug.

When it broke up, and they went out in twos and threes into the corridor, Lark stayed. So did he.

She sat down again under the marks, and after a moment he sat down beside her, on the cold floor, with his back against the old grey wall. He had not sat on a floor since he was a novice. His knees hurt.

"You lied in the book," she said, without looking at him.

"Yes."

"The Register. The holy one. Three hundred years, nobody's ever—you did that."

"Yes."

"For me."

"Yes."

She was quiet for a long time. The lamps on the floor had burned low and nobody had trimmed them, and the light had gone gold and small, and the marks on the wall above them were mostly in shadow now, except the lowest rows, the small ones, in their tens.

"Don't ever do anything for me again," Lark said.

"All right."

"I mean it. Not ever. I don't want to be a thing somebody does something for. I don't want to be the reason somebody's name goes on the cord. I'd rather go myself. I'd rather go a hundred times."

"I know."

"You don't know. You've never—" She stopped. She put her head back against the wall and shut her eyes. "Benno's going to the pens," she said. "Day after tomorrow. That's when they take the drawn. Ten days before Firstlight they go into the Levy pens at the Hub docks, by the berth where the *Remittance* is, and they don't come out again till they go aboard her. I went to see him. At the stall." She did not open her eyes. "I bought a cake. He gave me extra salt. He doesn't know who I am. He doesn't know anything. He thinks he's going to fly."

"He told me."

She opened her eyes and looked at him.

"You went," she said.

"Yes."

"And you didn't tell him."

"No."

"No," Lark said. "Neither did I."

They sat together on the floor of the old bunkroom in the Sump with their backs against the wall and the lamps going out round their feet. After a while, without looking, Lark reached up behind her and put her fingertips on the marks; and after another while, Idris did the same. The wall was cold. The little ridges of the scratches were rough under his fingers, very slightly, the way a scar is rough, and he found the bottom rows by feel, the small ones, in their tens, the ones some long-dead hand had cut to count the days it owed, and he ran his fingers along them in the dark, one row and the next and the next, and somewhere toward the end of them he found one with a deep hard line cut under it, deeper than any of the others, so deep his fingertip fit inside it.

"Feel that one," he said.

"I know," said Lark. "Everybody knows that one. The kids put their finger in it for luck. Teodor says it's where they stopped." She took her hand down. "Nobody knows what happened that day."

"No," said Idris, and left his fingertip in the cut for a long time, as though it might tell him.

"In the Square," he said. "When the cord went on. The Guard said *bit early for that*, and you said *not really, as it turns out*." He took his hand down from the wall. "You saw it before I did. All of it. Didn't you?"

"Not the Instrument," said Lark. "Not *together with their issue*." She looked at the dead lamps. "But the shape of it. Yes. Everybody down here's seen the shape of it all their lives. It's only you up there who didn't." She leaned her head, very slightly, against his shoulder. "Now you see it."

"Now I see it," said Idris.

# Chapter 12: Brine

*The Dark · Year 419*

It rains in Brine.

That was the first thing I learned there, and the thing I remember first, still, when I think of it: the rain. It does not rain anywhere else in the world. Not in Tithe. In Tithe water is a thing that comes in tanks, and goes into pipes, and comes out of taps in a thin cold string that you catch in a cup and do not spill. In Brine it falls from the sky.

The sky in Brine is ice. The city was cut into the underside of the moon's crust in the Light, Marrow told me, when there were machines to cut it: great caverns hollowed out of the ice a kilometer under the surface, one above another, with the dark ocean underneath them all, and the ice above them for a roof. And in the Light there were heat-pumps in the roof, thousands of them, that kept the ice so cold that it was as hard as stone, and the city lived under it as dry as a Hold. But the pumps are dying, like everything, and the roof is going soft, and so it drips. Everywhere, all the time. A slow cold fall of drops from the blue-black ice overhead, far up, so far that you cannot see where they start; they come down out of the dark and hit your face and your hands and the oilskin coats everybody in Brine wears, and they patter on the roofs of the houses and run down the streets in channels, and the whole city is full of the sound of it, a soft endless whisper, like a crowd talking in another room.

"You get used to it," said Marrow.

"Do you?"

She thought about it. Water ran off the brim of her hood and down the white scar on her face.

"No," she said.

We had come down through a shaft in the ice, in a cage on a cable, the *Patience* left in a dock on the surface with her engines ticking in the cold. The cage had gone down for a long time through the blue dark, the walls of the shaft sliding past close enough to touch, glassy and lit from inside in places by old lamps buried in the ice, so that we went down through pools of blue light and then dark and then light; and then the shaft opened out, and the cage swung out into nothing, and below us was Brine.

I had thought Tithe was big. Tithe is a broken thing on the outside of a ring. Brine is a world inside a world. The cavern we came down into was wider than I could see across, and its roof was ice, black-blue, ribbed and fluted like the inside of a shell, hung with long spears of ice that dripped; and its floor, far below, was a city. Streets and houses and towers and bridges, stone and metal, built in tiers up the cavern's sides like the seats in a theater. Lamps everywhere. Not many—most of the city was dark, the way most of Tithe is dark—but more than I had ever seen in one place. Thousands. Little yellow lamps in windows, white lamps on corners, a long string of blue ones along a bridge. And under it all, below the lowest tier, where the city should have had a floor, black water.

"That's the Eighth district," Marrow said. She pointed down at the water, where you could see, under its surface, roofs. Towers. The tops of streets, going down into the dark. "Went under when I was a girl. The Ninth's below it. The Tenth below that. That's where we dug the lung out of. Took us four months, in suits, under the water, in the dark." She looked down at it. "We lose a district every year or two, now. The roof gets soft, a piece comes down, the ocean comes up. You move up a tier. And then another." She turned away. "The Reeve's Hall is on the Second. We'll walk."

We walked. Up through the city, through streets where the water ran down the gutters and people in oilskins turned to watch us pass, the Reeve's armored men in front and behind and Marrow beside me and Brother Cinder shuffling at the back in his chains. They were pale, the people of Brine. Paler than in Tithe, as though the rain had washed the color out of them. Many of them coughed. Children stood in doorways and watched me with big eyes, and one of them, a girl with her hair in a wet braid, pointed at the white string at my throat and said something to her mother, and her mother pulled her inside.

"They know what you are," said Cinder, behind me, softly.

"How?"

"The string. There are no Tallies in Brine. There never have been. But everybody knows the stories. The Tallies of Tithe, who keep the Count." He was breathing hard from the climb. "They've heard you've come. The whole city's heard. They're saying the Reeve's brought a Tally to wake the Light."

"Is that what they want?"

"It's what everybody wants," said Brother Cinder, and stumbled, and one of the men caught him by his chain and pulled him upright, not gently.

* * *

The Reeve's Hall had been something else in the Light. You could see it in the shape of it: a great square building of grey stone on the Second tier, with tall windows and a wide stair, and over its door, carved into the stone, a lamp. A lamp with a flame, like the ones the Lamplighters burn. Under it were letters, old ones, worn by four hundred years of rain. *CONCORD OF THE REACH — BRINE WATER AUTHORITY.* I read them aloud without meaning to, and Marrow looked at me.

"You can read that?"

"Quill taught me the old letters."

"Huh." She looked up at it. "I always wondered what it said." She did not ask what the Concord was. Neither did I.

They gave me a room on the Hall's top floor. It had a window, a real one, glass, looking out over the city and its lamps and its rain, and a bed with blankets, and a jug of water on a table that nobody would measure. I stood and looked at the jug for a long time. Then I drank all of it, and then I was ashamed, and then I lay down on the bed with my face to the wall, and thought of the children in the dome in Hold Fourteen, and could not stop.

The Reeve sent for me on the second day.

He took me down. Not up, to his rooms, as I had thought; down, under the Hall, through a door of old iron and down a long stair cut into the rock under the ice, where the rain could not reach, so that for the first time since I had come to Brine there was no sound of dripping; only our feet on the stone and his breathing, which was bad on the stairs, and the echo. Marrow came with us, and two men. Brother Cinder came too. I did not know why. He walked behind the Reeve with his chain held in his hands so it would not clink, and his eyes were very bright.

At the bottom of the stair there was another door, round, like a lock, and through it a room.

It was the strangest room I had ever been in. It was round, and domed, and its walls were metal, old, gone green and black, and they were covered—all over, every part of them, the walls and the dome and even the floor under our feet—in lines. Gold lines. Fine ones, close together, running in curves and loops and spirals all round the room, like frost on a window if frost were made of gold. Like the lines on the Halo. In the middle of the room there was a pillar of the same metal, waist high, with a flat top, and on the flat top a machine: a thing of brass and glass and old dials, with wires running from it to the walls, and other wires, newer ones, rough, patched with tape, running to a box on the floor that hummed. Two of the Reeve's people were sitting by the box on stools. Tinkers, by the look of them, with tools in their aprons. They stood up when the Reeve came in.

"This was a Keepers' house," said the Reeve. He went to the pillar and put his hand on it, as though it were a horse. "In the Light. The Keepers kept the Halo, and the Eye, and they had houses like this all over the Reach. Listening houses. Cinder found the word for it in a book."

"Phase-ear," said Brother Cinder softly.

"Phase-ear." The Reeve nodded. "It listens to the Halo. From here. From a hundred thousand kilometers off. All those lines—" he waved his hand at the walls— "are tuned to the lines on the Halo. When the Halo rings, they ring. In the Light, the Keepers sat in here and listened to the Eye breathe, so they'd know if it was sick." He looked at the machine on the pillar. "It took my tinkers six years to make it go. It's been going for five."

"What does it listen to now?" I said. "The Halo's dead."

The Reeve looked at me for a long moment.

"Play it," he said to the tinkers.

One of them bent to the humming box on the floor and turned something on it, and the hum changed, and then out of the machine on the pillar, out of a brass horn on its side that I had not noticed, there came a sound.

It was very quiet at first. A hiss. Like the rain upstairs, like breath. Then, under the hiss, a beat.

A beat. And another. A soft deep knock, far off, like someone striking a great hollow thing a long way away. And another. And then a run of them, quick, together, *knock-knock knock-knock*, and then a stop. And then another run. And a stop.

I stood very still.

It was what Ness had told me. Under the dome in Hold Fourteen, with her shoulder against mine, tapping her fingers on her leg. *A run of beats and then a stop. Over and over. Like somebody saying something.*

"The hullwalkers have felt it for a hundred years," said the Reeve. "In their boots, on the Halo. They call it the Halo singing. They say it means bad weather." He stood with his hand on the pillar, listening. "It doesn't mean weather. I've had my people listen to this every day for five years, Tally, and write down every beat. It's got a pattern. It repeats. It's always the same, over and over, and then sometimes it stops for days, and then it starts again, the same." He turned his head toward the brass horn. "And it's getting stronger. Five years ago my tinkers had to turn the box up as far as it would go to hear it. Now they turn it down."

*Knock-knock knock-knock. Knock. Knock-knock.* A stop.

"Where's it coming from?" I said.

"The Eye," said the Reeve.

I did not understand.

"Not the Halo. Not the Spindle. Not the ring. The middle." He held out his hand flat, palm down, in front of him, and moved it in a circle. "The phase-ear can tell. The lines on the walls can tell where on the Halo a sound starts, by which of them rings first. And this one doesn't start anywhere on the Halo. It starts in the middle. In the hole. Where the Eye was." He let his hand fall. "Where the door was."

*Knock-knock knock-knock.*

"Somebody's on the other side," said the Reeve of Brine.

The room was very quiet, except for the hiss and the knocking.

"In the Light," said the Reeve, "the Eye went somewhere. Everybody knows that. The Lamplighters know it and the walkers know it and your Tallies know it. Ships went into it and came out somewhere else. A long way off. That's what it was for." He looked at the brass horn. "And then it shut. And nobody knows why, and nobody knows where it went, and for four hundred years it's been a hole full of nothing. Except there's somebody on the other end of it, still. Somewhere. And they're knocking."

"The Founders," said Brother Cinder.

We all turned and looked at him. He was standing by the round door with his chained hands pressed together in front of his mouth, and his eyes were full of tears, and the tears were running down his starved face in two bright lines, and he was smiling.

"It's the Founders," he said. "Don't you see? They went after the Light, and they found it, and they're bringing it home. They're at the door. They're knocking to be let in." His voice broke. "Four hundred years. We kept the lamps burning. We kept them burning so they'd see." He turned to me. "Tally. Tally, they're *knocking*."

"Shut up, Cinder," said the Reeve, not unkindly.

"They're *knocking*—"

"I know they're knocking. That's why we're here." The Reeve turned back to me. "I don't know who it is. Cinder thinks it's his Founders with their lamps. Maybe it is. Maybe it's somebody else. Maybe it's the Houses, that the Lamplighters say stole the Light in the first place. Maybe it's somebody we've never heard of." He coughed, and wiped his mouth. "What I do know is, they're trying to open it. From their side. And they can't. And they've been trying for a hundred years, and getting louder, and one of these days maybe they'll find out how." He looked at me with his pale grey eyes. "And when that door opens, Tally, whoever opens it is going to be the one standing in it. Whoever owns the door owns what comes through it. That's how doors work. That's how they've always worked."

"So you want to open it first."

"I want to be the one holding it," said the Reeve of Brine. "When it opens. From this side. With the key in my hand." He turned to the pillar again, to the knocking. "So that whatever comes through comes through to me. Not to every Hold in Tithe with a knife. Not to them, whoever they are, walking through into the Reach like it was theirs. To me. And then we'll see what they've got to say."

*Knock-knock knock-knock. Knock. Knock-knock.*

I listened to it. I did not want to; I could not stop. It went on and on, out of the brass horn, under the hiss, a run and a stop, a run and a stop. And the strangest thing, the thing I did not say to the Reeve or to Marrow or to Cinder weeping by the door, was that it was not strange. It was not strange at all. It was like a word on the tip of your tongue. Like a tune you heard once, in another room, through a wall, a long time ago, and that you would know at once if you could only hear one more note of it.

"Do you know it?" said the Reeve, watching me.

"No," I said.

It was true. I did not know it. I stood in the gold-lined room under the ice with my hands at my sides and the knock going through me like a second heart, and I did not know it, and I knew that I did.

"Turn it off," said the Reeve, and the tinkers turned it off, and in the silence I could hear my own blood.

* * *

They let me walk in the city after that. Marrow walked with me, and two men, at a little distance; but they let me walk. I think the Reeve wanted me to see it. I think he wanted me to see what he was trying to save.

So I saw it. I walked down the tiers of Brine in the rain with the white string at my throat and people turning in doorways to watch me. I saw the Third tier, which was mostly dark, and the Fourth, where the ice of the roof had come down in the hot month and crushed a whole street, and they had not cleared it yet, and you could see a shoe under the ice, and an arm. I saw the Fifth tier's edge, where it went down into the water, and children fishing from it with lines, for the blind white fish that live in Brine's ocean, and an old woman with a basket of them on her head, shining. I saw a Lamplighters' chapel with a hundred little lamps burning on its steps in the rain, the flames hissing and spitting when the drops hit them and somehow staying lit, and old men and women on their knees in the wet in front of them, praying. They had heard about me. When they saw me they stopped praying and turned on their knees and stretched out their hands toward me, and Marrow put her hand on my arm and walked me on, fast.

I saw a door, too, on the Third tier, in an alley, that nobody prayed in front of. It was an old door, iron, set into the stone, rusted shut, and carved into the stone above it there was a sign I had never seen before: a hook. A plain hook, like you would hang a pot from, or a line. Under it there had been letters once, but the rain had taken them.

"What's that?" I said.

"Keepers' house," said Marrow. "There's a few in Brine. All shut. Nobody goes in. They say they're cursed." She looked at the hook. "Kine had that one opened, a few years back, looking for books. Two of his men went in. They came out with nothing. Said it was empty. Said it was full of gold lines, like the ear, all over the walls, and that when they touched them they hummed." She shrugged. "Then they wouldn't talk about it, and one of them drank himself to death."

I looked at the hook for a long time. I did not know why it held me. It was only a hook.

"The Reeve's hiring walkers," Marrow said, when we had gone on.

"For what?"

"For the Halo." She did not look at me. "He's going to the Spindle, Tally. In ten days. With you. You knew that."

"Yes."

"Did you know you can't fly there?"

I looked at her.

"The Keepers left dogs," said Marrow. "At the Spindle. Machines. Old ones, from the Light, still going. Nobody knows how. They sit on the Halo round the Spindle for kilometers and if a ship comes near they shoot it. Kine's lost two boats finding that out. Twenty people." She walked on. "So you can't fly in. You've got to land a long way out, and walk. On the Halo itself. On the outside. In suits. And the dogs don't see you, mostly, if you're small enough and slow enough and close enough to the metal." She paused. "Mostly."

"How far?"

"Thirty kilometers. Maybe more."

I had never walked thirty kilometers in my life. I had never walked one, outside. I had never been outside except in the *Patience*, behind glass.

"So he's hiring walkers," said Marrow. "Every walker in Brine and every walker in Tithe who'll come for water. He sent boats round the Holds last week. He's paying a tank a head." She stopped walking. We were at the edge of the Second tier, by the Reeve's Hall, where there was a yard behind a wall, and a gate, and through the bars of the gate I could see people in the yard, a lot of them, in the rain. "There," she said. "That's them. You'll be walking with them. You might want to look."

I looked.

There were forty or fifty of them, in the yard behind the Reeve's Hall, standing about in the rain, or sitting on crates under a tarpaulin, or checking suits laid out on the wet stone: hullwalkers' suits, patched and repatched like the funeral cloth, every one different, every one somebody's whole life. They were the hardest-looking people I had ever seen. Scarred, and burned, and missing fingers, and thin, with the walkers' look in their eyes that I had seen all my life in Hold Fourteen, in Barrow, in Barrow's boy, in Ness: a look that is always half somewhere else, out on the hull, listening for a seal to go.

And at the back of the yard, by the wall, sitting on a crate with a suit across her knees and a patch kit open beside her, with her head shaved and the burn scar black along her jaw, Ness.

She was looking at me. She had been looking at me, I think, since I came to the gate. She did not get up. She did not wave. She did not do anything that Marrow or the men could have seen. She only looked at me through the rain and the bars of the gate with her face perfectly still, and then, very slowly, she lifted her hand off the suit on her knees and tapped her fingertips on her leg, just above the knee.

*Tap tap. Tap tap.*

Then she bent her head and went back to her patching.

"Know any of them?" said Marrow.

"No," I said.

# Chapter 13: Command Deck

*The Combine · FY 2291*

They pulled the dogs on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Three, as Haddad had said they would, in four hours.

Gang Four on the drivers and Gang Nine on the crane, in the Barn, in the dark, with their lamps swinging off the governor's gold and the great steel arms reaching in from the walls. Sol called the Count. Every dog took three rounds of it to back out even: a quarter-turn on each of the four big drivers at once, in the order of the star, so the dog came up out of its notch level and did not bind. *One for the hook.* Bracken and Pell and Juno and Sol on the four drivers, Pell one-handed with his bad arm strapped to his chest and a brace on the driver so he could lean his weight on it. *Two for the line.* Teague on the tether lines, keeping them all clear of the teeth. *Three for the dog.* And when the third round was done Oyelaran's crane came in out of the dark on its boom and gripped the dog's head in its claws, and walked it out of the bore, slowly, four meters of steel as thick as a man's thigh sliding out of the cuff with a long grinding whisper you could feel through your gloves, and swung it away to the racks on the Barn's wall where it would stay.

Twelve dogs. Four hours. Nobody spoke on the gang channel except to count.

When the twelfth came out, the governor moved.

Sol felt it before she saw it. She had her hand on the cuff of the twelfth arm, steadying herself while the crane swung the dog away, and the steel under her glove did something it had never done in three years: it shifted. A hair. Less than a hair. A tremor, as though something enormous on the other side of it had taken a breath. Then her lamp, which was on the governor's teeth, slid very slightly sideways along them without her moving it, and slid back.

"Oh," said Pell, on the channel.

"It breathes," said Teague. "See? Told you, son." And coughed.

The white scooter came up out of the dark from the Barn's lower hatch with its running lights blazing, and Haddad in her clean white suit rode it across the Barn to the third arm, the big one, the Inner Housing, and stopped there, and got off, and clipped on, and opened the plate.

Sol watched her do it. The plate was round, the size of a cart wheel, set into the Housing's flank, and it was held by ten bolts in a star, like a collar; and Haddad took them out with a small white driver from her belt, one by one, in the order of the star, and Sol caught herself saying the Count under her breath as the doctor did it. *Four for the time. Five for the air.* When the tenth was out Haddad swung the plate back on its hinge and pulled herself into the dark inside, and her lights went in after her, and for a moment the Housing glowed from within like a lantern.

Then she came out. She swung the plate shut. She put the bolts back in, in the order of the star, backward, ten to one. *Count it back down.*

"Chief Dace," she said, on the open channel, in her dry Halvard voice.

"Doctor."

"It's running."

"How long?"

"A hundred and one hours." Haddad's visor was turned toward the Housing, not toward Sol. "First light at the fourth hour of first shift on Day Two Eighty-Eight. Thank you, Gang Four. Thank you, Gang Nine. Good work." Her scooter puffed white and turned and went back down into the dark through the lower hatch, its lights getting smaller, and went out.

They rode home on the *Patience* with their helmets off and nobody spoke. Juno flew. Sol sat in the crew bubble and watched the Spindle get smaller behind them, black against Kettle, with its gold lines and its needle tip, and thought about the clock in the steel room in its belly, counting down; and she found that she did not feel anything at all about it, not fear, not anger; that she was perfectly calm, and very cold, and that her hands were steady.

"Tomorrow," said Bracken, beside her, in the bubble, very low.

"Tomorrow," she said.

* * *

Third shift change on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Four was at the eighteenth hour, and at the seventeenth there were three hundred and sixty bonded workers in the hab ring transfers and the galley and the ballast hold and the long cold corridors at the bottom of the spine, waiting, in their liners and their work boots, with whatever they had been able to bring.

It was not much. Drivers. Pry-bars. Cutters, with the gas turned down so the flame was a short blue tongue; Sol had told them not to light them unless they had to, and not at anybody. Lengths of cable. Bracken had a wrench as long as his arm. Mother Szell had a frying pan from the galley. Teague had nothing. He had said he did not need anything, and stood by the ladder at the bottom of the spine with his cloth in his hand, coughing, and watching their faces come up out of the dark.

The plan was simple, because plans that were not simple did not work, Teague said, not with three hundred people who had never done anything like it before. At the eighteenth hour, when the shift horn went, the spine corridor would fill, as it always filled at shift change: crews going up to the dock and down to the rings, Company clerks going off duty, Proctors walking their rounds. And in the middle of it, the gangs would go up. Gangs Two and Six to the dock lock at the spine's head, to hold it shut. Gangs Four and Nine and Eleven to the command deck, to take it. The rest to the admin stack's three doors and the Proctors' barracks and the comms room. Nobody hurt if it could be helped. Nobody killed. Take, and hold, and then wait, and let the Company do the sum.

At the seventeenth hour and forty minutes Sol went up the ladder from the ballast hold and out into the bottom of the spine, alone, to look.

The spine corridor ran the whole length of the platform, from the ballast hold at the bottom to the dock at the top, six hundred meters of white-lit grey corridor four meters wide with hatches off it every forty paces to the hab ring transfers and the admin stack and the stores. At shift change it was always crowded. She had walked up it ten thousand times, at shift change, in a crowd.

It was empty.

She stood at the bottom of it and looked up its length. The white lights hummed. The grey walls went up and up toward the dock, narrowing in the distance, with nobody in them. No crews. No clerks. No Proctors walking their rounds. Somewhere a long way up, a door was banging, slowly, as the platform's spin moved it on its hinge, and the sound came down the empty corridor small and clear, *clang*, and a pause, and *clang*.

She went back down the ladder very fast.

"They know," she said.

The ballast hold went quiet.

"What do you mean they know?" said Oyelaran.

"The spine's empty. All the way up. Nobody. There's no shift change. They've cleared it." She looked round at them, at three hundred faces in the low light of the work lamps. "They know we're coming. They've known all day."

"How?"

Nobody answered. Sol did not look at anyone. She was afraid of who she would look at.

"We go now," said Teague, from the ladder.

They all turned to him.

"Now," he said. "Not at the horn. Now. If they've cleared the spine, they're waiting for us at the top of it, and every minute we stand down here they're getting readier. Or they're not waiting at all, and they're going, and every minute we stand here they're closer to gone." He coughed, and spat, and folded the cloth, and put it in his pocket. "Either way. Now."

"Teague—"

"I'm going first." He put his hand on the ladder. "Don't argue with me, Sol. You don't let the young ones go first. That's the rule. That's the oldest rule there is." He looked round at them, all of them, in the lamplight. "Come on, then," he said, and began to climb.

* * *

They were waiting at the top.

The command deck was at the head of the spine, below the dock, behind a pressure bulkhead with a hatch in it big enough for a cargo sled. The hatch was shut. In front of it, across the full width of the corridor, there was a line of Proctors: twelve of them, in grey armor, faceless, with their visors down and their long-barreled riot guns held across their chests. Behind them, at the hatch, Lusk. He was not wearing a helmet. He stood with his hands behind his back and his square pale face perfectly calm and watched them come up the spine toward him, three hundred of them, filling the corridor from wall to wall, with Teague in front, walking slowly, coughing.

When they were forty paces off Lusk said, without raising his voice, "That's far enough."

Teague stopped. They all stopped behind him.

"Go back down to your rings," said Lusk. "All of you. Now. And I'll forget I saw any of you."

"No, you won't," said Teague.

"No," Lusk agreed. "I won't. But you'll be alive to be disappointed about it." He looked over Teague's head at the crowd. "Dace. I know you're in there. Tell them to go home."

Sol did not answer. She was in the third rank, between Bracken and Oyelaran. She could feel Bracken breathing beside her, slow and deep, like a man about to lift something.

"We want to see the Director," said Teague.

"The Director's busy."

"Then we'll wait."

"No," said Lusk. "You won't." He brought his hands round from behind his back. There was something in one of them, small and round, like a can. "Last chance, old man."

"I had my last chance in '64," said Teague, and walked forward.

The Proctors fired.

It was not bullets. Sol understood that later. At the time she understood only noise, a flat hard coughing roar from twelve guns at once in the narrow corridor, and Teague jerking and stumbling and going down on one knee, and somebody behind her screaming. They were firing stingers: blunt heavy rounds of some soft dense stuff that did not go into you but hit you like a fist the size of a fist, and knocked you down, and broke things. She saw a man in the front rank take one in the face and go backward into the people behind him with his nose spread across his cheek. She saw Teague get up off his knee. And then Lusk threw the can.

It burst on the deck in front of the front rank with a soft pop, and a cloud came out of it, white, fast, filling the corridor from floor to ceiling in a breath, rolling toward them. She smelled it before it reached her: sharp, like hot metal and pepper. Then it reached her, and she could not see, and she could not breathe, and her eyes were full of fire, and her lungs had turned to stone.

She had heard about stinger gas. Every bonded worker had. The Company used it on the Halvard yards when there was trouble. It did not kill you, they said; it only made you wish it would. She was on her knees on the deck. She did not remember going down. Around her in the white there were people screaming and people retching and people trying to run and falling over the people on their knees. The stingers kept firing, the flat coughing roar, *whump whump whump*, into the white. Someone fell across her legs.

And then Bracken was on his feet.

She saw him through the fire in her eyes, a great dark shape rising up out of the white beside her, with the wrench as long as his arm in his fist. He did not make any sound at all. He walked forward through the gas, into the stingers, and she saw one hit him in the chest and saw him rock back on his heels and keep coming; and then he was at the Proctors' line and the wrench came round in a long slow arc, and a Proctor's gun went flying, and the Proctor went down; and Bracken kept going.

And then they were all going. The whole spine. She did not decide to get up and she was up. She did not decide to go forward and she was going forward, half blind, choking, with Oyelaran on one side of her and somebody else on the other, and the people behind pushing, three hundred people pushing up a corridor four meters wide into twelve guns; and the guns fired, and people went down, and the people behind them stepped over them and kept coming, because there was nowhere else to go, because the corridor was full behind them and the gas was in their lungs and the only way out of it was forward.

The Proctors broke. Not all at once. One, and then another, backing toward the hatch, and then all of them, and Lusk shouting something, and then the hatch was opening behind them and they were going through it, and Lusk was gone, and the last Proctor turned in the hatch's mouth and fired one more round into the white and then the hatch was closing; and Bracken got there.

He got his wrench into the gap. Sol saw it. The great steel jaw of the hatch coming down on the wrench's shaft and stopping, with a scream of metal, a hand's width from the deck. And Bracken got his fingers under the hatch's edge beside the wrench and lifted.

He could not have lifted it. Nobody could have lifted it. It was a pressure hatch, a meter thick, driven by hydraulics, rated to hold the command deck sealed against vacuum. Bracken lifted it. She saw his back bow and the cords stand out on his neck, and the hatch went up a hand's width, and another, and then somebody else's hands were under it, and somebody else's, and a pry-bar, and a driver, twenty people heaving at it with their eyes streaming and their lungs on fire; and the hatch went up, screaming, and stuck at half a meter, and the hydraulics behind it blew with a bang, and it stayed there.

They went under it on their hands and knees.

The command deck was empty.

She came through under the hatch into clean air, and fell on the deck, and lay there with her face on the cold metal breathing, and breathing, and could not stop; and when she could see again she lifted her head and looked round and there was nobody. Consoles. Chairs. Screens, still lit, green and amber. A coffee cup on a desk, still steaming. A coat over the back of a chair. A door at the far end, standing open, with a ladder beyond it going up. Up, to the dock.

"They're on the *Assessor*," said Oyelaran, on his knees beside her. His voice was a croak. "They're going. They're going now."

She got up. She did not know how. She went to the long window at the end of the command deck, the one that looked out along the mooring arm toward the dock, and put her hands on it.

The *Assessor* was moving.

She was a long grey ship, sleek and clean, with the Combine's blue on her flanks and lights along her length, and she was sliding away from the dock as Sol watched, slowly, very slowly, her docking clamps open, her lock sealed, her running lights blinking green and red. Her thrusters puffed white along her side. The gap between her and the dock was ten meters. Fifteen. Twenty. Sol could see her ports, lit, a row of them along her flank. In one of them there was a man standing. Tall, soft-shouldered, in a dark blue coat with the collar open. He was too far to see his face. He was standing at the port with his hand flat on the glass, looking back at the platform, the way you look back at a house you are leaving.

Then the *Assessor*'s main drive lit, a long blue-white flare along her stern, and she turned, and went, and the man at the port was gone.

* * *

Teague was in the corridor where he had fallen, twenty paces from the hatch, in the last of the white.

He was alive. He was lying on his side with his knees drawn up and his cloth pressed to his mouth, and the cloth was red, all of it, and his breath came in and out of him with a sound like a cutter's torch, a high rasping whistle, and his lips were blue. Two stingers had hit him: one in the shoulder, which was broken, and one in the chest. The gas had done the rest.

They carried him to the infirmary. It was empty, too. Amsel was gone; his stool by the instrument cabinet was pushed back against the wall, and the burn kit he had used on Pell's arm was still on the counter, open, and the stack of paper with the blue letterhead was not. Sol laid Teague on the table where Pell had lain and stood by him and did not know what to do. Nobody did. They knew how to splint a broken finger and seal a suit breach and drag a man out of a flare. They did not know how to do anything about glass lung and stinger gas, and the man who knew was on the *Assessor*.

Mother Szell came in, and pushed them aside, and put a mask on him from the cabinet, oxygen, and turned it up. It helped. A little. The whistle went lower. His eyes opened.

"Sol," he said, through the mask.

"I'm here."

"Did we get it."

"We got the deck," she said. "We got the deck and the dock and the stack. The whole platform. All of it. Nobody's left. They're gone. They left before. They knew we were coming and they left."

Teague's eyes moved behind the mask. She thought it might be laughter.

"Somebody told," he said.

"Yes."

"Always somebody." He breathed. "Doesn't matter. Don't—don't look for them. Not now. Later." His hand came up off the table, the one with the bloody cloth in it, and found her sleeve, and held on. "You've got the deck. You've got the platform. Now you've got to do the next thing."

"What's the next thing?"

"I don't know," said Teague. "That's why it's yours." His eyes closed and opened. "Sol."

"Yes."

"The letter. Dov's. In my kit. Open it. I think he'd want—" He coughed, under the mask, and the coughing went on, and when it stopped the whistle was louder again. "He'd want you to read it. Not me. He was writing to the factory. But he'd want you."

"All right."

"And the Count." His fingers tightened on her sleeve. "Keep the boy singing. You hear me? The farm boy. Keep him singing. When it's bad. It's—" he breathed— "it's the singing that gets you through. Not the—not the thing you're singing about. The singing." His mouth moved under the mask in what might have been a smile. "Ten for the Company."

"And damn the Company," said Sol.

"And damn the Company," said Teague. And then he was quiet for a while, breathing with that high whistle, his hand on her sleeve; and then he was quiet altogether.

* * *

They found out who it was an hour later, and they did not have to look, because Lusk told them.

It came over the platform speakers. Every speaker on every deck, the soft double chime first, high and low, the Company's chime; and then not the recorded woman's voice, but Lusk's own, flat and clipped and calm, from somewhere out in the dark on the *Assessor*.

"Attention all personnel on Platform TTH-9," it said. "This is Proctor-Captain Lusk. You'll be wondering how we knew. I'll save you the trouble. The Combine thanks Bonded Laborer Juno Szabo, zero three three one, Gang Four, for her information regarding tonight's planned disturbance, which she provided to my office on Day Two Hundred and Sixty-Four. Her service is noted." A pause. "Her request for passage on the *Assessor* was not approved. Lusk out."

The chime again. Then silence.

Sol was in the galley when it came, with a hundred others, sitting at the long tables with their eyes still red and streaming, drinking water, holding cloths to their faces. She heard it and did not move. She heard the room go still around her. And then she heard it begin to turn, the way a crowd turns, all those heads, looking; and she turned with it, slowly, and looked where they were looking, at the end of the far table by the wall where Juno Szabo was sitting alone with her hands flat on the table in front of her and her face the color of ash.

Juno did not try to run. She did not try to say anything. She sat with her hands flat on the table and looked straight ahead of her, at nothing, as they came.

There were a lot of them. Sol was on her feet and pushing through, and she was not fast enough; by the time she got to the end of the far table they had Juno up off the bench and against the wall, and somebody had her by the hair, and somebody had a cutter, unlit, against her throat, and somebody was shouting *the lock, the lock, put her out the lock*, and the shout was going round the galley and coming back louder. Ruzicka from Gang Nine had Juno's arms. Her face was wet and twisted. The man with the cutter was Danek from Gang Two, who had said *that's not true* in the ballast hold, whose bunkmate had taken a stinger in the eye in the spine and would not see out of it again.

"Stop," said Sol.

Nobody heard her.

"*Stop.*" She got her hands on Danek's arm and pulled the cutter down, and he swung round on her with his face wild, and she held on. "Stop it. Stop it. Teague said. You heard him. You don't kill anybody. Not one. Not a Proctor, not a clerk, not a Director." She looked at Juno, pinned against the wall with Ruzicka's hands on her arms and somebody's fist in her hair. "Not her."

"She sold us," said Danek. "She sold Teague. She sold *everybody*—"

"I know what she did."

"Then *why*—"

"Because Teague said." Sol's voice cracked. She did not care. "Because he said the minute you kill somebody they'll stop talking about what they did and start talking about what you did. Because he's lying on a table down the hall with his lungs full of blood and he told me that with the last thing he had. That's why." She looked round at all of them, the faces packed in round the end of the table, red-eyed, streaming, mad with it. "You want to put her out the lock? Then you put me out first. Go on. I'm her chief. Gang Four. My gang. You want her, you go through me."

Nobody moved.

Then Bracken was there. He came through the crowd the way he had come through the gas, not fast, not saying anything, and the crowd let him through the way the Proctors had; and he stood beside Sol, very big, with his arms at his sides. His eyes were swollen almost shut. There was blood all down the front of his liner from where the stinger had taken him in the chest. He did not look at anybody. He just stood there.

After a while, Ruzicka let go of Juno's arms.

After another while, whoever had her hair let go of it too.

Danek looked at Sol, and at Bracken, and at the cutter in his own hand. Then he threw it on the floor, hard, so it skidded away under the tables, and turned and pushed his way out through the crowd and was gone.

Juno slid down the wall until she was sitting on the floor. She did not cry. She sat with her back against the wall and her knees drawn up and her arms round them, and looked at the floor, and said, very quietly, very precisely, to nobody: "Tibor. They said they'd take Tibor off my bond."

Nobody answered her.

"He's nine," said Juno.

Sol looked down at her for a long moment. Then she turned away.

The speakers chimed.

Everybody in the galley flinched. The double chime, high and low; and then not Lusk, this time, but a different voice, a tired low reasonable voice, a little hoarse, as though the man it belonged to had been crying, or had been about to and had stopped.

"This is the Site Director," said Imre Castellane. "I'm speaking to everyone on the platform.

"I want you to know that the Spindle's clock is armed and running, and that it cannot be stopped from the platform. Not from the command deck. Not from anywhere. I want you to know that so you don't waste your time trying. First light will occur on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Eight at the fourth hour of first shift, as scheduled.

"I want you to know also that any approach to the Ring by any vessel or person from the platform will be treated as an act of sabotage against Combine property and will be met with force. The *Assessor* is armed. I'm sorry. I'm telling you that, too, so that you don't waste your time.

"I'm—" He stopped. The speakers hissed. For a long moment there was nothing. Then: "I'm sorry. I'm so sorry. I want you to know that. I don't expect it to—I'm sorry. Castellane out."

The chime. Silence.

The galley was quiet. A hundred people, and nobody spoke. Somewhere out in the spine the banging door had stopped.

Sol stood in the middle of the galley with Juno on the floor at her feet and Bracken at her shoulder and Teague dead on a table down the hall and the clock in the Spindle counting, and she found that she was calm again. Perfectly calm, and very cold, and her hands were steady, the way they had been on the *Patience* coming home from the Barn. She thought about the clock in its steel room. She thought about the governor, breathing. About the twelve dogs on their racks on the Barn's wall where Oyelaran's crane had swung them, four meters of steel as thick as a man's thigh, each one.

*Because it holds on and doesn't let go*, Teague had said.

She turned. Pell was by the galley door, with his bad arm strapped across his chest and his face grey and his eyes on her. Behind him, in the door, small and dark and narrow in a plain Company coverall with no white suit and no running lights and no tablet, her hands hanging empty at her sides, stood Yusra Haddad. Sol had not known she had stayed. She had not known anyone had stayed. The doctor met her eyes across the galley and did not look away.

"Gang Four," said Sol. "Suit up."

"For what?" said someone.

"To go and put a dog back in," she said.

# Chapter 14: The Knot

*The Concord · 336 AL*

"We hold the Eye," said Teodor Gaunt.

He was standing in the middle of the old bunkroom with a sheet of salvaged plating propped against the wall behind him, and on the plating, in chalk, he had drawn the Spindle. Not well. Idris had given him the shape of it over three nights, the gallery and the Housing and the four quarters and the long stair of the Keepers' Spoke, and Teodor had drawn it as a tall thin box with lines across it, like a ladder, and marked the quarters with crosses, and the north quarter, where Idris would stand at Firstlight, with a circle. There were thirty of them in the room tonight. More than ever. They sat on the floor in the lamplight and looked at the chalk drawing as though it were an icon.

"Not the Hub," Teodor said. "Not the Basilica. We'll never hold the Basilica, not against what came off those ships. Not the docks. The Eye." He put his chalky finger on the circle. "Your brother's at the north quarter at Firstlight, by the Housing, with his hands on the governor. We get twenty people into the Spindle the night before, through the Keepers' Spoke, in habits. He can get us habits. And in the morning, when the Squadron's coming through, nine abreast, and the Prelate and the Factor are up in the gallery watching it like a feast-day parade—we take the north quarter. We take the governor. And we tell them: tear up the Instrument, or the Eye goes out with your Squadron in it."

There was a murmur round the room.

"How?" said someone. The old docker with the hook for a hand.

"Charges," said Teodor. "On the governor. We've got the stuff. We've been saving it for years—mining charges, from Gall, the kind they use in the deep shafts. We pack the governor with them. And if they don't sign, we blow it." He looked round. "They won't let it come to that. Not with forty-one ships in the Eye. Not with the Factor standing in the gallery."

They were all looking at Idris now. He had been sitting against the wall under the marks, beside Lark, with his knees drawn up the way she drew hers, and he had not said anything for a long time. He got up.

"You can't blow the governor," he said.

"Why not?"

"Because it isn't a lock on a door." He went to the chalk drawing and stood in front of it, and looked at the tall thin box with its ladder of lines, and tried to think how to say it. "The governor doesn't hold the Eye shut or open. It holds it *steady*. It breathes, all the time, a hair one way and a hair the other, and that's what keeps the phase from running. If you blow it—" He put his hand flat on the chalk, over the circle. "If you blow it while the Eye's open, the phase runs. All at once. Every phase-tap in the city's drawing off it, every one, the power for every Spoke and every ward and every lung, and the phase runs back up every one of them at once like lightning up a wire." He looked at Teodor. "The Squadron goes, yes. And the Spindle goes. And the phase-taps go, and the Spokes they're in. Maybe all of them. Maybe half. Nobody knows, because nobody's ever been stupid enough to try it. But you'd be killing millions of people to kill forty-one ships."

The room was silent.

"And they know it," Idris said, more quietly. "The Factor knows it. Sallow's answered all his questions in writing, under seal. If you stand at the north quarter with your hand on a charge, the Factor won't tear up anything. He'll stand in the gallery and wait. Because he knows you won't do it. And if you do, he's dead, and you've killed the city, and the next Squadron comes through a year later and finds it already in ruins and nobody left to argue." He took his hand off the plating. There was chalk on his palm. "It's not a threat. It's suicide with extra steps."

Teodor looked at him for a long time. His face in the lamplight did not change.

"Then what," he said. "Keeper. You tell me. You're the one who knows the Eye. What do we do?"

Idris did not answer. He did not have an answer.

"Benno," said Lark, from the wall.

They all looked at her.

"I don't know what we do about the Squadron," she said. "I don't know what we do about the Instrument. I don't know if there's anything. But I know there's a boy in the Levy pens at the Hub docks who's there because of my name, and I want him out." She got up. "Before Firstlight. Before the *Remittance* sails. I want him out and onto a barge to Gall where nobody'll look for him, and his mother with him. That's something we can do. That's something we've done before." She looked at Teodor. "Isn't it?"

Teodor looked at her, and then at Idris, and then back.

"Out of the pens is harder than off the cord," he said. "The pens are locked. Guarded. You'd need somebody inside."

"I can get inside," said Idris.

They looked at him.

"The Keepers bless the Levy," he said. "Every year. On Firstlight Eve. Four Keepers go down to the pens and say the invocation over the drawn, and bless them for the crossing. *One for the hook and two for the line.*" He heard how it sounded. He said it anyway. "It's on the rota. I'll put myself on it."

"And then what?" said Teodor. "You walk out with him under your habit?"

"I walk out with him in a habit," said Idris. "A novice's. Four Keepers go in. Five come out. Nobody counts Keepers." He looked at Lark. "But then he needs a ship."

"Then he needs a ship," Lark agreed, and looked at Teodor; and Teodor, slowly, began to smile.

"Then you'll want Dagny Shrike," he said.

* * *

Dagny Shrike drank on Gall Quay, in a place with no name at the end of a pier, where the ore barges tied up and the air tasted of rock dust and old oil. It was a long low room under a curved roof of bare hull plate, dim and loud, full of barge crews and dock hands and people who did not look like either, and when Idris came in at the fourth bell of first watch in his grey habit with his hood up, the room went quiet for about the time it takes to draw a breath, and then got louder.

She was at a table at the back. He knew her at once from Teodor's description, *you can't miss her, she looks like a crane with a beard*, though she did not have a beard. She was a big woman, broad and tall and heavy-shouldered, sixty perhaps, with a long grey braid down her back as thick as a hawser, and a face like something left out in the weather, and hands covered with tattoos so old they had gone blue and blurred and run together into a kind of second skin. She was smoking a pipe. When he sat down across from her she did not take it out of her mouth.

"Keeper," she said.

"Captain Shrike."

"Captain." She seemed to find that funny. "Teodor sent you."

"Yes."

"Teodor thinks I owe him." She drew on the pipe, and the bowl glowed. "I don't. He thinks so because I took six of his people to Gall last spring and didn't charge him full. That wasn't a debt. That was a mood." She looked at him through the smoke. "What do you want?"

He told her.

She listened. She did not say anything while he talked. When he had finished she took the pipe out of her mouth, at last, and knocked it out on the table's edge, and looked at the little heap of ash.

"Out of the pens," she said.

"Out of the pens. Firstlight Eve. During the blessing."

"And onto my boat. And out past the Hub docks, and the House pickets, and the Guard cutters, and out to Gall." She turned the empty pipe in her blue fingers. "On Firstlight Eve. With the city full of House soldiers."

"You know about the soldiers."

"Everybody on the Quay knows about the soldiers. You can't land three ships' worth of armor in a city without the people who move cargo noticing the cargo." She put the pipe away inside her coat. "And I'll tell you something else everybody on the Quay knows, Keeper, that you might not. Every ship in Tithe's been ordered to stay docked on Firstlight. Every barge, every tug, every cutter. Order came down from the Factor's House through the Harbor Office three days ago. No departures from the end of Firstlight Eve to the end of Firstlight Day. The lanes are closed to everything but House traffic and the *Remittance*." She looked at him. "So anything that leaves on Firstlight Eve's going to be the only thing moving in the sky. You understand what that means?"

"It means they'll see you."

"It means they'll see me." She did not seem troubled by it. "It means it'll cost."

"How much?"

She named a figure. It was less than Oake's, by half. It was more than the Unlevied had; Teodor had told him what they had.

"I can't—"

"I didn't say money." Dagny Shrike leaned back. Her chair creaked under her. "You're a Keeper. Teodor says you're on the governor. North quarter, Firstlight." She watched his face. "You know what I'd like, Keeper? I'd like to know what's coming through that Eye on Firstlight, and when, and in what order. Exactly. Lane by lane. Ship by ship. Nine abreast in the van, people are saying. I'd like to know which nine." She smiled, a little. "Not because I'll do anything with it. I'm an old woman with an old boat. Just because I like to know where the rocks are before I sail."

"That's not mine to give."

"No," Dagny agreed. "Neither's a boy out of a Levy pen."

He sat for a long time across the table from her in the loud dim room under the hull plate. He thought about Oake, eating candied kelp. *It's a number in a book.* He thought about Benno Kask at the foot of the Nine stair, grinning. *Benno Kask, pilot. You write that. Big letters.*

"I'll have it by Firstlight Eve," he said.

"Good." She stood up. She was taller than him, standing; she was taller than nearly anyone. "Come and see my boat. You should see what you're buying."

Her boat was at the very end of the Quay, past the last of the ore barges, in a berth so small and dark that he would have walked past it. She was a cutter. Small, old, ugly, with a blunt nose and stubby wings and a hull patched so many times that it looked like the funeral cloths the Sump wrapped its dead in, a quilt of mismatched plates in a dozen shades of grey; and she had a name on her flank, in old letters, faded and then painted over and then painted again by someone who had wanted it kept.

*PATIENCE*.

"Built before anybody's grandmother," said Dagny, with her hand on the hull the way a farmer puts a hand on a horse. "My mother had her, and her father before that, and back and back. There's a plate in her that says she's older than the Concord. Older than the Light, maybe. Older than the Founders." She looked at Idris sidelong. "You don't believe me."

"I didn't say anything."

"Come aboard, then, and see."

He went aboard. It was cramped and dark and smelled of oil and old pipe smoke, and she took him forward, up a short ladder and through a hatch into the cockpit, where there were two worn couches and a bank of gauges he half recognized and a long low window; and she pointed, without a word, at the bulkhead over the pilot's couch.

There was a plate there. Brass, green at the edges and bright in the middle, bolted to the bulkhead with four bolts polished flat.

*PATIENCE*, it said, in raised square letters. And under it, smaller: *OSTRAVA*. And a number. And words: *TUG CLASS III*. And at the bottom, almost worn away, a single word he had seen before. He had seen it every Levy Day of his life, at the end of the crier's call, and in the children's rhyme in the Sump, and in the Book of the Lighting, and under the Founders' feet in Basilica Square, cut into the plinth.

*COMBINE*.

"Told you," said Dagny Shrike.

* * *

He took the grille off at the second bell of third watch, alone, in the dark, with the Eye breathing below him through the gallery's long window and nobody on the gallery but himself.

He had thought about it for six days. He had thought about it on the stool in the Register hall at the end of the long table, and on the floor of the old bunkroom with Lark's head against his shoulder, and in the Archkeeper's study with the Instrument in his hands. He had thought about it standing in front of Teodor's chalk drawing with his hand flat on the circle. *You're the one who knows the Eye. What do we do?* He did not know. He knew the gauges and the governor and the Register and the Rule. And the Rule's first verse said there was one thing in the Spindle that a Keeper must never turn; and nobody knew what it did.

The grille was held by eight bolts round its frame, and each bolt was sealed with a disk of lead stamped with the hook. He cut the seals with a Keeper's knife, one by one, with his heart going so hard that he could hear it over the governor's hum. He had brought new lead, and the hook-stamp from his own desk, to reseal them after. He did not let himself think about after. He took the bolts out. He lifted the grille away from the Housing's face—it was heavier than he had thought, three centuries of brass—and set it down on the gallery floor, leaning against the wall, and stood back, and looked at the Knot without anything between them for the first time in his life.

It was smaller without the grille. It was only a lump of old steel. A cylinder as thick as a man's thigh, standing a hand's width out of the Housing's face, ending in a squared boss with a deep socket. He lifted his lamp and held it close.

There were marks on it. He had seen them through the grille all his life, fine regular scratches too worn to read. Up close, with the lamp, he could see that they were not letters. They were not decoration. They were scratches made by a tool. A wrench, maybe, or a driver, slipping in the socket, a long time ago, again and again, as someone turned it. And round the socket's mouth the steel was worn bright in a ring, the way a bolt-head goes when a driver has been on it many times. Someone had turned this. More than once. Someone had put a tool in this socket and turned it, and turned it, and then stopped.

And on the Housing's face beside it, cut into the steel, so small and fine that he had never seen them through the grille, there were three short lines. Scribed. Side by side, a finger's width apart, each one the length of a fingernail. Witness marks, a rigger would have called them, or a Keeper laying out a phase-line: marks you cut so that you would know, later, where a thing had been, or where it was meant to go.

He stood there with the lamp and looked at them.

Then he went to the round plate beside the Knot, the plate the size of a cart wheel, and held the lamp to it too. It had been painted over in the Keepers' grey so many times that its bolts were soft lumps. Ten of them. He counted them by touch, under the paint, going round. Ten bolts round a plate. Not in a ring, quite. He had always thought of them as a ring. He saw now, with the lamp close, that they were set so that the bolt opposite each bolt was not quite opposite, that if you joined them across the plate with lines—one to the one across from it, and that one to the one two places round, and that one across again—you would draw a star.

*One for the hook and two for the line.*
