He stood very still in the dark gallery with the lamp in his hand.

*Three for the dog and four for the time.*

He had looked it up, two days before, in the Archkeeper's library, in one of the old maintenance manuals in their cracked leather that nobody had opened in a century: *dog*. It was there. In a glossary at the back, in small faded type. *Dog (rigg.): a locking pin, driven through a fixed collar to engage a notch in a moving member and secure it against motion during assembly. Cf. governor dogs.* And then nothing more, because the pages after that one were gone.

He looked at the Knot. A pin through a collar. A cylinder of steel as thick as a man's thigh, driven into the Housing's face and standing out of it a hand's width, with a socket for a driver and a ring of bright wear round the socket and three witness marks scribed beside it.

*Sun on your left and the kettle below.*

He turned. Without meaning to. He turned where he stood, in front of the Knot, until the long gallery window was on his left; and through it, far off, past the curve of the Ring, small and dim and red as a banked coal, Hearth.

And below him, through the gallery's floor of glass, beyond the Ring, filling everything, Kettle.

*Dog in the box where the old men go.*

"Idris."

He spun round. The lamp swung in his hand and threw his shadow huge up the gallery wall.

Archkeeper Sallow was standing at the end of the gallery by the lift, in his nightclothes, with a coat thrown over them and his black stick in his hand. He was very white. He did not come any closer. He stood at the end of the gallery and looked at the grille leaning against the wall, and at the cut lead seals scattered on the floor, and at the Knot standing out of the Housing's face bare in the lamplight; and he did not look angry. He looked frightened. Idris had never seen him frightened.

"I couldn't sleep," the old man said. "I came up to look at the Eye. I do, sometimes, when I can't sleep. I've done it for fifty years." He did not move. "What are you doing?"

"Looking."

"At the Knot."

"Yes."

"*The Knot is the Founders'*," said Sallow, very quietly, "*and shall not be turned.*"

"I haven't turned it."

"No." The old man let out a long breath. "No. Thank the Light." He came down the gallery then, slowly, leaning on his stick, until he was standing a few paces off, and looked at the bare Knot in the lamplight the way you would look at a sleeping animal. "Put it back, Idris. Please. The grille. Put it back and seal it and we'll say no more about it."

"What is it?"

"I don't know."

"You must know something. You're the Archkeeper. You've been a Keeper fifty-six years. You keep the Rule."

"I keep the Rule," said Sallow. "I don't know what it's for. Nobody does. That's the truth, my boy, I swear it on the Light. I asked my master, when I was a novice, as you asked yours. He told me what his master told him. That the first verse of the Rule is older than the rest of it. That it was cut over the grille before there was an order of Keepers. Before the Concord. While there were still people alive who had seen the Lighting. That the first Keepers found it there, cut in the lintel, and the grille already round the Knot, and didn't know who had put either there; and they were afraid of it, and so they made it the first verse of the Rule, and forbade anyone to touch it, and wrote no commentary on it, because they had nothing to say." He looked at the three witness marks on the Housing's face, which he could not possibly see from where he stood. "That's all. That's all anybody's known for three hundred years."

"It's a dog," said Idris.

The old man looked at him.

"A locking pin," said Idris. "It's in the old manuals. In the glossary. Riggers used them, when the Ring was built. You drive them through a collar into a notch in something that moves, to stop it moving. *Governor dogs.*" He turned and looked at the Knot. "It's a pin, Archkeeper. In the Housing. Where the governor is. Someone put it there and turned it, more than once—you can see the wear—and left it. And cut those marks beside it, so they'd know where. And then someone else cut a verse over it saying never to touch it." He looked back at Sallow. "And there's a song in the Sump about a dog and a notch and a door."

"A song."

"The children skip to it. Our invocation's the first two lines. The rest is about—" He stopped. He did not know how to say it without it sounding mad. "*Sun on your left and the kettle below. Dog to the notch. Twice to the dark and once to the light.* That's instructions. That's a procedure, Archkeeper. It's a procedure like the phase tables. Turns. Directions. You don't put directions in a skipping song unless somebody once needed them remembered and couldn't write them down."

Sallow was quiet for a long time.

"And what does the procedure do?" he said at last.

"I don't know."

"*That is the way that you lock the door*," said the old man.

Idris stared at him.

"I was a novice from the Sump," said Sallow. "Did you not know that? Most of us were, once. They've stopped taking us now. But I skipped to it too, seventy years ago, on the Eleventh stair." He smiled, very faintly, and the smile went at once. "I'd forgotten it till you said. I'd forgotten I knew."

"What door?" said Idris.

"I don't know." The old man looked past him at the Knot. "I've wondered all my life, you know. Standing here. Every morning. Saying the invocation with my hand on that grille. I've wondered, my whole life, whether it holds the Light up." He paused. "Or holds it down."

The governor hummed under their feet. Out beyond the long window the Eye breathed, slow and even, blue-white at the rim and darkening at the heart to the color that had no name.

"Put the grille back, Idris," said Archkeeper Sallow. "Please. I'm asking you. Seal it with your own lead. And then go home and go to sleep, and on Firstlight stand at the north quarter and tune the governor for the Squadron as well as you've ever tuned it, and let the Light hold. Whatever that is—" he lifted his stick a little, toward the Knot, and lowered it— "it's been there since before the Lighting. Somebody put it there and somebody told us never to touch it, and for three hundred and thirty-six years we haven't, and the Light has held. Every day." His voice was not steady. "If you open that plate, I don't know what's behind it. Nobody does. It might be nothing. It might be a dead man's joke. Or it might be the end of everything. Of the Eye. Of the city. Of all of us." He looked at Idris with his long sorrowful face, and his eyes were wet. "Put it back."

Idris stood in front of the Knot with the lamp in his hand for a long time.

Then he bent, and picked up the grille, and lifted it, three centuries of brass, and set it back in its frame over the Knot; and put the eight bolts in, one by one; and melted his own lead over each one, and pressed his own hook into it.

Sallow watched him do every one. Then he turned without a word and went back down the gallery to the lift, slowly, leaning on his stick, and the lift took him down.

Idris stood alone in the gallery. The new lead was still warm under his fingers. He put his palm flat on the grille, where the brass was brightest, where three centuries of Keepers' hands had touched it, and he said the invocation, because it was what you did.

"One for the hook," he said, "and two for the line."

And then, because he could not stop himself, under his breath, in the dark, with his hand on the brass and the Eye breathing below him and Hearth small and red on his left and Kettle filling everything underneath:

"Three for the dog. And four for the time."

# Chapter 15: Kettle-Weather

*The Dark · Year 419*

They gave me a suit on the morning we left Brine, and Ness fitted it.

I do not know how she arranged it. She would not tell me, afterward; she only said that walkers fit walkers, and that nobody who was not a walker would know the first thing about getting a Tally into a suit, and that Marrow had looked round the yard for someone small enough to get the inside seals done up without stepping on me, and there she was. So I stood in a room at the back of the Reeve's Hall with my arms out, in my shift, while forty walkers argued and swore and patched their own suits in the yard outside the door, and Ness knelt on the wet stone floor in front of me with a suit spread out round her like a skin, and did not look at my face.

"Arms in," she said.

I put my arms in. The suit was old and heavy and stiff and smelled of other people. It was too big. She folded the excess at the wrists and bound it with tape.

"Don't talk," she said, very low, with her head down at my wrist. "Marrow's at the door. Just listen."

I listened.

"The lung was a shell," said Ness.

I did not move.

"The one they rolled down the ramp," she said, binding the tape round my wrist, round and round, her fingers quick and hard. "The day you went. Veska had Barrow's boy open it up that night to put it on the loop. It's empty. The core's gone. The filters, the fans, the whole heart of it. Somebody took it all out and put the casing back on and sealed the intakes with cloth so nobody'd look. It's a box. It's a big grey box full of nothing." She moved to my other wrist. "Number Three died four days after. Like Quill said. The whole Hold's breathing the black. Hesta's girl went. And Barrow. And three more before I left. I don't know since."

I was looking at the wall over her head. It was grey stone, wet, with a long dark stain down it where the rain had come in.

"Veska sent me," said Ness. "When the Reeve's boat came round the Holds hiring walkers. She came to me and said, *go, and find her, and tell her.*" She tore the tape with her teeth. "She said to tell you she was wrong. About what a song's worth. She said to tell you that."

"There's no second lung," I said.

"There's no second lung. There's no first one either. Arms up."

I lifted my arms, and she pulled the suit's body up over my shoulders, and her face came up close to mine for a moment as she reached round behind my neck for the collar seal, and I saw that her eyes were wet, and that her jaw was set so hard the scar stood out on it like a cord.

"I'm sorry," she said, against my hair, where Marrow could not see. "I'm sorry. I'm sorry. I'm sorry."

Then she stood back and said, loudly, "Collar's good. She's sealed," and Marrow at the door nodded, and Ness bent and began to pack her tools, and did not look at me again.

* * *

There were thirty-one of us on the *Patience* when she lifted off Brine. The Reeve. Marrow. Nine of the Reeve's soldiers. Eighteen walkers. Brother Cinder, in his chains. And me. They packed the walkers in the hold, aft, in the dark, among the cargo nets and the water tanks and the boxes of tools and charges and the sledges they would drag across the Halo, and they put me forward, in the cockpit, in the chair along the side wall under the brass plate that said COMBINE, and strapped me in.

The Reeve sat in the front chair beside Marrow. He did not look at me. He had not spoken to me since the phase-ear. I sat in my chair in my too-big suit, with my helmet in my lap, and looked at the back of his big grey head, and thought about a grey box full of nothing on the dock floor of Hold Fourteen, and the children in the dome, and Hesta's girl, and Barrow with his three missing fingers, and the knots I had not been there to tie.

I had never hated anyone before. I did not know what it was. I thought it would be hot. It was not. It was cold and slow and heavy, and it sat in the middle of me like a stone in a pocket, and it did not move.

We were two days out from Brine when Kettle flared.

I did not know what was happening. There was a sound first, from the front of the ship—a shrill rising whoop from somewhere in the bank of screens—and Marrow swore and hit something and the whoop stopped, and the Reeve leaned forward and looked at a screen, and said, "How long?"

"Forty minutes, maybe. Big one."

"Get us behind the Halo."

The *Patience* turned. I felt it in my stomach. Through the long front window Kettle swung across, huge, filling everything, banded purple and oxblood and yellow, and I saw what the screen had seen: on its face, low down near the limb, a place where the bands had gone white. Not white like a lamp. White like the inside of a burn. It was spreading. Sheets of lightning were running away from it across the cloud tops in every direction, very fast, faster than I had ever seen Kettle's lightning go, so fast that it looked like the white place was throwing them.

"Kettle-weather," said Brother Cinder, beside me. He had been put in the chair next to mine, with his chain looped through a ring in the wall. His voice was very quiet. "Have you seen it from outside before?"

"No."

"It's beautiful," he said. "Isn't it. Terrible and beautiful." His eyes were on the white place, and his face was full of light.

The Halo came up across the window. A wall of black metal, laced with dead gold, sliding across the stars and across Kettle's face until there was nothing in the window but the Halo, close, so close I could see the seams in its plates, and the pits and scars of four hundred years, and here and there, frozen to its face, things: pieces of old machines, a tangle of cable, a cluster of white shapes that might have been tanks or might have been something else. Marrow brought us in closer, and closer, until the *Patience* was sliding along the Halo's face a hundred meters off, in its shadow, with the whole bulk of it between us and Kettle.

"Hold here," said the Reeve. "Everybody behind the steel."

In the Holds, when Kettle flares, you go behind the steel: into the deep levels, under the old reactor shielding, where the walls are thick, and you sit in the dark and play cards and wait. On the *Patience* the steel was the hold, aft, where the water tanks were; the tanks and the cargo and the engines between you and the outside. So they unstrapped us and we went aft, all of us, the Reeve and Marrow and the soldiers and Cinder in his chains and me, down the ladder and through the hatch into the hold, where the eighteen walkers were already sitting in the dark among the cargo nets with their helmets in their laps, waiting.

Ness was there. I saw her at once, in the dim red light, sitting against a water tank with her knees drawn up. I did not go to her. I sat down where Marrow put me, against the forward bulkhead, between two soldiers, with Cinder on the far side of one of them. The hatch closed. The red light dimmed. Somebody began to deal cards.

The flare came twenty minutes later. You could not see it, behind the Halo, behind the hull, behind the tanks. But you could feel it. The ship's metal began to tick, all round us, a fast faint ticking like rain on a roof, and the red light flickered, and every suit in the hold that had a dosimeter on it began to chirp, softly, one after another, until the whole dark hold was full of little chirping sounds like crickets. Nobody spoke. The cards stopped. We sat and listened to the ship tick.

And then one of the walkers stood up.

He was a big man, bald, with a black beard. I had seen him in the yard in Brine; he had been one of the quiet ones, patching his suit alone by the wall. He stood up in the red dark among the cargo nets and took something out of the front of his suit, out from under the seal at his chest, where he must have had it all along; and held it up.

It was a lamp. A little oil lamp, brass, like the ones on the steps of the Lamplighters' chapel in the rain. It was not lit. He held it up over his head in the red light so everyone could see it, and he said, in a deep calm voice that carried all through the hold over the ticking: "The Light belongs to the Wick."

Then four more of them were on their feet. Walkers. Two women and two men, from different corners of the hold, each with something in their hands that was not a lamp. One of them had a gun. I did not know where she had got it. One of the Reeve's soldiers started to rise and she shot him, very fast, in the throat, and the sound in the hold was so loud that I did not hear it at all; I only saw the soldier go back against the bulkhead and slide down it with his hands at his neck, and then my ears were ringing and the hold was full of people moving.

I do not remember all of it. I remember the red light, and the ticking, and the chirping, and the shouting. I remember the bald man with the lamp walking through the middle of it toward the forward bulkhead, toward me, very calm, with his eyes on me and the lamp held up, saying something I could not hear. I remember Marrow coming up off the floor with a knife in her hand and putting it into one of the zealot women under the arm, where the suit's seals were, and the woman folding over it. I remember the gun going again. I remember a soldier falling across my legs.

And I remember Brother Cinder.

He was on his feet. The soldier beside him was dead or down, and the bald man with the lamp had come to him, and was bending over him with a key in his hand—a key, to the cuffs, I never knew where it came from—and Cinder's chain fell off his wrists and rattled on the deck. And the bald man held out the lamp to him. Held it out, in both hands, the way you would hold out a child. And said, in that deep calm voice, loud enough now that I heard it over the ringing in my ears: "Brother. Take it. Take the Singer. Take her to the Spindle. The Light is coming home and it belongs to the Wick."

Cinder looked at the lamp.

He stood there in the red light with his thin starved wrists bare for the first time since I had known him, rubbed raw where the cuffs had been, and looked at the little brass lamp in the bald man's hands. His face was full of light. It was the face he had had in the cockpit, watching Kettle's white place spreading. Terrible and beautiful.

Then he looked past the lamp, at me.

I do not know what he saw. I was sitting against the bulkhead with a dead soldier across my legs and blood on my hands from trying to get him off, and I suppose my face was what you would expect. Cinder looked at it for a long time. Long enough that the bald man said, "Brother?" and then, sharper, "*Brother*."

"It doesn't belong to anybody," said Brother Cinder.

And he put out his hand and took the lamp, gently, from the bald man's hands, and turned, and threw it as hard as he could across the hold; and as the bald man turned to watch it go, Cinder put his whole thin weight into him, sideways, and they both went over into the cargo nets in a tangle, and the bald man came up first, roaring, with his hands at Cinder's throat; and then Ness hit him from behind with a suit helmet, twice, and he did not come up again.

It was over very quickly after that. It had all been quick. Afterward Marrow told me the whole thing, from the bald man standing up to the last zealot dying, had taken less than two minutes. There were five of them, and three soldiers dead, and one walker, and the Reeve had been shot through the meat of his left arm and was sitting against a water tank with Marrow binding it, saying nothing, with his face grey. The red light flickered. The ship ticked. The dosimeters chirped.

And then the ticking stopped, and a new sound began.

It was not inside the hold. It was the ship. A long groaning whine, rising, from somewhere aft, through the bulkheads, through the deck, and a shudder under it, and then a jolt, hard, that threw everybody who was standing off their feet; and then another; and the *Patience* began, very slowly, to turn.

"The bridge," said Marrow, and was on her feet and at the hatch.

It was locked. From the other side.

"There were six," said Ness, on the floor where she had fallen, staring at the hatch. "In the yard. There were six who prayed. I counted them. There were *six*—"

The ship turned. Through the deck you could feel the engines, now, a growl rising to a howl. And the floor tilted under us, and pressed up, and the water tanks shifted in their nets with a groan, and we were moving. Fast. Faster than I had felt the *Patience* move before. And there was only one place, I understood, that anyone who believed what the bald man had believed would be flying her.

"They're taking her in," said the Reeve, from the floor against the water tank, very quietly. "To the Spindle. Straight in." He looked at Marrow. "Into the dogs."

* * *

Cinder opened the hatch.

He did it with the key. The bald man's key, to his cuffs, that he had picked up off the deck when everyone else was looking at the hatch; and he looked at it, and looked at the lock, and said, in his soft hoarse voice, "It's a Keepers' key. The Wick has kept them. They'll open anything on a ship this old," and he put it in the lock, and turned it, and the hatch opened.

Marrow went through first, with her knife, and two soldiers behind her, and I heard the shouting forward and then a shot and then nothing, and then Marrow's voice yelling for someone who could fly; and then the Reeve was up off the floor, somehow, with his arm bound to his chest, and gone through the hatch after them. I went too. Nobody stopped me. I went up the ladder and along the corridor and through the hatch into the cockpit and stood in the hatchway with my hands on its frame.

There was a dead man in the front chair, slumped against the straps. Marrow had cut him out of one side of them and was hauling him out of the chair by his collar. And through the long front window—

We were not behind the Halo any more. We were out from it, a kilometer out, two, and running along it fast, so fast that its face slid past below us in a blur of dead gold and black; and ahead of us, getting bigger, standing up off the Halo's rim against the stars and Kettle's turning face, was the Spindle. The needle. I had seen it from Brine and from the dome of Hold Fourteen all my life, small and sharp. It was not small now. It was a tower, black, laced with gold, going up out of the window's top, with something wrapped round it three-quarters of the way up, like a belt, or a band of glass. And all round its foot, on the Halo's face, scattered across the dead metal for kilometers, things were moving.

I could not see them clearly. Small dark shapes, low and many-legged, like beetles on a plate. Dozens of them. Moving. Turning toward us.

"Dogs," said the Reeve, behind me.

The first one fired.

It was a light. That was all you could see: a thin bright line, red, from one of the beetles on the Halo's face, very fast, reaching up toward us; and it missed, and went past the window on the left, so close that its glare lit the cockpit red; and then there were three more lines, and four, and the cockpit was full of red light from every side, flickering, and the *Patience* bucked and screamed under us like an animal, and something behind us went *bang*.

Marrow was in the front chair. I do not know how she had got the dead man out of it so fast. She had her hands on the controls and the *Patience* was turning, pulling up and away, hard, so hard that I lost the hatch frame and fell back into the corridor and lay there pressed against the deck with a weight on my chest like a hand. Something else went *bang*, aft, and the lights went out and came back red. The engines' howl went ragged. The red lines were all round us.

And then they stopped.

We were out of range. I felt it before I knew it: the weight on my chest easing, and the screaming of the hull dropping to a groan, and then to a long ragged shudder, and the engines coughing. Marrow was shouting at the screens. The Reeve was in the second chair, his face grey, his good hand on something, shouting back. The *Patience* was going down. Not down to anything. Down toward the Halo's face, which was close below the window now, closer, a field of dead gold lines rushing up.

"Clamps," Marrow was saying. "Clamps, clamps, clamps—"

We hit the Halo.

I was on the floor of the corridor and I went along it, all the way to the aft hatch, and hit it, and the world went black and red and came back. The ship was screaming. Not the engines; the hull. A long grinding shriek of metal on metal, going on and on, as the *Patience* went along the Halo's face on her belly, and I could feel it through the deck, in my teeth, every seam and pit of the dead ring going under us. And then a deep heavy *clunk*, and another, and another, and the shriek stopped, and the ship jerked once, hard, and was still.

Everything was very quiet.

Somewhere aft, somebody was crying. Somewhere forward, the screens were chirping. The red light flickered and steadied. And under me, through the deck, through the hull, through the clamps that had bitten into the Halo's skin and held us there, faint and far off and perfectly clear, I felt it.

*Knock-knock knock-knock. Knock. Knock-knock.*

* * *

They counted us in the hold an hour later, in the red light. Nineteen. Three soldiers dead in the fight and one walker and six zealots, and two more soldiers dead in the crash when a water tank tore out of its net and crushed them against the aft bulkhead. The Reeve with his arm. Marrow with a cut across her scalp that bled into her eyes until somebody tied it. Brother Cinder with the marks of the bald man's hands on his throat, black, sitting by himself against a cargo net with his raw wrists in his lap and his chain in a heap beside him, not chained to anything. Nobody had put it back on him. Nobody had told him he was free. I do not think anybody knew what to do with him.

Ness was alive. She had a cut on her cheek, and she had come and sat beside me in the dark without asking anybody's leave, and nobody had told her not to.

The Reeve stood in the middle of the hold. He had to hold onto a cargo net with his good hand to stay up. He told us what we were. We were on the Halo, he said, clamped on, thirty-one kilometers round the rim from the Spindle, just outside the dogs' reach. The *Patience* was not going to fly again without a dock. The drive was cracked. The air was good for twenty days, the water for longer. The radio still worked; the relay on her back still reached Brine, and Tithe, and every Hold that had a box like Veska's. He had already called Brine. A boat would come, in time, if one could be found that the dogs had not already killed.

"Or," said the Reeve, "we walk."

Nobody said anything.

"That's what we came to do," he said. "That's what I hired you for. That's what you were always going to do: land outside the dogs and walk in. We've landed outside the dogs." He looked round at us. "So now we walk. We've got the suits. We've got the sledges. We've got the Tally." He did not look at me. "We meant to land at thirty. It's thirty-one. That's all. The dogs don't see small and slow, mostly. We go small and slow."

Still nobody said anything.

I stood up.

I had not known I was going to. I stood up in my too-big suit in the red light, with Ness's hand falling away from my arm, and everyone turned and looked at me, and the Reeve of Brine turned too, at last, and looked at me for the first time since the phase-ear.

"There was no second lung," I said.

The hold was very quiet.

"You said you didn't lie about lungs," I said. "You said you'd buried too many people to lie about lungs. You said two. And you rolled one down the ramp at Hold Fourteen, and it was empty. It was a box. You took its heart out and sent the box." My voice was not loud. I did not need it to be loud. "Number Three died four days after I left. Hesta's girl. Barrow. Three more, before Ness came away. And how many since, I don't know. Because I'm not there. Because I'm here. Because I came with you for a lung that was a box."

The Reeve of Brine looked at me for a long time. Marrow had gone very still beside him.

"Yes," he said.

He did not say anything else for a while. He stood in the red light holding the cargo net with his good hand and his bad arm bound to his chest, and his face did not move.

"There was one whole lung in Brine," he said at last. "One. The one we dug out of the Tenth, four months under the water, in the dark. One. And I had a city of eleven thousand people under a roof that's falling in, breathing the black. And children. And I had a Hold of eighty in Tithe with a Tally I needed." He breathed. "I couldn't give it to you. I'd have had to take it out of a children's ward on the Third tier and put it on a boat. I couldn't. So I sent you the box." He looked at me steadily. "And I told myself that when the Light came back there'd be lungs for everyone. In Brine and in Tithe and in every Hold. That the only lung that mattered was the Halo. That if I could get you to the Spindle, nobody would ever need to choose again."

"You told yourself."

"I told myself," the Reeve agreed. "I'm still telling myself. I think it's true." He let go of the cargo net, and stood on his own, swaying. "You can hate me, Tally. I'd hate me. But it doesn't change anything. They're still breathing the black in Fourteen. The only thing that changes it is up there." He lifted his chin toward the forward bulkhead, toward the Spindle, thirty-one kilometers round the rim. "And whether you sing."

I looked at him. The stone in the middle of me did not move.

Through my boots, through the deck, through the clamps and the Halo's skin, I could feel it. *Knock-knock knock-knock.* A run and a stop. It was louder here than it had ever been in the phase-ear under the ice. It was in the soles of my feet and the bones of my legs and the floor of my belly, like a second heart, like something counting. I did not know what it was. I knew that I knew. And I knew one more thing, standing there in the red light in the dark hold of the oldest ship in the Reach with the Reeve of Brine looking at me: that I was going to go to the Spindle. Not for him. Not for the lung he had not sent, or the Light he wanted to hold in his hand. For the knock. Because something on the other side of the door was counting, and had been counting for a hundred years, and getting louder, and I was the only one left in the world who might know what it was counting.

"I'll walk," I said.

The Reeve looked at me for a moment longer. Then he nodded, once, and turned away.

"We go at the next bell," he said to the hold. "Suit up."

Beside me, on the floor, Ness let out a long breath, and got up, and put her shoulder against my shoulder, hard, the way she had under the dome; and on the far side of the hold, sitting alone against the cargo net with his raw wrists in his lap and his chain on the floor beside him, Brother Cinder lifted his head and looked at me, and his eyes in the red light were wet and very bright, and he did not say anything at all.

# Chapter 16: The Dog

*The Combine · FY 2291*

The tug locks were on the command deck, on a panel by the long window that nobody in Gang Four had ever seen, and Haddad unlocked them in eleven seconds.

"That's all?" said Sol.

"That's all." The doctor stood back from the panel. Its lights had gone from red to green, seven of them, one for each tug on the platform. "They're not complicated. They were never meant to stop anyone who could get to this room. Only people who couldn't." She looked at the green lights. "Like most things."

"And the *Assessor*'s guns?"

"Are on the *Assessor*." Haddad turned from the panel. She had found a suit somewhere, not her white one, a grey rigger's hardsuit too big for her in the shoulders, and she was holding its helmet under her arm like a thing she did not know how to carry. "Castellane won't fire on a tug. Not unless Lind makes him. And Lind won't make him until he knows what you're doing, because Lind doesn't do anything until he knows what everything costs." She looked at Sol. "What are you doing, Chief?"

"I told you. Putting a dog back in."

"Into a governor that's breathing. Into a notch that's moving."

"You can tell me when it's still."

Haddad was quiet. Then something in her narrow face moved, very slightly, at one corner of the mouth; and Sol realized, with surprise, that the doctor was trying not to smile.

"Yes," she said. "I can tell you when it's still."

* * *

There were six of them on the *Patience*, in the end. Sol. Bracken, with his chest strapped where the stinger had cracked his ribs, saying nothing. Pell, with his burned arm in its blue sleeve inside his suit, bound across his chest, who had said he was coming and had not argued when she said he wasn't; he had simply been at the dock lock when they got there, suited, with his helmet on, and had looked at her through his visor, and she had looked at the lock, and let him through. Oyelaran, for the crane. Haddad. And Juno.

Juno had been at the dock lock too. She had been standing a little way off from Pell, not with him, in her suit, without her helmet, with her hands at her sides. When Sol came down the spine she had not said anything. She had only stood there, very straight, the way she had stood against the galley wall.

"No," Sol had said.

"I'm Gang Four," Juno had said. "You said so. In the galley. *My gang.*" Her voice was very precise. "Nobody else on this platform can fly the *Patience* like I can. You know that. She pulls left under load and her number three arm sticks at forty degrees and her attitude jets are two seconds slow on the port side, and you need her to sit still off the Spindle's flank while Oyelaran works the crane, and nobody else can make her sit still. Nobody." She had looked at Sol then. Her eyes were dry. "Let me fly her. Then you can put me out the lock, after. I won't argue."

And Sol had looked at her for a long time, and thought about the eleven seconds it had taken Haddad to turn the lights green, and about Teague saying *don't look for them, not now, later*; and she had said, "Fly her, then," and gone past her into the lock.

Now Juno flew her. Nobody else in the bubble spoke to her, and she did not speak to anybody. She flew. The *Patience* went out from the dock and away from the platform and down along the Ring's face toward the Spindle the way she had gone ten thousand times, nine kilometers round the rim, low and steady, with her old Ostrava plate glinting over Juno's head in the light of the board; and the Spindle came up ahead of them, black against Kettle, laced with gold, standing up off the rim like a needle.

"*Assessor*'s turning," said Haddad, beside Sol, quietly, with her tablet in her lap.

Sol looked. On the board a small amber mark, a long way off, was changing its course.

"Coming back?"

"Turning. Watching." Haddad looked at the mark. "They'll have seen the tug locks go green on their own board. They'll see us now. They'll be wondering."

"Let them wonder."

The *Patience* came round the Spindle's flank and Juno brought her in, close, closer than Sol had ever seen anyone bring a tug in to the Spindle, until the rigging hatch into the Barn was right there off the bubble's port side, ten meters, a square black mouth in the tower's flank; and held her. She held her still. Sol had seen tugs held off the Spindle a thousand times and they always drifted, a little, a meter, half a meter, the pilot chasing it with the jets. The *Patience* did not drift. Juno sat in the pilot's couch with her hands on the board and her eyes on the hatch, not blinking, and the tug hung off the tower's flank as though she had been bolted there.

"Go," said Juno, without looking round.

They went.

* * *

The Barn was dark, and the governor was breathing.

Sol had felt it from outside, through the hatch frame under her glove: the faint tremor, the shift, as though something enormous inside were alive. Inside it was worse. Their lamps went out across the dark and touched the governor's gold, and the gold lit and went dark again as the light moved; and under the light, so slightly you could only see it if you held your lamp still on one tooth and watched, the whole ring was moving. A hair one way. A hair back. Slow, and endless, and alive.

"Racks," she said.

The dogs were where Oyelaran's crane had swung them, on the racks on the Barn's wall: twelve of them, side by side, four meters each, dark steel, their squared heads catching the lamps. Oyelaran went to the crane. It was bolted to the Barn's wall on its long boom, folded, its claws hanging open. He clipped in at its control seat and the crane woke, its little lights coming on amber, and the boom unfolded out into the dark with a long hiss of hydraulics you could feel through the wall.

"Which one?" he said.

"Any of them. They're all the same." She looked across the Barn at the governor, at the twelve arms reaching in from the walls with their empty cuffs, and the twelve empty bores. "Number One. It's closest. And it's square to the rack."

The crane took the first dog off the rack. It gripped it by the head in its claws and lifted it, and the dog came off its cradle with a slow heavy grace, four meters of steel turning very slightly on the crane's grip as the boom swung it out into the Barn; and Sol and Bracken and Pell went with it on their lines, alongside, hands on it, steadying it, the way you steady a cassette going in. It took a long time. Everything in the Barn took a long time. There was nothing to push against but your line.

They brought it to the first arm. To the cuff, the great steel collar through which the governor's teeth passed, with its bore standing empty where the dog had been the day before. The crane held the dog's point a meter off the bore's mouth. Sol hung on her line beside it and looked through the bore, past its mouth, into the notch in the governor's teeth that it lined up on; and watched the notch move. A hair one way. A hair back.

"Doctor," she said.

Haddad was on a line by the cuff, with her tablet strapped to her forearm, its screen glowing in the dark. She had a cable plugged from the tablet into a port in the cuff's flank. She did not look up from the screen.

"The cycle's eleven point four seconds," she said. "End to end. The notch is aligned with the bore for—call it a third of a second, at the mid-point. Each way."

"A third of a second."

"Twice every eleven point four." Haddad looked up. "You'll have to drive it in a third of a second, Chief. Four meters of steel. On a crane."

"No," said Sol. "I'll have to get the point in, in a third of a second. Just the point. Ten centimeters into the notch. Then the notch can't move off it, because it's got a dog in it. Then we can drive the rest at our leisure." She looked at the crane. "Oyelaran."

"I heard."

"When she says now, you put it in. Straight. Don't push. Just let it go in. We'll be on it." She looked at Bracken, and Pell. "Hands on the head. When it goes in, push. Everything you've got. Bracken, mind your ribs."

Bracken did not say anything. He put his hands on the dog's head.

"Count me down, Doctor," said Sol.

Haddad watched her screen.

"Ten," she said.

Sol put her hands on the dog's head beside Bracken's. Pell put his good hand there, and his shoulder.

"Seven," said Haddad. "Six. Five."

Through the bore, the notch was sliding. Slowly. Toward the mid-point.

"Three. Two."

*Three for the dog*, thought Sol, *and four for the time*.

"*Now*."

Oyelaran let the crane go.

The dog went forward. Not fast; it could not go fast. A slow heavy slide, the point going into the bore's mouth, a meter of steel and then two, the grinding whisper of it through her gloves, and the notch at the end of the bore sliding toward it, sliding, and the point reaching the end of the bore and going on into the notch—and Sol and Bracken and Pell all pushed at once, every gram they had, against nothing, against their own lines, and she felt the notch catch the point. Felt it, through four meters of steel, through her gloves, in her wrists. A small hard jolt.

And the governor stopped breathing.

She felt it stop. The whole Barn felt it. The tremor in the steel under her hands, that had been there since the twelfth dog came out yesterday, that she had felt in the hatch frame and the cuff and her own teeth—it caught, and held, and was gone. The governor was still. Her lamp lay on one of its teeth and did not move at all.

"It's in," said Haddad. Her voice was not quite steady. "It's in. Ten centimeters. It's holding."

"Drive it," said Sol.

They drove it. Three rounds of the Count on the four big drivers, the way they had backed it out the day before but the other way, *one for the hook*, driving it down into the notch, deeper, *two for the line*, until its head stood a hand's width out of the cuff's face and no more, the way it had stood for three years, *three for the dog*, and on the last round, at *ten*, Bracken said, very quietly, through his cracked ribs, "And damn the Company," and Sol said it with him.

Then they hung on their lines in the dark round the dog's head and breathed.

* * *

She called the *Assessor* from the Spindle, on the open channel, so that everyone on the platform would hear it too.

"*Assessor*, this is Dace. Gang chief, Four. I'm at the Spindle. Answer me."

It took a long time. Long enough that Pell, beside her, said *maybe they're not*—and then the channel clicked.

"Chief Dace." Castellane's voice. Tired, low. "I warned you about approaching the Ring."

"You did. Here's my answer. There's a dog in your governor."

The channel hissed.

"Governor dog number one," said Sol. "Seated. Driven home. Three rounds. Your clock fires in eighty-one hours, and when it fires it's going to try to couple a governor that can't move. Dr. Haddad's here. She can tell you what happens then."

"Dr. Haddad," said Castellane, after a moment. "Is that true?"

"It's true, Imre." Haddad's voice was quite steady now. "The governor can't breathe. When the clock couples it to the field, the field will try to move it anyway. It'll shear. The phase-lines are already partly coupled. The energy will run back up them, all of them, ten thousand kilometers of them, at once." She paused. "The Ring will tear itself apart. You'll lose it. All of it. Thirty years."

"And the platform?"

"Six kilometers off the rim. Some of the Ring will reach it. I don't know how much. Nobody knows. Nobody's ever done it."

"So you'll die too," said Castellane.

"Yes," said Sol. "That's the idea. We all die together. Or none of us does." She held on to her line and looked at the dog's head in her lamp, standing out of the cuff. "Come back and take us off. All of us. Every bond on the platform. Put us on the *Assessor* and take us out past two hundred kilometers, and wait for your first light with us, and then you can have your Ring. Or don't, and there's no Ring to light."

The channel hissed for a long time.

When it clicked again, it was not Castellane.

"Chief Dace," said the dry soft voice, like paper being folded. "This is Gessler Lind."

"I know who you are."

"Then you know I'll want to be sure of the figures." Lind did not sound angry. He did not sound anything. "You understand that the clock can be stopped from the *Assessor*. By signal. At any time. We needn't let it fire with your dog in it. We can stop it, and wait."

"Wait for what?"

"For you to run out of food. Or patience. Or air." A pause. "You have sixty days of stores on the platform. We have a ship."

"And you have a policy," said Sol, "that runs out on Day Two Ninety."

The channel was silent.

"Stop the clock," she said, "and wait. And on Day Two Ninety you've got four hundred bonded labor on a platform and no indemnity and a Ring with a dog in it that you can't pull without coming in here to get it. And then you owe us all completion bonuses and passages home and you can't pay them. You did the sum, Mr. Lind. I've seen it. On a single page." She breathed. "So did I."

The channel was silent for a long time. Long enough that she began to think he had gone. Then it clicked.

"Very good, Chief," said Lind. "Very good. I'll consider it."

The channel went dead.

"What does that mean?" said Pell.

Sol did not answer. She was looking at the board on her forearm, where the *Patience*'s relay was showing her what the tug could see. The small amber mark that was the *Assessor* had stopped turning. It was coming back. And off its flank, separating from it as she watched, a smaller mark. Much smaller. Fast.

"It means he's sending someone to pull it," she said.

* * *

It was Lusk. She had known it would be. There were twelve of them, Proctors in grey armored hardsuits, faceless, and they came in a Company boat, a fast ugly wedge of a thing with its own guns on its nose, and they did not try to dock. They came straight at the Spindle's flank, at the rigging hatch, at the *Patience* sitting still beside it; and Juno, without anybody telling her, took the *Patience* away from the hatch and out and round, fast, so fast the old tug groaned, and put her between the boat and the tower.

The boat's guns fired. Sol saw it on her board, and felt it, a moment later, through the Spindle's flank: a long stutter of impacts, small, hard, like gravel thrown at a window. Not at the tower. At the *Patience*.

"Juno," said Sol.

"I'm fine." Juno's voice was precise. "They hit her arms. Number two's gone. She doesn't need number two." The *Patience* swung again on the board, out and round, and the boat had to turn to keep its guns on her, and while it turned, it was not pointing at the hatch. "She's had worse. Haven't you, love? She's had worse than this."

The boat fired again. The *Patience* took it.

And then the Proctors were coming in. Not through the hatch. Over the tower's skin, on lines, fired from the boat on harpoons that bit into the Spindle's plating and held; Sol saw them coming on her board, twelve marks swinging across the gap and landing on the tower's flank and moving toward the hatch, fast, the way Proctors moved, the way they had been trained to move in the Proctor school on Ostrava.

"Lights out," she said.

They put their lamps out. All of them. The Barn went black. Utterly. A blackness you could not see your own hand in, could not see your own visor in, with only the faint amber glow of the crane's control lights, far off on its boom, and the tiny green of Haddad's tablet, and then Haddad put that out too.

"Riggers," said Sol, very low, on the gang channel. "This is our house. We built it. They didn't."

The Proctors came in through the hatch with their lights blazing.

It was not a fight. Not the way the spine had been a fight. Sol thought about it afterward and could not find a word for what it was. The Proctors came in through the hatch on their lines, twelve of them, with their helmet lamps and their gun lamps lit, sweeping, and their lights went out across the Barn and found nothing, and found the governor, gold, and lost it again; and they had to go forward, across the Barn, toward the governor, toward the first arm where the dog was; and the Barn was forty meters of nothing to cross, with nothing to push against but their lines.

The riggers were already in the nothing. On their own lines. In the dark. Where they had worked for three years.

Sol went across the Barn on her line hand over hand in the black, toward the nearest light, and the light was a Proctor, swinging, sweeping his gun lamp across the governor's teeth, and she came up under him in the dark, under his light, and found his line by feel where it ran back from his harness to the hatch frame, a thin taut cord in the black; and she had her cutter in her other hand, unlit, and she lit it.

The flame was a short blue tongue. It lit her own glove and his line, and nothing else. She put it to the line.

The line parted.

He did not feel it at first. He was swinging, and the line went slack behind him, and he kept swinging, out, across the Barn, toward the far wall, very slowly, the way Kostya had stepped back from the cassette, sweeping his light. And then his light swung round and found the cut end of his own line drifting past him, and stopped. And she heard him on the open channel.

He did not say *oh*. He screamed.

He screamed and grabbed for the line's end and missed it, and grabbed again, and missed, and his light went wild, spinning, across the Barn's walls and the governor and the other Proctors' visors, and he was still going out, very slowly, across the dark, toward the rigging hatch on the Barn's far side, the second hatch, the one that was also open, the one that looked out on nothing; and she watched him go through it. Still screaming. His light going round and round. Out into the black off the Spindle's flank, nine hundred meters over the Ring, with Kettle beneath him filling everything.

She switched the open channel off.

She did not switch it back on for the rest of it. She did not hear the rest of it. She saw it, in pieces, by the light of the Proctors' lamps: Oyelaran's crane coming round out of the dark on its long boom with its claws open, and a Proctor in the claws, and the claws closing; and a Proctor's gun firing, real rounds now, not stingers, little bright flashes in the black, and a rigger from somewhere jerking on a line; and Haddad, somewhere, on a line by the governor, with her tablet lit again, doing something, so that all at once every one of the Proctors' helmet lamps went out together, and they were in the dark too.

And Bracken.

He was at the dog. He had been at the dog the whole time. He was holding on to its head with both hands and his boots locked to the cuff's face, his great dark shape against the faint gold of the governor's teeth, because if they were going to pull the dog they were going to have to go through him first; and a Proctor came at him out of the dark, along the arm, with his gun lamp lit, the only light in that part of the Barn, and Bracken did not let go of the dog. He did not move at all. Sol saw it from across the Barn. She saw the Proctor stop on the arm a meter from him and put the gun's muzzle against Bracken's visor, and saw Bracken look at it, and not let go. And then the flash.

She did not hear it. The channel was off. She saw Bracken's head go back, and his visor go white, all at once, frost blooming across it from a small black hole, and saw his hands stay on the dog's head. They stayed there. His boots stayed locked. He hung there on the cuff's face with his hands on the dog and his head back and his visor white, and did not let go.

The Proctor reached past him for the dog's head.

And Pell hit him.

She did not know where Pell had come from. He came out of the dark along the arm on his line one-handed, with his burned arm bound across his chest and a driver in his good hand, one of the big drivers, a meter and a half of steel, and he swung it the way you swing a flail at a harvest, the way his father must have taught him in the pear orchards on Tamsin's Reach, a long flat arc from the shoulder with his whole weight behind it; and it hit the Proctor's helmet from the side and the Proctor went off the arm and away into the dark, spinning, his gun gone, his line paying out behind him.

Pell hung on the arm with the driver. He looked at Bracken. Sol saw his face, lit by the dead Proctor's gun lamp floating past, through his visor. He was nineteen. He looked about ten.

Then the Proctors were going.

She did not know why, at first. Then she switched the channel back on, and heard Lusk's voice on it, flat and clipped, saying *withdraw, withdraw, all units withdraw*, and saw their lights, the ones that were still lit, going back across the Barn toward the hatch, fast, hand over hand on their lines. Seven lights. There had been twelve. And outside, on her board, the boat was pulling away from the tower's flank; and the *Patience* was still there. Her number two arm was gone and her number four arm was hanging by its cables and there were holes in her hull you could see through, and she was still there, between the boat and the tower, still.

"Juno," said Sol.

"I'm here." Juno's voice was very precise. "She's holding air. Just. She's a good old girl."

"Juno, you did—"

"Don't," said Juno. "Don't say it. I'll fly her home. Don't say anything."

* * *

Castellane called them two hours later.

They were still in the Barn. They had not left. They had taken Bracken's hands off the dog, finally, one finger at a time, because they would not come off any other way; and Oyelaran and Pell had taken him on a line to the rigging hatch and held him there, at its edge, looking out, while Sol said the Count over him, all of it, from *one for the hook* to *home we go*; and then they had let him go. Out. The way Kostya had gone. They had not had to do anything; the Spindle's spin did it. He went out from the hatch slowly, turning, with his visor white, toward Kettle, and they watched him until they could not tell him from the dark. There had been three riggers dead in the Barn besides him, from the Proctors' guns. Two from Gang Nine. One from Eleven, the boy who had said *that's mutiny* in the ballast hold. Oyelaran had sent them out too.

And there were five Proctors who had not gone back to the boat. Sol had not counted them. She did not want to.

"Chief Dace," said Castellane, on the channel.

"I'm here."

"Mr. Lind has done the figures again." The Director's voice was very low. "He's asked me to read you his terms. I'd like to say first that they're his terms and not mine, and I know that doesn't matter to you, and I'd like to say it anyway."

"Read them."

He read them.

The *Assessor* would dock at the platform on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Seven, at the start of second shift. Every bonded worker on the platform would board her. She would stand off to a thousand kilometers, beyond any reach of first light, and wait there until it had passed. Then she would return them to the platform. Nobody would be left behind.

The clock would not be stopped. First light would occur as scheduled, on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Eight, at the fourth hour of first shift.

Every bonded worker party to the mutiny of Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Four—"which is," said Castellane, and stopped, and started again, "which Mr. Lind takes to be all of them"—would have their bond extended, for breach, to the term of their natural life. In the service of the Combine. At the platform. Which would need, after the Lighting, a permanent crew.

And the dog would be removed. But not before the *Assessor* had undocked with the workers aboard and was clear. And since the Combine could not verify the removal of a mechanical pin from a distance—since Dr. Haddad's instruments could tell them the governor was breathing, but not that it would go on breathing, not that someone had not put the dog back after the *Assessor* had gone—one person would remain at the Spindle. Until first light. To remove the dog, and to be seen to have removed it, and to ensure it was not replaced. Under observation. As surety.

"Mr. Lind has asked that it be you," said Castellane. "Chief Dace. Since you put it in."

The Barn was very quiet.

"And after?" said Sol.

"After first light, the Combine will send a boat to the Spindle to retrieve you. As soon as it's safe."

"I'll be at the Spindle at first light."

"The Inner Housing," said Castellane. "It's four meters of steel. It's the most shielded structure on the Ring. Dr. Haddad—" He stopped. "Dr. Haddad will tell you."

Sol turned her head. Haddad was on a line beside her, by the dog, with her tablet dark on her arm. Her visor was turned toward Sol. Behind it her narrow face was quite still.

"Forty percent," said Haddad. "Maybe. If the Housing's seals hold. If the front's what I think it is. If you're in the middle of it, in the dark, and you don't move." She did not look away. "Maybe forty."

Sol looked at the dog's head, standing out of the cuff, where Bracken's hands had been.

She thought about the ballast hold, two hundred faces in the lamplight, and Teague saying *you don't break, once you've started you don't break, they'll promise you anything to make you break and then they'll hang you.* She thought about the term of their natural life, which was not what Teague had meant by hanging and was not so very different from it either. In the service of the Combine. At the platform. She thought about Juno's son, Tibor, nine, on Juno's bond; and Pell's sisters, crossing off days on a kitchen wall on Tamsin's Reach. She thought about four hundred people alive on a platform for the rest of their lives, and four hundred people dead on it in eighty-one hours. She did the sum. It came out the same every time. Haddad had said that. It did not help.

"Is there a condition?" she said. "Can I have one?"

"You can ask," said Castellane.

"Dr. Haddad goes with the workers. On the *Assessor*. She reads the governor. Her instruments. Not your engineers. Her." She looked at Haddad. "If she says the dog's out, it's out. And nobody comes near the Spindle till she says so."

The channel hissed. She heard, very faintly, voices, behind Castellane, away from his pickup. A dry soft one.

"Agreed," said Castellane.

Sol let out her breath.

"Then tell Mr. Lind," she said, "yes."

# Chapter 17: Firstlight Eve

*The Concord · 336 AL*

On the afternoon of Firstlight Eve the whole city smelled of lamp oil.

It always did. Every household in Tithe that could afford it bought a lamp for Firstlight, a little brass storybook lamp like the one in the Founder's hand in Basilica Square, and filled it, and set it in a window to be lit at the twelfth bell, when the old year ended; and for days before, the lamp-sellers went through the Spokes with their carts, and the oil-sellers after them, and the smell of it got into everything, into the corridors and the lifts and your clothes and your hair, sweet and thick and a little rancid. Idris had loved it as a child. He had stood on a chair on the forty-first level with his father's hand on his shoulder, and Lark in his mother's arms, very small, and watched his father light their lamp at the twelfth bell, and all across the Spokes, all at once, ten thousand little flames had come up in ten thousand windows, and his father had said *there*, very quietly, *there's the Light*, as though he had made it himself.

He went down to the Sump through the smell of lamp oil with a folded paper inside his habit, against his chest. It was the transit order. The Squadron's, lane by lane and ship by ship, forty-one hulls, nine abreast in the van, the times of each, written out in his own small square hand from the copy that had been given to the four Keepers of the Firstlight rota that morning in the chapter-house, under seal. It was what he had promised Dagny Shrike. He felt it against his chest all the way down the service stair like a hot coal.

C-14 was full. It had been full every night that week, and tonight it was fuller than ever, people sitting shoulder to shoulder on the floor and standing against the walls and crowding the doorway, forty of them, fifty, so many that the air was thick and warm and the lamps on the floor guttered. They had their bags with them, some of them. Their children. The Unlevied were moving tonight, the ones who could; Teodor had arranged it, a dozen barges and Dagny's *Patience* and anything else that would fly, out to Gall and Brine before the lanes shut at the twelfth bell. Benno was to be on the *Patience*. Benno, and anybody else Dagny could carry.

Idris gave Teodor the paper. Teodor unfolded it and read it in the lamplight, his lips moving slightly, and then folded it again and put it inside his coat.

"She'll have it in an hour," he said. "Thank you, Keeper."

"Don't."

"All right." Teodor looked at him with something that was almost a smile. "I won't."

Lark was under the marks. She was always under the marks. She looked up at him as he came over, and moved her bag so he could sit, and he sat, with his back against the old grey wall. Mother Rook was there, too, which she had not been before. Someone had carried her down from her room in her chair, and she sat in it near the port, small as a child in her shawls, with her black eyes moving over everything.

"You're going to the pens tonight," Lark said. "For the blessing."

"At the tenth bell."

"And Benno comes out in a habit."

"If it works."

"It'll work." She said it as though she could make it true by saying it. Then she looked at him, sidelong, and her eyebrows met in the middle. "What else?"

"What?"

"There's something else. You've had a face on you for six days. Since you went up the gallery that night." She lowered her voice. "Teodor told me. He said you came down after and wouldn't say anything, and sat in the corner like you'd seen a ghost. What did you see?"

He looked at her. Then he looked across the room at Teodor, who was talking to the old docker with the hook, and at Mother Rook, who was looking straight back at him.

"I took the grille off the Knot," he said.

Lark went very still.

He told her. Quietly, with his back to the wall and his head close to hers, under the noise of the room. The marks on the steel; the wear round the socket. The three witness lines. The ten bolts in a star. The glossary in the old manual. *Dog: a locking pin.* The gallery window on his left and Hearth in it, and Kettle under his feet. Sallow at the end of the gallery in his nightclothes, saying *that is the way that you lock the door*.

When he finished she did not say anything for a long time.

"A lock," she said finally.

"I think so."

"On the Eye."

"I think so."

"A lock that does what? Shuts it?"

"I don't know." He looked down at his hands. "I think so. I think somebody built a way to shut the Eye. A long time ago. Before the Concord. And hid it in a song, and put a grille round it and a verse over it saying never to touch it, so nobody would." He turned his hands over. "I don't know why. I don't know who. I don't know what happens if you do it, or if it can be undone, or if it even still works after three hundred years. I don't know anything."

"If you shut it," said Lark slowly, "tomorrow. With the Squadron coming through."

"Then the Squadron doesn't come through."

"And the Instrument—"

"Is a piece of paper with nobody to enforce it." He made himself say the rest. "And the city's dark. Lark. Every phase-tap in Tithe draws off the Eye. The lungs. The lifts. The pumps. The lights. If the Eye's shut there's no power, nowhere, except what's in the cells, and the cells are for emergencies, they last weeks, not—" He stopped. "Nine million people. And the moons. In the dark. With no ships. No Inward. No medicine, no parts, nothing, ever again, unless somebody opens it. And nobody would know how."

"Forever?"

"I don't know."

Lark looked at the lamps on the floor.

"*Count it back down*," she said, very softly, "*and you're home once more*."

He stared at her.

"It's in the song," she said. "Isn't it. If it's instructions. The lock's the Door verse. And then *count it back down*." She looked at him. "That's how you open it again. You do it backward."

"Maybe."

"Maybe." She was quiet. Then she said, in a voice so low he barely heard it: "Do it."

"Lark—"

"I mean it. If it's real. If you can. Do it." Her eyes were very bright. "I don't care about the dark. The Sump's been in the dark for three hundred years. We know how to live in the dark. We know how to make lamps out of fish oil and grow chicory in a vat and fix a lung with wire. You know who doesn't? Them." She jerked her head upward, at the ceiling, at the Spokes and the Hub and the Basilica and the Factor's House, all of it, above. "Together with their issue. Do it, Idris."

Before he could answer, someone touched his sleeve.

It was Mother Rook. He had not seen her move; someone must have wheeled her over, or she had got out of her chair, he did not know how. She was beside him, small and folded in her shawls, and she had something in her lap, in her knuckled hands.

The flask.

"Here," she said, and held it out.

He did not take it. "Mother—"

"Take it." Her gravel voice did not rise. "I told you. It goes where it's wanted. When somebody's in trouble it goes to them for a while." She pushed it at him, against his chest, where the transit order had been. "You're in trouble, Keeper. I've never seen a man in so much trouble. Take it."

He took it. It was heavy, and cold, and smooth under his fingers, and the dent in its shoulder fit his thumb.

"And when you're done with it," said Mother Rook, and her black eyes held his, "take it back where it came from."

"Where did it come from?"

"How should I know?" She sat back. "Somewhere up. It's always wanted to go up. Every time I've held it. You'll know." She looked at him a moment longer, and then something in her ancient folded face moved, and it was a smile, the first he had seen on it. "You've got her eyebrows," she said, nodding at Lark. "Or she's got yours. Go on, now. Put it away."

He put it inside his habit, against his chest.

And the door blew in.

* * *

He did not hear the charge. He felt it: a hard flat push of air across the room that knocked the lamps over and threw the people nearest the door off their feet, and then the noise came after it, so loud it was not a noise but a blow, and then the dark, because the lamps were out, all but one, rolling on its side on the floor, its flame streaming. And in the doorway, in the smoke, lights. Hard white lights, the kind you strap to a gun. Many of them. Coming in.

People were screaming. He was on the floor. He did not remember falling. Lark was on top of him, or he was on top of her, he could not tell. The lights swept across the room, across faces, across the marks on the wall, and men came in behind them, in armor, dark, faceless, helmeted, with long weapons; and behind them, in white and gold, the Wardens' Guard, and a voice he knew, a woman's, hard and clear, shouting *down, down, everybody down, hands where we can see them*. Captain Varro of the Guard. He had seen her on Levy Day, on the Basilica steps.

Somebody did not get down.

It was Teodor. Idris saw him in the sweep of a gun light, on his feet in the middle of the room, with something in his hand—a knife, a docker's hook, Idris never knew—and his scarred face perfectly calm, and he went forward at the nearest armored figure as though it were a door he meant to walk through. The guns fired. Not stingers. Not anything soft. Flat hard cracks in the small room, three of them, four, and Teodor stopped going forward and stood still for a moment, quite upright, as though he had forgotten something; and then sat down on the floor, and lay down.

Someone fired again. At nothing. At the dark.

Mother Rook was in her chair by the port. She had not moved. She had not got down. She was sitting very straight with her hands in her lap, looking at the door, at the lights, at the armored men, with her black eyes perfectly steady; and a round came through the smoke from somewhere, from no one, aimed at nothing, and hit her in the chest. She rocked back in the chair. She did not make a sound. Her hands came up off her lap a little, and settled again.

Idris was crawling to her. He did not know he was doing it. Somebody stepped on his hand and he did not feel it. He reached her chair and got up on his knees beside it and took her hands in his, and they were very small and very cold, all knuckle.

She looked at him. Her black eyes were still bright. She opened her mouth and he leaned close to hear.

"Up," said Mother Rook. "Take it up."

And then the eyes were not bright any more.

He was still kneeling there holding her hands when they took him. Two of them, armored, faceless, one at each arm, lifting him up off his knees as though he weighed nothing. He did not fight them. He saw, as they turned him toward the door, Lark on the floor with a Guard's knee in her back and her face against the deck, looking at him, her mouth open, shouting something he could not hear. He saw Teodor lying still by the wall. He saw the marks on the wall in the gun lights, row on row, and the one at the bottom with the deep cut under it.

And he saw, in the doorway, behind the armored men and the white-and-gold of the Guard, out in the corridor in the smoke, a large soft figure in the brown coat of a senior clerk of the Concord, with a round pink face and very small pale eyes, standing well back, out of the way, with a handkerchief pressed to his nose against the smoke. Watching.

Ferrant Oake met his eyes. He lowered the handkerchief a little. He looked, Idris thought, almost apologetic.

Then they took Idris out past him into the corridor, and up.

* * *

The Factor's House was very quiet, and very beautiful, and smelled of lamp oil like everywhere else.

They took him up by a lift he had never seen, that went straight from the Hub's lowest level to its highest without stopping, with his arms held and a hood over his head, and when they took the hood off he was standing in a long room with a floor of black stone polished so bright that he could see himself in it, upside down, small, grey, with blood on his hands. Mother Rook's blood. And Teodor's, maybe. He did not know. There were windows down one side of the room, tall ones, looking out over the city, and the city was beginning to light its lamps.

Factor Severs was at the far end of the room, at a table, with a slate. Prelate Marsh was beside him, in a chair, in her white and gold, with her pleasant motherly face. And standing a little apart, by the windows, with his hands folded in front of his brown coat, Oake.

"Keeper-Adjunct Vey," said Severs. He did not get up. His voice was light and cool and very clear, with the long vowels of the Inward. "Please. Come closer. You can let go of him."

They let go of him. He did not come closer. He stood where he was on the black stone.

"I'll be brief," said Severs. "It's Firstlight Eve, and I'm sure we all have somewhere we'd rather be." He looked down at his slate. "You falsified an entry in the Transit Register on the thirty-seventh day before Firstlight. The *Clemency*. In exchange for a reconciliation in the Levy, removing your sister's name. Mr. Oake has very kindly brought this to my attention, along with the names of the two other Keepers who did the same, and the reconciliations they were paid in." He did not look at Oake. "Mr. Oake is to be Assessor of the Third Spoke, under the new administration. He has a gift for lists."

Oake inclined his head.

"You've since associated yourself with a body of persons calling themselves the Unlevied," Severs went on, "who have for some decades been removing Levy-drawn persons from the Concord's custody. You were present at their meeting this evening. You gave them this." He held up a folded paper between two fingers. Idris's own transit order, in his own small square hand. "Which is, I think you'll agree, a great deal more than a number in a book."

Idris said nothing.

"The persons arrested this evening," said Severs, "are to be added to the Levy. All of them. They'll sail tomorrow on the *Remittance*, at Firstlight, with the rest. Inward. As it happens, the Levy's the last one; after tomorrow there won't be a Levy. So I'm afraid they'll be the last people ever to go Inward on twenty-year bonds. Rather than the other kind." He glanced at the Prelate, who smiled. "Your sister is among them. So is a boy called Kask, whom I gather you planned to remove from the pens this evening in a novice's habit." He set the transit order down on the table. "That's not going to happen either."

"What do you want?" said Idris.

"I want the Light to hold." Severs looked at him for the first time directly, with his pale narrow face perfectly composed. "Tomorrow at Firstlight forty-one ships of the House will transit the Eye, and the Eye will need to be tuned by hand at four quarters by the four best Keepers in the order, as it was for the colony fleets. Your Archkeeper tells me you're one of the four. He tells me you're the best of them, in fact, after himself. He was very insistent. He came to see me an hour ago." Severs's mouth moved very slightly. "He's fond of you."

"And if I don't?"

"Then someone less good will tune the north quarter. And perhaps the Eye will hold, and perhaps it won't, and if it doesn't, some of my ships will be lost, and I shall be very angry. And your sister will not sail Inward on a Levy bond tomorrow. She'll hang in Basilica Square tomorrow, beside the man Gaunt's body and the other leaders of the Unlevied, where the city can see them on the first morning of the new administration." He said it in exactly the same voice. "You understand. I'm not threatening you, Keeper. I'm describing the figures."

The Prelate, in her chair, folded her hands in her lap.

"It's a very small thing we're asking, Keeper Vey," she said, in her motherly voice. "Only what you've always done. Keep the Light."

Idris looked at them. At the slate in Severs's hand, and the transit order on the table, and the Prelate's folded hands. At Oake by the window, with the city lighting its lamps behind him, ward by ward, ten thousand little flames.

He thought about the gallery at dawn, his palm on the brass. *One for the hook and two for the line.* He thought about Mother Rook's hands in his, and their coldness. About the flask, against his chest, under his habit; nobody had searched him for it, nobody had thought to. *Take it up.*

"I'll keep it," he said.

"Good," said Severs, and looked down at his slate again. "You'll be escorted to the gallery at the sixth bell. Two of my people will stand with you at the north quarter for the duration. They're not engineers, so you needn't worry about their getting in the way. They're simply there to see that you do what you're there to do." He touched the slate. "Oh, and you'll keep the Register for the transit, too. The Squadron's masses. We'll want them recorded accurately this time." He did not smile. "I'm told you have a steady hand."

* * *

They put him in a penitent's cell in the chapter-house, at the foot of the Spindle, a room he had not known the Keepers still used: three paces by two, stone, with a cot and a bucket and a window the width of his hand looking out at the Eye. Two of the House's armored men stood outside the door. He heard them shift, sometimes, and the small sounds their armor made.

At the twelfth bell the city lit its lamps.

He saw it through the slot of a window. Not the Spokes, which were behind him; but the reflection of them, in the Eye. The Eye was a mirror, at night, if you looked at it from the right angle; and at the twelfth bell, in its slow-breathing blue-white face, ten thousand little points of gold came up, all at once, reflected, scattered across the light like seeds. His father's voice, very quietly: *there's the Light.*

The door opened at the second bell after.

It was Sallow. The armored men let him in, and shut the door behind him, and he stood just inside it leaning on his black stick, in his vestments—the full vestments of the Archkeeper, the grey and silver, with the great hook worked on the breast and his old furred line round his neck—and looked at Idris sitting on the cot. He did not say anything for a long time.

"They killed the old woman," said Idris. "Rook. They killed Teodor Gaunt. They've got Lark. She's going Inward tomorrow on the *Remittance*. She and Benno and all of them."

"I know." Sallow's voice was very quiet. "I went to the Factor's House. I asked for her. I knelt, Idris. On his black floor. I haven't knelt to anyone but the Light in fifty-six years." He shook his head slowly. "He was very polite."

"He told me you said I was the best."

"You are the best." The old man came across the cell, slowly, leaning on the stick, and lowered himself onto the end of the cot beside him, with a long breath. "While I was waiting to be let in I saw his manifest, you know. On his table. For the Squadron. He didn't hide it; why would he. Forty-one hulls. And in the fourth rank, three ships of engineers. *For the reconstitution of the Keepers*, it said. Under the House." He looked down at his hands on the head of the stick. "We're to be assessed too. All of us. Every Keeper in the order. Bonds of the House, under House engineers, to keep the Light for the House's ships. Every novice. Tobiah. You. Me." He was quiet. "I asked him whether that was in the Instrument. He said *every person*, Archkeeper. *Of whatever age or condition.* He said he'd thought I'd read it."

Idris said nothing.

"I've served the Light all my life," said Sallow. "I told you it doesn't care who owns it. That it breathes, and that's all, and we keep it breathing. I believed that." He lifted his head and looked out of the narrow window at the Eye, with the city's lamps scattered across its face. "I think I believed it because it meant I didn't have to care either."

"What's the Light, Archkeeper?" said Idris.

The old man was quiet for a long time.

"A door," he said at last. "That's all. That's all it's ever been. A door. And for three hundred and thirty-six years I've told myself that a door doesn't care who comes through it." He turned the stick in his hands. "But doors are made by somebody. And somebody decides who holds the key." He looked at Idris. "Somebody put a lock on this one, my boy. Before the Concord. Before any of us. And then hid the key in a song, and gave the song to children, and cut a warning over it so that the people who thought they owned the door wouldn't go near it." He reached inside his vestments. "I've thought about what you said. Up there, in the gallery. All week. Holds it up or holds it down."

He drew something out from under the grey and silver. It was long, and heavy, and wrapped in a strip of altar cloth, and he laid it across Idris's knees.

Idris unwrapped it.

It was a driver. A great old driver, a meter and a half of dark steel, with a long shaft worn smooth and bright by hands, and a crossbar at one end for leverage, and at the other a squared head, heavy, blunt. He knew it. Every Keeper knew it. It had hung over the altar of the chapter-house chapel for three hundred years, on two brass hooks, under a lamp, and was carried in procession once a year on Firstlight morning; and it was called, in the Book of the Lighting, *the Founders' Wrench*, and it was said to be the tool with which the Founders had raised the Ring.

"Nobody's ever known what it's for," said Sallow. "It's been on those hooks since before the order. The first Keepers found it in the Spindle, they say, the same as the Knot. In the gallery. Lying on the floor under the grille." He looked at the squared head. "I measured it, after you went home that night. With calipers. Against the socket in the Knot. I took the grille off myself and put it back after, with my own lead." He almost smiled. "It fits, Idris. Exactly. To a hair. And the bolt-heads on the plate. Under the paint. I measured them too."

Idris sat with the Founders' Wrench across his knees.

"I'll carry it in procession in the morning," said Sallow. "As I always do. Up the Keepers' Spoke and into the gallery, before the transit, with the four of you behind me and the Prelate and the Factor watching from the high gallery and his men all round. And I'll lay it on the gallery floor, under the grille, at the north quarter, where they say it was found. Where you'll be standing." He put his dry light hand on Idris's wrist. "And then I'll go to the east quarter and tune the governor. And when you need them not to be looking at you—the two men he'll put beside you—you tell me. On the Keepers' channel. The old words. *The light holds.* And I'll make them look at me."

"How?"

"I'm an old man," said Archkeeper Sallow, "in the full vestments of the Light, with a stick. I'll think of something." He took his hand back. "I don't know what's behind that plate, Idris.
