I've told you that. It might be nothing. It might be the end of the world. But I know what's coming through the Eye in the morning. And I find I'd rather it was the end of the world than that." He began, slowly, to get up off the cot. "Wrap it up again. I'll take it with me. They'd find it on you."

Idris wrapped it in the altar cloth and gave it back to him. Sallow put it under his vestments. Then he stood a moment in the narrow cell, leaning on his stick, looking down at Idris.

"I taught you the invocation," he said. "Do you remember? You were fifteen. In a habit too long for you. I put your hand on the grille and said the words and you said them after me, and then you asked me what they meant." He smiled, and the smile was very sad. "And I looked at you so that you never asked anyone again. I'm sorry for that. I've been sorry for it for nineteen years." He turned to the door. "Get some sleep. You'll want a steady hand."

He knocked on the door with his stick, and the armored men opened it, and he went out.

Idris lay down on the cot. He did not sleep. He lay on his side with the flask inside his habit against his chest, cold, heavy, the dent in its shoulder under his palm, and looked through the slot of the window at the Eye, breathing, with the city's lamps scattered across its face; and toward morning, when the lamps had burned low and gone out one by one, he saw a ship move across it, slow and huge and lit along its whole length, from the Hub docks toward the Third Spoke lane.

The *Remittance*, taking up her station.

He watched her until the window's slot cut her off. Then he lay and listened to the governor hum through the stone under him, and counted its breaths, one, and two, and three, until the armored men opened the door and said it was the sixth bell, and time.

# Chapter 18: The Halo

*The Dark · Year 419*

I had thought outside would be dark. It is not dark. It is the brightest place I have ever been.

There is no air outside to soften anything, Ness told me, so everything is as bright as it is and no brighter, and the shadows are as black as they are and no less: and so you walk on the Halo in a world cut in two with a knife. Where Kettle's light falls, the dead metal of the ring shines purple and oxblood and that slow sick yellow, every seam and pit and scar on it sharp as if it had been drawn with a pen, and the gold lines laced across it glint dully; and where Kettle's light does not fall, there is nothing. Not darkness. Nothing. You put your boot down into a shadow and your boot is gone. Your leg is gone to the knee. You have to trust that the metal is still there.

And above you—I did not look above me, the first day. Ness had told me not to. *Don't look up, not till you've got your feet. Look at my back. Look at your boots. Look at the metal.* So I looked at her back, and my boots, and the metal. But you cannot help seeing, at the edges. Kettle filling half of everything, turning, with its storms. The stars filling the other half, so many and so bright and so close that they did not look like lights; they looked like holes, as though the Dark were a cloth with the Light behind it, and someone had pricked it all over with a needle. And the Halo itself going away in front of us and behind us, curving, a road of black metal thirty kilometers long to the Spindle and on round beyond it, going up, and up, so that far ahead of us it rose like a wall across the stars, and went over the top of the sky, and came down again behind us, and we were walking along the inside of a wheel so big you could not see it was a wheel.

I was sick in my helmet on the first day. Twice. Ness talked me through cleaning it, on the private channel, very calm, the way she must have talked Barrow's boy through his first walk. Nobody else said anything. Walkers do not.

We walked in a line. Eighteen of us, at the start: the Reeve, and Marrow, and four soldiers, and ten walkers, and Brother Cinder, and me. The eleventh walker stayed with the *Patience*, with a leg the crash had broken, and the ship's radio, and twenty days of air. Ness in front of me and a big silent walker from Brine named Hollis behind. Clipped to a line, all of us, a long thin cord that ran from the first walker to the last, so that if anyone's boots lost the metal the line would hold them. Three sledges, dragged on lines by the walkers, with air and water and food and the Reeve's tools, and on the last sledge, wrapped in canvas, a black box the size of a man's chest with a long folded spike on top of it, which Marrow said was the relay. *So that every Hold in Tithe hears it*, the Reeve had said, in the hold of the *Patience*, before we went out. *When I open it. So they know who.* We walked slowly. Small and slow. One boot, and the click of the magnets taking hold, and the other boot, and the click. A kilometer an hour, on the good stretches. Less on the bad.

And all the time, through my boots, the knock.

It never stopped, out there. It was louder every hour. Not in my ears—there was nothing to carry it to my ears, out in the Dark—but in my feet, and my legs, and the floor of my belly, and my teeth when I clenched them. *Knock-knock knock-knock. Knock. Knock-knock.* A run and a stop. I walked to it. I could not help it. After the first day everybody did. You could see it in the line, if you looked back: eighteen people in patched suits on the black metal, putting their boots down on the beat.

* * *

We slept, the first night, in the lee of a thing on the Halo's face that might have been a machine once. A great square housing of dead metal the size of a Hold's dock, frozen to the ring, with a door in its side rusted open. The Reeve's soldiers went in first with their lamps and came out and said it was empty, and we went in, all of us, and sat against its inside walls in the dark with our helmets on and our suits' little heaters ticking, and ate through the feeds, and did not talk.

I could not sleep. Nobody can sleep the first night out, Ness said. She was beside me with her shoulder against mine, the way she always was, and she slept anyway, almost at once, with her head tipped back against the wall and her breath slow in my private channel. I listened to it. And then, because I could not sleep, and because I was a Tally and a Tally keeps things, I began to go through the channels on my suit's wrist, one by one, the way Ness had shown me on the *Patience*, to learn them.

Walkers' channel: someone snoring. Private channel: Ness breathing. Soldiers' channel: nothing.

The fourth channel was the command channel. I should not have been able to get it; my suit was a walker's suit, an old one, and nobody had thought to lock it out. It was the Reeve.

"—told you before," he was saying, very low. "I'm not going to say it again."

"She's a child," said Marrow's voice.

"She's nineteen."

"She's a child." A pause. "And the walker's nothing. The walker's a hullwalker from a dying Hold who came for a tank of water."

"The walker," said the Reeve, "has been sitting next to the Tally for three days with her shoulder against her. Every time we stop. You've seen them. The Tally doesn't look at anybody else." I heard him breathe; the rot was bad in him, out here, in the cold. "The Tally's taught her. I'd bet the *Patience* on it. Tallies teach. That's what they do. That's the whole of what they are."

Marrow did not answer.

"I need the Count," said the Reeve of Brine. "I need it once. At the Spindle. In the Tally's mouth, with my hand on whatever it is she says to put my hand on. And then I need it never to be sung again by anybody who isn't me." His voice did not rise. "That's what a key is, Marrow. A key you can copy isn't a key. It's a rumor. If I open that door and come back across this ring to Brine with every hullwalker and Tally and Lamplighter in Tithe knowing how to close it again, or open it again, or open the next one—then I'm not holding anything. I'm just the first man through."

"So."

"So the Tally and the walker don't come back across the Halo."

The channel was silent for a long time.

"I won't do it," said Marrow.

"I didn't ask you to." The Reeve coughed, quietly, for a long time. "Go to sleep. It's a long walk."

The channel clicked. I sat in the dark with my back against the dead metal and Ness's breathing in my ear and my hands in my lap, and the stone in the middle of me, that had been there since Ness knelt in front of me in the Reeve's Hall with the tape in her teeth, did not get any heavier. It did not need to. It was as heavy as it could be already. I only understood, sitting there, that I had known. That I had known since the phase-ear, since *whoever owns the door owns what comes through it*, and had not let myself say it, the way Quill had said Veska would not let herself say that the lungs were dying.

I did not wake Ness. I did not tell her, then. I turned my suit back to the private channel and listened to her breathe, and toward morning I slept, and dreamed of Quill, sitting on his bunk under the marks with his blind eyes open, saying: *Nobody knows is not the same as there being no reason.*

* * *

We came into the dogs' country on the fourth day.

You could tell. Not because you saw them, at first; because you saw what they had done. The Halo's face changed. For three days it had been dead and pitted and scarred, but whole; now, as we came round the curve and the Spindle grew ahead of us out of a needle into a tower, black and gold, standing up off the rim against Kettle with the band of glass round its waist, the metal under our boots began to be cut. Long straight scars, bright-edged, burned into the dead gold. Hundreds of them, crossing and recrossing, like the lines a child draws on a slate. And between them, here and there, things that had been burned too. A piece of hull. A sledge, black. A boot, with the magnet still holding it to the ring. A suit.

There were people in some of the suits. You did not look. You looked at the back of the walker in front of you, and your boots, and the metal.

"Twenty kilometers," said the Reeve, on the walkers' channel. "From here in, they can see us. Smaller. Slower. If you see one, stop. Don't run. They see running. Stop, and get down flat, and wait."

We went smaller and slower. We went so slowly that the Spindle did not seem to grow at all. The knock came up through the metal, louder, louder, *knock-knock knock-knock*, until I could feel it in my eyes.

The first dog we saw was dead. It was squatting on the Halo's face a hundred meters to our left, in the full purple glare of Kettle, and it did not move. It was the size of a sledge. It had six legs, long and jointed, folded under it, and a body like a flat black stone, and on the front of the body a cluster of glass eyes, and above them, on a stalk, a short black tube. Its legs were rusted to the metal. Its eyes were cracked. It had been squatting there, Ness said, for a hundred years, or four hundred; there was no way to know.

The second dog we saw was not dead.

It was the second day in their country, the fifth of the walk. Hollis saw it first, behind me, and said "Stop," very quietly, on the walkers' channel, and we stopped. All eighteen of us—seventeen by then; one of the soldiers had lost his boots' grip at a seam the day before and gone off the side of the ring before the line could take his weight, and the line had parted at his clip, and we had watched him go, turning, out into the Dark—seventeen of us, stopped on the black metal in the open, in Kettle's glare, with nowhere to hide.

It was ahead and to the right. Two hundred meters. It was coming over a rise in the Halo's face, slowly, on its six long legs, the way an old man walks: lift, and reach, and set down, and the next. Its glass eyes were whole. They were turning. The short black tube on its stalk was turning with them.

"Down," said the Reeve.

We got down. Flat, on the metal, on our bellies, with our visors against the dead gold. I could feel the knock in my cheek through the helmet. I could see the dog sideways, out of the corner of my visor, coming on: lift, reach, set down. Its eyes turned across us. Across the line. Across the sledges. They did not stop. They went on turning, and the dog went on walking, slowly, across our path, a hundred meters ahead, and over another rise, and away.

We lay there a long time after.

"They don't see still," said Ness, on the private channel, very low. "Barrow told me. They don't see still and they don't see cold. They see moving and they see warm." I could hear her breathing. "We're warm, Tally. In the suits. Out here we're the warmest things for a hundred kilometers. They just haven't looked right at us yet."

The third dog looked right at us.

It came out of a shadow. That was why nobody saw it. A long black shadow cast by a ridge of the Halo's plating, where Kettle's light did not reach, where your boot disappeared to the knee; and the dog had been in it, squatting, still, cold, the way the dead one had been, for who knew how long. And we walked past the shadow's mouth, small and slow, one boot and the click, warm in our suits; and its eyes came open.

I saw them open. Red. Little points of red in the black, a cluster of them, very close, twenty meters. And the tube on its stalk turning.

Marrow was between me and it.

I do not know whether she meant to be. I have thought about it since, as I have thought about Cinder throwing the lamp. She was three walkers ahead of me on the line, with Ness between us, and she must have seen the red come up in the shadow's mouth when I did; and she did not get down. She stood up. All the way up, off the metal, tall, in the full glare of Kettle, with the white scar down her face lit purple through her visor, and she unclipped herself from the line, and she ran.

They see running.

The tube found her. A thin bright line of red, from the shadow to her chest, and she kept running for three steps, away from the line, away from us, across the open metal; and then she stopped running and stood still, and there was a small black hole in her suit where the line had been, and a thin white plume coming out of it, boiling away into the Dark. She stood quite still. Then she sat down on the Halo's face, slowly, the way you sit when you are tired, and folded over.

The dog came out of the shadow after her. It walked across the metal on its six long legs, past the end of our line, past Hollis lying flat and Ness lying flat and me lying flat with my visor against the dead gold, and its red eyes did not turn toward us at all. It walked to where Marrow had sat down, and stood over her, and its eyes looked at her, and the tube on its stalk looked at her, for a long time. She was getting cold. You could see it in the way the plume from her suit grew thin and stopped. And then, when she was cold enough, the dog turned away, and walked back into its shadow, and folded its legs, and was still.

Nobody moved for a long time.

Then the Reeve of Brine, at the front of the line, very quietly, on the walkers' channel, said: "On. Small and slow."

And we went on.

* * *

Kettle flared on the sixth day, and Brother Cinder died on the seventh.

The flare saved us. I know that now. The Reeve said it would kill us, when the dosimeters started to chirp, and it nearly did; we lay in a phase-trench in the Halo's face for nine hours, a channel cut in the metal for some purpose nobody knew, a meter deep and a meter wide and a hundred meters long, all of us in a row in the bottom of it like the dead in a grave, while Kettle's white place spread across its face over our heads and the whole Halo ticked and sang around us and our suits chirped and chirped. Two of the walkers died in the trench. Not of the flare. Their suits' heaters failed, in the cold at the bottom of the channel, and they got cold, and they went to sleep, and did not wake. One of them was Hollis.

But when the flare passed, and we came up out of the trench, the dogs were gone.

Not gone. Still. All of them. We could see them, now that we were close: dozens, scattered across the Halo's face around the Spindle's foot, the nearest a kilometer off, the farthest small as beetles. And none of them was moving. They were squatting where the flare had found them, with their legs folded and their eyes dark.

"It blinds them," said Brother Cinder.

He was sitting up in the trench, at the end of the row. He had not spoken in two days. He had walked behind me the whole way with his raw wrists in his suit's gloves and his chain left in a heap on the floor of the *Patience*'s hold, and he had kept up, somehow, thin as he was, and he had not said anything; and now he sat at the end of the trench with his visor turned toward the Spindle, and his voice on the walkers' channel was soft and hoarse and quite calm.

"The Wick has a book," he said. "Of the Keepers' machines. What's left of it. The dogs are old. Older than the Dark. They were made to guard the Spindle and they have no one to tell them to stop, so they don't. But they were made in the Light, when Kettle-weather was something you sheltered from behind the Mantle, and nobody thought to make them stand it. A big flare blinds them. For a day, the book says. Sometimes two. Until their eyes heal." He turned his visor toward me. "We should go now."

We went. Faster, now. Not small and slow. Fourteen of us. Across the open metal toward the Spindle's foot, past the squatting dogs with their dark eyes, close enough to one of them, once, that I could have reached out and touched its folded leg; and it did not move. The Spindle went up in front of us. Up and up. It filled the whole of the sky ahead, a wall of black metal laced with dead gold, going up out of the top of my visor to the band of glass round its waist, nine hundred meters, and beyond.

At the foot of it, there was a door.

And around the door, a ring of dogs.

Six of them. Squatting on the Halo's face in a half-circle round the door's mouth, a hundred meters out from it, facing outward. Their eyes were dark, like the others. But as we came across the last open stretch toward them, one of them moved.

Only a little. One leg, unfolding. Lifting, and reaching, and setting down. Its eyes did not open. But the tube on its stalk turned, slowly, toward us.

"They're waking," said Brother Cinder.

We stopped.

"These ones are closer to the Spindle," he said. "Under its shadow. They took less of the flare." Another one moved. A leg. Another. "They'll have their eyes back in an hour. Less."

"Then we go through now," said the Reeve. "Before they—"

"They see moving," said Cinder. "They're not blind enough to miss fourteen people walking through the middle of them. Not this close." He was quiet for a moment. "One, maybe."

Then he unclipped himself from the line.

I knew what he was going to do the moment he did it. I think we all did. Ness said "*No*—" on the walkers' channel, and the Reeve said "Cinder," sharp, and I did not say anything at all; I only turned and looked at him, through my visor and his, in Kettle's purple light, standing on the Halo's face a little apart from us with the line's end drifting from his hand.

"I said it doesn't belong to anybody," said Brother Cinder. "In the hold. Do you remember, Tally? When he held out the lamp."

"I remember."

"I've thought about it. All the way here. All the way across." His voice was very soft. "I've believed all my life that the Founders would come home through the Eye with the Light in their hands. I believed it so much I couldn't see anything else. And then I saw the Reeve's face in the phase-ear room, listening. And the bald man's in the hold, holding out the lamp. And yours." He was quiet. "And I thought: whoever's on the other side of that door, they're not going to come home to anybody if all that's waiting on this side is people who want to own it." He reached into the front of his suit, the way the bald man had, and took something out, and held it out to me in his glove. "Here."

It was the key. The Keepers' key, that had opened the hatch on the *Patience*. A plain thing, brass, long, with a bit at one end shaped like nothing I knew.

"It'll open anything the Keepers made," he said. "The Wick has kept them since the Light. I think it'll open that." He nodded at the door in the Spindle's foot. "Take it. Don't give it to him."

I took it.

"What are you going to do?" I said, though I knew.

"Something terrible and beautiful," said Brother Cinder, and I could hear that he was smiling. "Go when they turn."

He turned away from us. He walked out across the Halo's face. Not small, and not slow. He walked the way you would walk across a Hold's dock, upright, at an ordinary pace, with his arms swinging; away from us, away from the door, out into the open metal to the left of the ring of dogs, in the full glare of Kettle. And as he walked he did something to his suit, at the wrist, and its lamps came on. All of them. Every light on it, at once, white, blazing, so that he was a star walking across the black. And then he turned up his suit's heater, I think, too, as high as it would go; Ness said afterward that you could see it, the frost on the metal around his boots boiling away into steam as he walked.

And he began to sing.

It was not the Count. It was a Lamplighter hymn, one I had heard old Geller sing on the ninth level of Hold Fourteen, on the chapel steps, when the lamps were lit: something about the Founders coming home, and the Light in their hands, and every window burning. He sang it on the open channel, on every channel, so loud that it filled my helmet; and his voice was not good, it was thin and hoarse and cracked on the high notes, and it was the most beautiful thing I have ever heard.

The dogs turned.

All six of them. You could see it ripple round the half-circle: legs unfolding, lifting, reaching, setting down; the tubes on their stalks swinging, all of them, away from the door, away from us, toward the blazing singing star walking out across the Halo on the left. Their eyes were opening. Red, in clusters. One, and then another.

"Go," said the Reeve.

We went. Across the last hundred meters, between the dogs, behind their backs, fast, not running—Ness had my arm, and was pulling, and saying *don't run, don't run, walk fast, walk*—with the singing in my helmet and the red lines starting to reach out across the metal to our left, one, and then three, and then all of them, toward the singing, toward the star; and the singing did not stop. It did not stop when the first line touched him. It did not stop when the second did. It went on, thin and cracked and beautiful, *every window burning*, and I did not look, I looked at Ness's back and my boots and the metal, and the door was in front of us; and then the singing stopped.

There was a long silence on the channel. Only the knock, through my boots, in the Spindle's shadow, louder than it had ever been. *Knock-knock knock-knock.*

"Tally," said Ness.

I turned round.

She was standing behind me at the door's mouth with her hand on her side, just under her ribs, on the left. There was a little white plume coming out between her fingers. Very thin. Boiling away. One of the lines had found her, at the end, as she pulled me across—not the whole line, only the edge of it, a graze. Her face behind her visor was very white, and very calm, and she was looking at me.

"It's small," she said. "It's a small one. I've got a patch." She was already getting it out of her thigh pocket with her other hand, a grey square of the stuff walkers carry, and slapping it over the place between her fingers, and pressing, and the plume stopped. "See? Small." She swayed. "I'm fine, Tally. Don't look at me like that. I've had worse than this."

"Open it."

We both turned.

The Reeve of Brine was standing at the door. There were four of us left at the door's mouth, out of the thirteen who had crossed: the Reeve, and Ness, and me, and a soldier, a young one, whose name I did not know yet, with the Reeve's relay strapped to his back in its canvas, because the Reeve had made him cut it off the last sledge before we crossed. The rest were behind us on the open metal, or had stopped to go back to the sledges, or had not made the last hundred meters. I did not know which. I did not look.

The Reeve had a gun in his good hand. He was holding it quite steadily, for a man with the rot in his chest and a hole in his other arm and a seven-day walk behind him. It was pointed at Ness.

"I heard you on the command channel," I said. "The first night. You and Marrow."

"I thought you might have." He did not lower the gun. His voice was tired. "It doesn't change anything, Tally. It's the same as the lung. I'm sorry for it. But it's the same." He nodded at the door, without looking away from Ness. "Cinder gave you something. Before he went. I saw. Open it."

I looked at the door.

It was round, like the lock at the end of the Fourteen corridor. Steel, old, with a wheel on it. Above it, on the Spindle's black flank, there was a sign cut into the metal, a sign I had seen once before, over an iron door in an alley on Brine's Third tier: a hook. Plain, like you would hang a pot from. Or a line. And beside the wheel, a little brass plate with a slot in it.

I took out Brother Cinder's key and put it in the slot, and turned it.

Something inside the door, very deep, moved. I felt it through my gloves, through the key. A long slow grinding, like the inner door of the Fourteen lock sliding home, but backward; the dogs coming out of their seats, all round the rim. And then the wheel was free under my hand. I turned it. Through my boots, through the Halo, through the Spindle's foot, the knock was so loud now it was like a hand beating on my chest.

The door swung inward, into the dark.

"Up," said the Reeve of Brine. "All the way up."

# Chapter 19: Lock the Door

*The Combine · FY 2291*

Dov's letter was in the bottom of Teague's kit bag, under a spare liner and a pair of socks that had been darned so many times they were more darn than sock, in a Company envelope gone soft at the corners. Sol found it on the night of Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Six, sitting on Teague's bunk in Bay Fourteen with the bag open on her knees. Nobody else was in the bay. Pell was in the galley with the others. Juno was wherever Juno went now. Bracken's bunk was stripped. She had stripped it herself.

The envelope was addressed in Dov's hand, which was worse than hers, big and round and leaning backward, like a child's: *To the Engineering Office, Lindqvist Hardware & Fittings, Halvard Four.* There was a stamp on it from the Ostrava docks, and across it, in a Proctor's blue ink, *RETURN TO SENDER*.

She opened it with her scribe.

*Sirs,*

*My name is Dov Dace. I am a rigger on the Combine's transit works at TTH-9 by Kettle. I am writing about your harness clip, the Model Seven, the one with the swaged throat.*

*When the line comes off the clip at an angle, as it does when a man swings out on his line to ride a load, the throat flexes. Not much. You can't see it. But it does it every time, and it is not rated for it, and after enough times the steel inside the throat cracks where you can't see, and the eye pulls through. I have drawn it on the back of this so you can see what I mean. I am not an engineer but I have watched it happen twice and I am sure.*

*I have reported this to the Combine sixteen times. They say it is under review.*

*I am writing to you because you made it and you can make it better. I don't think it would cost much. A thicker throat, or a guard. You would know.*

*There are four hundred and some of us out here hanging off your clips, nine hundred meters up, over a brown dwarf. We don't mind the height. We mind the clips.*

*I have a sister on my gang. She counts the days till she can go home. She has eleven hundred and twenty-seven left, as of this morning. I would like her to get to the end of them.*

*Yours respectfully,*

*Dov Dace, 0418, Gang Four.*

On the back, in pencil, a drawing of a clip. Very careful. Much better than his handwriting. The throat, in cross-section, with little arrows showing where the line pulled and where the steel bent, and a crack drawn in, fine as a hair, where the crack would be.

She sat on Teague's bunk for a long time with the letter in her lap.

Then she folded it up along its creases and put it inside her liner, against her skin, where the sheet with the blue letterhead had been. And she got up, and went to her own bunk, and lay down with her face to the wall and her fingers on the marks, and found the bottom rows, and the line she had cut under Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Eight, deep, so that her fingertip fit inside it.

Two left, before the line. She crossed off one. She left the other.

* * *

The *Assessor* docked at the start of second shift on Day Two Hundred and Eighty-Seven, and the bonds went aboard her one at a time through the dock lock at the head of the spine, past the hatch that Bracken had lifted and that still hung jammed at half a meter with its hydraulics blown, past two Proctors with stinger guns who searched every one of them, and into her holds.

There were four hundred and six of them. Sol stood at the side of the dock lock and watched them go. It took four hours. They went in silence, mostly, carrying what they had been told they could carry, which was one bag each; and some of them looked at her as they passed and some did not, and some touched her arm, and Mother Szell stopped in front of her with her bag in one hand and her frying pan, which nobody had made her give up, in the other, and looked up into Sol's face for a long time, and said, in her voice like a rusty hinge, "They hanged nineteen on the cranes at Pier Six in '64. Teague told you."

"Yes."

"They're not hanging anybody this time," said Mother Szell. "Not even you, maybe. That's something." She looked at Sol a moment longer. "It's not enough. But it's something." And she went on, into the lock.

Oyelaran went. Ruzicka went, and Danek, with his bunkmate's hand on his shoulder, the bunkmate with a patch over one eye. The galley crew went. The air-plant crew. Gang Two and Gang Six and Gang Eleven, what was left of it. Faces she had seen every day for twelve years in the spine and the galley and on the Ring's face behind visors. Faces she did not know at all. The term of their natural life, in the service of the Combine, at the platform. They went into the *Assessor*'s holds to be carried out a thousand kilometers and kept safe and brought back, and they would come back to Bay Fourteen and the ballast hold and the galley and the Ring, and go out on it again tomorrow, or the day after, in Lindqvist clips, and not come home. Not ever. But alive.

Juno went near the end.

She came to the lock with her bag and stopped in front of Sol, and the Proctors waited, and Juno stood very straight and looked at her.

"I'd have flown her home," she said. "The *Patience*. After. If they'd let me."

"I know."

"She's at the dock, on the end berth. She's holding air. Just." Juno's voice was very precise. "Somebody should look after her. After. She's a good old girl."

"I'll tell them."

Juno nodded. She did not move. Then she said, in a smaller voice, "Sol—"

"Go on, Juno."

"Tibor's nine."

"I know."

"I'd do it again," said Juno. "That's the worst of it. I've thought about it every minute since Lusk came on the speakers, and if they came to me tomorrow and said Tibor, I'd do it again." She looked at Sol, and her eyes were dry, and terrible. "I'm sorry. I'm not sorry enough. I'm not going to be."

Sol looked at her for a long time.

"Go and see your boy, Juno," she said. "If they ever let you."

Juno went into the lock.

Pell was last.

He had waited. He had stood at the far side of the dock in his suit with his helmet under his good arm and his burned arm strapped across his chest, and let everyone go past him, all four hundred and five, and when the last of them was through and the Proctors were looking at their slate, he came across the dock and stood in front of her.

She had the flask in her hand. She had brought it from Bay Fourteen. She held it out.

He looked at it.

"Take it," she said. "Fill it with chicory. Every shift. It keeps it hot through the whole shift, even out on the face, even in the dark part. You'll want it hot, in the dark part."

"Chief—"

"Take it, Okafor."

He took it. His big cracked farm hand closed round it, and the dent in its shoulder fit under his thumb.

"I'll bring it back," he said. "After. When they bring you in. I'll—"

"Listen to me." She bent toward him, so that the Proctors could not hear. "Tonight. When I'm out there. I'm going to sing. On the open channel. All night, probably; I get bored. And some of it's going to be the Count, and some of it's going to be new. Verses you don't know." She looked into his face. "Learn them. All of them. Every word. Get everybody in that hold to learn them. Sing them back to me so I know you've got them. And then keep singing them. After. All of you. Teach them to anybody who'll listen." She held his eyes. "And never let it be in only one head. You understand me? Never one. If you're the only one left who knows it, you teach somebody before you do anything else. Before you eat."

"Why?"

"Because a head can stop," said Sol.

He looked at her. And she saw it come into his face, slowly, the look he got: the farm boy's look, at a thing that did not make sense yet but was going to.

"Okafor," said a Proctor, at the lock. "Now."

Pell did not move.

"Go on," Sol said. "Keep them singing, when it's bad. Teague said. It's the singing that gets you through."

He went. At the lock he turned round once, with the flask in his good hand, and she lifted her hand to him, and then the lock closed.

* * *

Haddad came to her in the dock lock's inner chamber, a little before, while the last of them were going through. She did not come close. She stood by the bulkhead in the grey rigger's suit too big for her in the shoulders, with her helmet under her arm, and said, without looking at Sol, in a voice so low that Sol had to step nearer to hear it:

"The third notch."

Sol waited.

"The deep one. In the Barn, on the third arm, with no dog in it. Your gang calls it the leftover." Haddad looked at the floor. "It isn't a leftover. It was the thirteenth notch. In my design. The quench position." She lifted her eyes. "The governor holds the throat steady by breathing. If you stop it breathing—a dog in an ordinary notch, like yours—and the field tries to couple, it shears. That's what you threatened him with. But if the throat's already open, and you seat a dog in the third notch, and turn it, the dog doesn't stop the governor. It *decouples* it. Throws it out of phase with the field. Cleanly. On purpose. And the throat closes." She paused. "Not a shear. Not a front. Nothing comes out. It folds in. Forty seconds, about. Like a hand closing."

"And then?"

"And then it's shut. And it stays shut, for as long as that dog's seated in that notch and turned. Nothing on the other side can open it. They can couple their ring as hard as they like; there's nothing on this side for it to couple to. They can knock on it till the end of time." Haddad looked at her. "I designed it in case the throat ever ran wild. A way to shut the door. From this side. Lind struck it out. He said a gate that could be shut by anybody with a wrench wasn't an asset, it was a liability. But the notch was already cut. And the bore through the Housing was already drilled. And nobody filled them in, because filling them in would have cost money."

Sol was quiet.

"A dog in the third bore," she said slowly. "Not seated. Just—sitting there. Waiting. Not in the notch. Would your instruments see it?"

"No." Haddad's voice was barely there. "Not until it was turned. Then everything would see it."

"And if somebody had the turns. Which way, how many."

"Twice counter to the governor's breath, then once with it, to seat the key in the step. Then pin it through the head so it can't back out. Then a quarter more." Haddad shut her eyes. "That's from the design. I drew it. I don't remember which way's which on the Housing's face; it depends where you stand. Left and right change." She opened them. "Chief. What you're thinking. It won't save you. It won't save anyone now. They're going to light it in the morning and come back and work this platform until everyone on it's dead. That's what you've bought them. A lock won't change that. Not now."

"Not now," Sol agreed.

They stood in the dock lock's inner chamber and looked at each other.

"Your instruments," said Sol. "Tonight. When I pull the dog out of number one. You'll tell them the governor's clear."

"It will be clear."

"And nothing else."

Haddad was quiet for a long time.

"Nothing else," she said.

Then she turned and went into the lock, and the Proctors searched her as they had searched everybody, and found nothing, because there was nothing to find; and Sol stood in the empty chamber and listened to the *Assessor*'s holds fill up through the bulkhead, four hundred and six people breathing.

* * *

They took her to the Spindle in the Proctors' boat, because the *Patience* was on the end berth holding air, just, with two of her arms gone and holes in her hull you could see through.

Lusk flew it himself. He did not speak to her. He sat in the pilot's seat with his square pale face behind his visor and his hands on the board, and she sat behind him in her rigger's suit with three extra air packs strapped to her harness and her own big driver, Gang Four's driver, a meter and a half of dark steel worn bright at the grip by twelve years of her hands, clipped across her back; and the Ring went by under them, and the Spindle came up, black against Kettle, laced with gold.

He put her off at the rigging hatch. He did not come in close; he held the boat twenty meters off and let her go across on her line, and when she was on the hatch frame and clipped to it, he said, on the open channel, flat and clipped: "Surety in place. Dace, M., zero four one seven. At the Spindle. Boat withdrawing."

And then, after a moment, on the private channel, so quietly she nearly did not hear it: "Twelve years, Dace. You'd nearly done it."

She did not answer him. The boat's thrusters puffed white and it turned and went, small and fast, back along the Ring's face toward the platform and the *Assessor*, and then it was gone, and she was alone.

The Barn was dark. The governor was still. Number One's dog stood out of its cuff a hand's width, where they had driven it home two days ago, where Bracken's hands had been.

She went to the crane.

She had never worked it alone. Oyelaran had, a hundred times, but Oyelaran was a crane man and she was a rigger and you did not touch another gang's crane, it was the oldest rule on the Ring after the one about not letting the young ones go first. She clipped in at its control seat. Its little lights came on amber. She looked at the board and thought about Oyelaran's hands on it, the way they had moved, and put her own hands where his had been, and the boom unfolded out into the dark with its long hiss.

It took her three hours to get the dog out of Number One. She had to back it out with the driver, alone, a quarter-turn and then the crane, a quarter-turn and then the crane, because there was no one else on the other three drivers to keep it even; and she called the Count to herself, out loud, *one for the hook*, and answered herself, *and two for the line*. Three rounds. When the last round was done the crane walked the dog out of the bore, four meters of steel, grinding, whispering through her gloves.

And the governor breathed.

She felt it, through the cuff under her hand. The hair one way. The hair back. Alive.

"*Assessor*," she said, on the open channel. "This is Dace. Number One's out."

It was Haddad who answered. "Confirmed. The governor's breathing. Cycle eleven point four. Nominal." Her voice was dry and level and accented and perfectly calm. "Governor clear."

There was a pause on the channel. Then Lind's voice, soft as folded paper: "Thank you, Doctor. Thank you, Chief. Please remain where you are."

"Where would I go?" said Sol, and switched them off.

Then she swung the crane round.

The dog hung in its claws, four meters of dark steel, turning very slightly on its grip as the boom swung it out across the Barn. Away from Number One, and its rack, and its empty bore. Toward the third arm. The big one, with the Inner Housing round it, the great boxy mass of steel plating four meters thick wrapping the cuff and a whole section of the governor ring behind it. Toward the empty bore through the Housing's face, that lined up on the deep notch, with its stepped profile, like a key-slot.

She brought the dog's point to the bore's mouth, and stopped the crane, and hung there on her line beside it in the dark with her lamp on the Housing's face, and breathed.

Then she switched the open channel back on, and began to sing.

* * *

She sang the Count first. The old one. All of it, the way Gang Four had always sung it, calling the numbers herself and answering herself, while she let the crane ease the dog into the bore, slowly, a meter, two meters, three, the long grinding whisper through her gloves, and stopped it with its point a hand's width short of the notch's mouth. Short. Not in. Waiting. Its head stood out of the Housing's face a hand's width more than a seated dog would, and no more.

*Eight for the dark and nine for the drop.*

She took her scribe out of her thigh pocket and cut three short lines in the steel of the Housing's face beside the dog's head. Side by side, a finger apart, each the length of a fingernail. Witness marks. So that whoever came would know where it had been, and where it was meant to go.

*Ten for the Company—ten, and stop.*

She stopped. And on the open channel, very faint, very far off, out of the *Assessor*'s holds a thousand kilometers away, she heard it.

*Count it up and count it slow,*  
*count it back down and home we go.*

Four hundred voices. Ragged, and late, and out of tune, because the *Assessor*'s holds were not built for singing and the bonds were packed into them like cargo, and some of them did not know the words, and some of them were crying. But singing. She heard Pell's cracked tenor in it, a little sharp, leading. She heard Mother Szell. She heard, she thought, Juno.

She hung on her line beside the dog in the dark and let it go through her.

Then she sang the new verse.

She had made it in the dock lock's inner chamber, while Haddad talked and while the four hundred and six went past her. She had made it the way the riggers had always made verses, out of what was in front of them: the Housing's face, and which way the light came from, and the turns Haddad had given her, *twice counter, then once with it*, and the dog and the notch and the scribed marks. She had made it so that it would go to the tune of the Count, and so that a child could skip to it, and so that anyone who stood where she was standing now, in front of the Housing's face, with their hand on the dog, could do what it said.

She sang it slowly, the first time. As though she were speaking to someone just beside her.

*Sun on your left and the Kettle below,*  
*dog to the notch where the old marks show,*  
*twice to the dark and once to the light,*  
*seat it and pin it and turn it tight.*

She had turned round, singing it, on her line, in front of the Housing's face; and the rigging hatch on the Barn's far side was on her left, the second hatch, the open one, and through it, small and dim and red as a banked coal, Hearth. And below her, through the Barn's open floor-hatch, beyond the Ring, filling everything, Kettle. And the dark was the way the governor did not breathe, and the light was the way it did, and that was how you would know which way to turn, wherever you stood, for as long as there was a Hearth and a Kettle.

*That is the way that you lock the door—*

She stopped. She breathed.

*—count it back down and it's open once more.*

The channel was silent.

She sang it again. And again. She sang the whole Count, the old part and the new, and stopped at the end of it, and waited. And the third time, out of the far-off hold, she heard Pell's voice come in under hers, on the second line, cracked and sharp and very careful, *dog to the notch where the old marks show*; and then others, a few, and then more, until by the fifth time through the whole hold was singing it back to her, ragged and late and out of tune, four hundred voices a thousand kilometers off, *twice to the dark and once to the light*, getting the words wrong and putting them right, over and over, the way you learn a song in a crowd, the way the riggers had always learned everything; and nobody on the *Assessor* who was not a bond had the least idea what they were singing about. It was a work song. A dying woman on the Spindle singing a work song to keep her spirits up, and her gang singing it back to her. That was all. Lind, she imagined, did not even look up from his book.

She sang it with them for an hour. Then she let them carry it, and went on working.

* * *

She opened the plate at the second hour of the night watch.

Ten bolts, in a star, round a plate the size of a cart wheel on the Housing's face, beside the dog's head. She took them out with her own driver in the order of the Count. *One for the hook.* Each one turned with a long dry squeal that she felt through her teeth. *Two for the line.* She stacked them on a magnet on her thigh. When the tenth was out she swung the plate back on its hinge, and her lamp went in through the opening and lit the chamber inside: a small steel room, four meters of steel on every side, with the governor's old instruments on its far wall, and, mounted in the middle of them, Haddad's clock. A grey box the size of a kit bag, with three cables running into it from three directions, and a small green display on its face, counting.

*3:41:09.*

*3:41:08.*

She did not look at it again.

She turned the plate on its hinge so that its inside face was toward her, in her lamp. Bare steel. Grey. Smooth. Five centimeters thick, Company plate, the best they had. Nobody would ever see this side of it unless they took out all ten bolts, in order, and swung it back; and nobody would ever take out all ten bolts in order unless they knew to.

She took out her scribe.

It was a rigger's scribe, a hand's length of hardened steel with a point like a needle, the same one she had used on her first night aboard to scratch six hundred and twenty-four marks into the wall by her bunk. She had kept it in the thigh pocket of her liner for twelve years. It had gone through the cloth and into her leg in the spine corridor on Day Two Hundred and Forty-Seven. It cut Company plate the way it cut Company polymer: slowly, with a squeal, a hair at a time.

She cut the letters large, and square, and deep, in the plain face of the stencils on the platform's walls, because that was the only lettering she knew how to make properly, and because she wanted them to last.

IF YOU ARE READING THIS YOU OPENED IT RIGHT.

WE BUILT THIS. 412 OF US. BONDED. THE COMBINE MEANT TO BURN US AT FIRST LIGHT FOR THE INSURANCE AND WE MADE THEM STOP. THEY PUT US ON FOR LIFE INSTEAD.

THIS IS A LOCK. IT WORKS FROM THIS SIDE ONLY. COUNT IT UP TO LOCK. COUNT IT BACK DOWN TO OPEN. THE SONG IS THE KEY. WE ALL HAVE IT.

DON'T LET THEM OWN THE DOOR. LOCK IT IF YOU HAVE TO.

FIRST LIGHT IS A WAVE. GET BEHIND THE STEEL.

It took her a long time. Her hand cramped in her glove and she stopped and worked it and went on. The squeal of the scribe was in her teeth and her jaw and the back of her skull. On the open channel, the hold on the *Assessor* was still singing, softer now, some of them asleep, some of them not, a few voices going round and round the new verse like water round a stone. Sometimes she sang with them. Sometimes she only listened.

Under the words she cut the verse. All of it. The old Count, the numbers, and then the Door. In the order she had made it. And beside the Door, two small curved arrows, one marked DARK and one marked LIGHT, the way a rigger marks a valve, so that nobody would ever have to guess. So that if the song drifted—and songs drifted; she had sung the Count on the Ring for twelve years and it had lost a verse and gained one in that time—whoever opened the plate would have it the way it was meant.

And then her name. She thought about it for a while before she cut it. Then she cut it the way it was written on the base of her flask, and on the sheet with the blue letterhead, and on the wall of Bay Fourteen above the door.

M. DACE. 0417. GANG FOUR. RIGGER.

And under it, after a moment, smaller, because it was true and there was no one else to say it:

AND DOV DACE 0418. AND KOSTYA LEM. AND ABRAM TEAGUE. AND BRACKEN. WHO HELD ON.

She looked at it for a long time in her lamp.

Then she took the locking pin out of her harness pouch—a short fat plug of hardened steel the length of her finger, the pin that would go through the dog's head when the dog was seated and keep it from ever backing out—and wired it to the inside of the plate, under her name, with a twist of tether wire through two holes she burned for it with the cutter's short blue tongue. Where the next pair of hands would find it, with the words.

Then she swung the plate shut, and put the ten bolts back in, in the order of the star, backward. Ten to one. *Count it back down.*

The display on the clock behind it had said *2:12:40* when she closed the plate. She did not need to see it any more. She could feel the time going, in her own body, like the governor's breath.

She unclipped her driver from her back, Gang Four's driver, a meter and a half of dark steel worn bright at the grip, and laid it on the steel lip at the foot of the Housing's face, under the dog's head, where the next pair of hands would find it. Then she clipped herself to the hardpoint by the plate, and hung there, and switched the private channel on, because the light on it had been blinking for some time.

"Chief Dace," said Castellane.

"Director."

"I wanted—" He stopped. She could hear him breathing. He was alone, she thought; there was no one behind him. "I wanted to say that as soon as the front's past I'll have the boat at the Spindle. Lusk's flying it. I've told him. Myself. The minute Dr. Haddad says it's safe."

"How long will that be?"

He did not answer.

"Six hours," said Sol. "Eight. To be sure. That's what Haddad'll say. Maybe a day."

"I'll send it the minute—"

"You won't," said Sol. "But you'll mean to."

The channel was quiet.

"I'd like to ask you for something," she said.

"Anything."

"There's a dog in the third bore. On the Housing. Sticking out of the face a hand's width." She looked at it in her lamp, its squared head, the three marks scribed beside it. "When your people come after, they'll see it. Your engineers. They'll want to pull it, because it's not in the plans. Don't let them."

"What is it?"

"Tell them it's structural," said Sol. "Tell them it holds up the Ring. Tell them anything. Tell them the Founders put it there." She almost laughed. "You'll be a Founder. All of you, after this. You'll have your names on things. Tell them it's yours, and nobody'll touch it."

He was quiet for a long time.

"Is it structural?" he said.

"It holds up something," said Sol.

She heard him breathe, a long breath, in and out.

"All right," said Imre Castellane. "I'll see to it. I promise you."

"And Annick," said Sol. "When you go home. When you see her."

"Yes?"

"Tell her who built it," Sol said. "That's all. Not the Founders. Tell her who built it."

"I will," he said; and then, after a while, in a different voice, very low: "I'm sorry. I'm so sorry."

"I know," said Sol, and switched him off.

* * *

She went into the Housing at the third hour of first shift, through the crawl hatch round the back, and dogged it shut behind her, and sat down in the dark.

It was very small. She had forgotten. She had been in it twice, and the second time with Dov, and they had sat back to back with their knees drawn up and their suit lamps off, and he had told her the plots of every serial on the platform screens, wrong. She sat with her back to the place where he had been. On the far wall, where she could not see it, Haddad's clock was counting. She could hear it, very faintly, through her helmet, through the steel: a tick, too small and high to be the governor, once a second.

She turned off her lamp to save the cells.

It was the darkest place she had ever been. Darker than the Barn. Darker than the shadow of Kettle on the Ring's face. Four meters of steel on every side, and nothing coming through it, and nothing in it but her own breath in her helmet and the tick of the clock and, under that, the governor, breathing. A hair one way. A hair back. She put her glove flat on the steel floor and felt it.

The open channel was very quiet now. The hold on the *Assessor* had gone to sleep, mostly. She could hear, faintly, one voice still going, low, cracked, a little sharp. *Sun on your left and the Kettle below.* Over and over. As if he was afraid that if he stopped he would forget.

She sang it with him for a while. Then she let him go on alone, and listened.

She thought about the soup. Pier Six, under the old cranes. Real fish from the tanks, with the bones still in. She thought about the cranes, where they had hanged nineteen men in '64 for the ships coming in to see, and about Teague at nineteen walking under them every day of his life. She thought about Kostya, sixty meters past the end of the chain, turning slowly in the work lights, saying *I never saw the whole thing*. About Dov, writing a letter to a factory on Halvard Four, with a careful drawing on the back. *I would like her to get to the end of them.* About the marks on the wall of Bay Fourteen above her bunk, six hundred and twenty-four of them, crossed through; and the small ones at the bottom, in their tens; and the one she had left uncrossed last night, before the line.

She had not got to the end of them. She thought Dov would mind that more than she did.

"Okafor," she said, on the open channel.

The singing stopped.

"Chief?" Very far off. Very small.

"Have you got it?"

"I've got it. We've all got it. Chief, they're saying it's an hour—"

"I know what they're saying. Fill the flask in the morning. Chicory. It's free."

"Chief—"

"Keep them singing," said Sol, and switched off the open channel, and the private, and every channel there was, and sat in the dark with her glove on the floor, feeling the governor breathe.

She began, under her breath, to count.

Not the days. She had stopped counting the days. The Count. *One for the hook.* Slowly, the way she had taught Pell. *Two for the line.* As though she were speaking to someone just beside her. *Three for the dog.*

The tick of the clock stopped.

And the governor took a breath it had never taken. She felt it begin, under her glove: not the hair one way and the hair back, but one way, only one, a long slow shift, as though something enormous on the other side of the steel had leaned its whole weight against the world. The floor of the Housing tilted under her. Not much. Enough. The whole Spindle around her, nine hundred meters of it, began to sing—a deep note, rising, in the steel and her bones and the floor of her belly and her teeth, a note she had never heard it make and nobody had ever heard it make, the note of a door opening that had never been opened.

*Four for the time*, she said.

Through four meters of steel she could not see anything. She did not need to. She could feel it. The throat forming, out there, in the Ring she had built; space tearing along a line drawn with ten thousand kilometers of gold, very precisely; here and Ostrava becoming two places and then one. Forty seconds, Haddad had said. She counted them. *Five for the air. Six for the seal.*

And then the front.

It did not feel like anything she had expected. It did not feel like heat, or light, or a blow. It felt like being remembered. That was the only word she ever had for it, in the few minutes she had to look for one: as though every part of her, every bone and cell, were suddenly and completely known, all at once, by something enormous that had been looking for it; and then let go. Her suit's dosimeter screamed in her helmet, a long high whoop, and then stopped, all at once, burned out. The note of the Spindle went up and up out of hearing. And through four meters of steel, through her closed eyes, through the back of her skull, there was a whiteness. Not light. Whiteness. Like snow on the hills above Ostrava that she had seen once as a girl, from a train.

*Seven*, she said, *the hand on the cold steel wheel.*

Then it was past.

The note came down. The floor of the Housing settled under her glove. And the governor—she felt it, she would swear to it afterward to anyone who would listen, except that there was no afterward and nobody to swear to—the governor breathed. A hair one way. A hair back. Steady. Slow. Alive.

She sat in the dark for a while with her hand on the floor.

Then she found the crawl hatch by feel, and undogged it, and went out.

It was not sensible. Haddad would have told her it was not sensible; the front had passed but the throat was open now and it would be throwing off a steady burn of its own for as long as it was open, and the Barn was not four meters of steel. She did not care. She went out through the crawl hatch and round the Housing on her line in the dark, past the dog's head standing out of its face, past the plate with its ten bolts, past Gang Four's driver lying on the steel lip where she had laid it; and across the Barn to the second rigging hatch, the one on the far side, the open one, that looked out on nothing.

And looked out.

The Ring was lit.

Not with work lights. With itself. The whole inner face of it, thirty-eight kilometers round, the gold lines that she and four hundred and eleven others had laid and seated and torqued by the Count for twelve years, were burning—not bright, not like fire, but alive, a soft clean gold, all the way round, so that from the hatch she could see the whole curve of it at once, going away on both sides and meeting on the far side, small, a bracelet of light. And inside it, filling it, where Kettle should have been—

She did not have a word for it. Nobody would ever quite have one. A disk of light from rim to rim, blue-white at the edges, deepening toward the center into a color that was not violet and not black. And it was moving. Not like water, not like fire. Like a held breath moves in a chest. A slow swell, and a settling. And through it, if you looked long enough, other stars.

She looked a long time.

"Kostya," she said, to nobody, on no channel. "I can see the whole thing."

She sat down on the hatch's lip with her boots locked to the frame and her line clipped to the hardpoint, the way you would sit on a pier, and looked at it. Her suit's air was at sixty percent. She had three packs on her harness. The boat would come when Haddad said it was safe; six hours, eight, a day. Castellane would send it the minute. He would mean to. She found that she was very tired, and that her hands, inside her gloves, were beginning to feel strange, as though they belonged to someone else, someone a long way off; and that she did not mind very much.

She sat on the hatch's lip and watched the Eye breathe, and counted. Not the days. Its breaths. A slow swell, and a settling. One. And two. And three.

Somewhere behind her in the Barn, in the dark, a dog stood out of the Housing's face a hand's width, by three short lines scribed in the steel. Waiting.

* * *

*OSTRAVA MUTUAL ASSURANCE SOCIETY*  
*CLAIM UNDER CONSTRUCTION LOSS POLICY OM/2291/0044*  
*TRANSIT RING TTH-9 · LOSS EVENT D288*

*INSURED BONDED LABOUR LOST: 1 (ONE).*

*DACE, M. — 0417 — GANG 4.*  
*ORIGINAL TERM REMAINING AT LOSS: 12 DAYS.*  
*TERM AS EXTENDED D286 (BREACH OF BOND): NATURAL LIFE.*  
*ACTUARIAL VALUE OF TERM AS EXTENDED: 34 YEARS.*  
*COMPLETION LIABILITY AVOIDED: 1.*

*RETRIEVAL D289, 2ND SHIFT. SUBJECT DECEASED AT SITE. REMAINS COMMITTED TO THE THROAT BY RETRIEVAL CREW (SITE DOSE PROHIBITS RECOVERY).*

*CLAIM APPROVED. PAID IN FULL.*

# Chapter 20: The Quench

*The Concord · 337 AL*

The Firstlight procession went up the Keepers' Spoke at the seventh bell, as it had gone up every Firstlight morning for three hundred and thirty-six years, and Idris walked in it between two men in armor.

Novices first, with lamps. Twelve of them, in white over their grey, carrying the little brass storybook lamps lit from the chapter-house altar, two by two up the long stair; Tobiah among them, with his long neck, not looking at anybody. Then the four Keepers of the Firstlight rota in their full habits, with their lines crossed over their chests: old Holloway, and Marra from the First Spoke, and Desmond Vane from the Fourth, and Idris. Then the Archkeeper, alone, in the grey and silver, slowly, leaning on his black stick with one hand and carrying in the other, upright against his shoulder like a staff, wrapped in a strip of white altar cloth so that only its squared head showed, the Founders' Wrench.

The House's men walked where they liked. Two of them behind Idris, close, in their dark faceless armor, with their long weapons across their chests. More of them on every landing of the stair, standing against the wall as the procession passed, their helmets turning to follow it. Nobody in the procession looked at them. Nobody spoke. That was the rite: the procession went up in silence, with the lamps, from the chapter-house to the gallery, nine hundred meters of stair, and only at the top did anybody speak.

Idris climbed. He had climbed it every Firstlight since he was fifteen. His legs knew it. His heart did not; his heart was going so hard that he thought the men behind him must hear it through their armor. Inside his habit, against his chest, the flask was cold and heavy and its dent fit under his arm where he pressed it.

At the top, the gallery.

It had been dressed for the feast. Garlands of white silk along the brass rails, and the lamps lit, all of them, in their chains, and the gauges polished until they shone, and the long window onto the Eye washed so clean that it seemed there was no glass in it at all. And above the gallery, set into the Spindle's curved wall where a gallery of the Light's servants had no business to be, a box of glass. New. The Factor's people had built it in the last month. Through its windows Idris could see them already in their places, looking down: the Prelate in her white and gold, small and round, with her acolytes; and beside her, in black, tall and narrow, Factor Severs, with a slate. Oake was not there. Oake was a clerk; Oake had lists to keep.

Sallow went down the gallery to the north quarter, slowly, on his stick, with the procession behind him. He stopped in front of the grille. He did not look at Idris. He lifted the Founders' Wrench off his shoulder, and unwrapped the white cloth from it, and held it up, a meter and a half of dark old steel, so that the high gallery could see it; and then he bent, very slowly, with his old joints cracking, and laid it on the gallery floor at the foot of the Housing's face, under the grille, under the Knot.

"Where it was found," he said. It was the first thing anyone had said since the chapter-house. It was the rite. "Where the Founders laid it down."

Then he straightened, and turned, and said the invocation, and the four Keepers of the rota said it after him with their hands on their lines.

"One for the hook, and two for the line. Three for the dog and four for the time."

Sallow put his hand flat on the grille, where the brass was brightest. He held it there a moment. Then he took it away, and went on down the gallery to the east quarter, leaning on his stick, without looking back; and the procession broke, and each Keeper went to his quarter, and Idris went to the north quarter's bank of gauges by the Housing, with his two men behind him, and put his hands on the brass.

The Founders' Wrench lay on the floor a pace from his feet.

* * *

The transit began at the eighth bell.

He had the order in his head. He had written it out twice: once for himself and once for Dagny Shrike, who had never got it, who had—he did not know what had happened to Dagny Shrike. The *Remittance* first. Outbound, alone, in the Third Spoke lane, at the eighth bell exactly: two thousand and some souls in her holds, the last Levy, going Inward. Then, as she cleared the far side, the Squadron's van. Nine abreast. Inbound. Entering from the Ostrava side as the *Remittance* left it; so close behind her, the order said, that her beacon and theirs would cross on the Keepers' boards within the same minute. The House did not like to waste the Eye's time. Then the second rank, and the third, and so on, forty-one hulls in six hours, the governor tuned by hand at four quarters all through it.

Through the long window, the *Remittance* was moving.

She was the largest ship he had ever seen go through his lane. A Levy hauler, built for it, three hundred meters long, blunt and grey, with her holds along her flanks in rows like the cells of a comb, and her running lights burning gold and red and white all down her length. She went down the lane toward the Eye's center, slowly, on her tugs' plumes, getting smaller; and the Eye took her. He felt it on the governor before he saw it: a deepening, a weight arriving, the whole tower taking her mass. He put his hands on the north quarter's dials and tuned it, as he had always tuned it, the tiny turns of the bezels that held the phase. The Eye swelled. Its color at the center went darker. And the *Remittance* went down into the light as though into water, her lights going pale, and paler, and then gone.

He looked at the board. Her beacon, a small gold mark, was moving down a line that ran from the Eye's face toward its center. The board counted. Twelve minutes, to the far side. He had tuned ten thousand transits; twelve minutes was the Eye's time for a ship that size.

The men behind him did not move. He could see them in the gauges' glass, reflected, dark.

The Keepers' channel was in his ear. A little brass button in a cup, on a cord, that every Keeper wore at the quarters to hear the others; and through it he could hear Holloway at the south quarter breathing heavily, and Marra at the west humming to herself the way she did when she worked, and Sallow at the east saying nothing at all.

Then, under them, faint, another voice.

It was very faint. It crackled. It was coming in on the Keepers' frequency from somewhere that should not have been able to reach it. A girl's voice.

"—third Spoke Adjunct. Third Spoke Adjunct, are you there. Idris. It's me."

He did not move. He did not let his hands move on the dials. He felt the blood go out of his face and kept his hands still.

"I don't know if you can hear me. Benno's rigged the hold panel. He says it's on your channel but he's never done it before and the hold panel's not meant for it." A crackle. "He says to say he's good with machines."

Idris looked at the board. The *Remittance*'s gold mark. Three minutes in. Nine to go.

"Idris. If you're where I think you are. If you're doing what I think you're doing." Lark's voice was very steady. He had heard it steady like that once before, holding out her wrist in Basilica Square for the cord. "Don't you dare. Not yet. Not till we're through. There's two thousand of us in here. Do you understand me? Two thousand. Not just me. Benno and his mum's neighbors and the old docker with the hook, and Hesk's sister, and half the Sump. They put everybody from Fourteen in here. And all the drawn. Two thousand." A crackle. "Let us through. And *then* lock it."

He said, very quietly, with his lips barely moving, into the brass button on its cord: "Lark."

"Oh," she said. "Oh, there you are."

"I can't hear you very well."

"I can't hear you at all. Benno says you're coming in like a ghost." Something in her voice moved. "Idris. Listen. Dagny's out here."

He stared at the board.

"Her boat. The *Patience*. She came out of Gall Quay at the eighth bell with every House picket in the sky shouting at her, and she's flying alongside us in the Eye right now. Right now. I can see her out of the hold port. She's so close I could touch her. She says if I want, she'll cut through the hold wall and take me off. Me and Benno. And as many as she can carry, which is about twelve." A crackle. "She says say the word."

He did not breathe.

"I told her no," said Lark.

The board. Five minutes in. Seven to go.

"I'm not getting off," said Lark. "Not with twelve. Not with two thousand behind me. I told you. I'm not going to be a thing somebody does something for. Not ever again." Her voice did not shake. "So I'm going Inward. With all of them. And you're going to wait till we're through. And then you're going to do it. And I'm going to teach them, Idris. Inward. Wherever we end up. All of them. Everybody I meet. I'm going to teach them the song. All of it. So that whoever's over there knows it too." A long crackle. "So it's not just in one place. So it's never just in one place."

He heard, behind her voice, very faint, through the hold panel, a sound like the sea. It took him a moment to understand that it was people. Two thousand of them, packed into the holds of the *Remittance* like the cells of a comb. And then he understood that they were singing.

*One for the hook and two for the line—*

"Benno says to tell you," said Lark, "write him down. Big letters."

*—three for the dog and four for the time—*

"I love you," said Lark. "You idiot. I always did. Do it."

The panel crackled, and the sea of voices swelled, and went out.

* * *

The board said the *Remittance* had six minutes to go.

He did not have six minutes. He knew it the way he knew a ship's mass before the gauges showed it. The Squadron's van would enter from the far side as she left it, inside the same minute; and the plate had ten bolts, painted over three hundred times, and eight lead seals on the grille, and he did not know what was behind the plate. He did not know if there was anything behind it. He did not know if the song was anything but a song.

"*The light holds*," he said, into the brass button.

There was a pause, on the Keepers' channel, the length of a breath.

Then, at the east quarter, at the far end of the gallery, Archkeeper Benedek Sallow began to scream.

It was not a man's scream. It was the governor's. Sallow had his hand on something at the east quarter's bank, something Idris could not see from where he stood, and he had opened it, and out of the east quarter's gauges came the phase alarm: a deep rising whoop, so loud it shook the lamps on their chains, that Idris had heard only once in his life, in a drill, as a novice. It went up and up. And under it Sallow's voice, high and cracked and old, on every channel at once, the gallery's horns and the Keepers' buttons and the high gallery's speakers: "*Phase run at east! Phase run at east! Clear the quarter! The governor's running at east! Clear the—*"

The men behind Idris turned. Both of them. Their faceless helmets swung toward the east quarter at the gallery's far end, where the old man in grey and silver was standing in front of his gauges with his arms spread wide, as though to hold something back, and the alarm whooping, and the lamps swinging on their chains. In the high gallery the Prelate was on her feet. Severs was on his. Somebody up there was shouting.

One of the men went. He ran, down the gallery, toward the east quarter, with his weapon up. The other stayed. He took a step after the first, and stopped, and half turned back toward Idris, his helmet swinging between the north quarter and the east, uncertain.

Idris bent and picked up the Founders' Wrench.

It was heavier than he had thought. A meter and a half of old steel, worn bright at the grip by somebody's hands three hundred years ago. He had never in his life hit anyone. He held it the way you would hold a pole, across his body, and he swung it the only way he knew how, the way a novice swings the long-handled scrub-brush in the chapter-house, in a long flat arc from the shoulder with all his weight behind it; and it hit the man's helmet from the side with a sound like a bell, and the man went down on the gallery floor and lay there.

Idris stood over him with the Wrench in his hands. He was shaking. He could not stop.

The alarm whooped. The board said five minutes.

He turned to the grille.

He had his Keeper's knife in his sleeve. He cut the lead seals he had set himself thirteen nights ago, eight of them, the new lead soft under the blade. He did not take the bolts out one by one. He did not have time. He put the Wrench's squared head through the lattice and levered, and the grille's old brass frame screamed and bent and came away from the Housing's face, and he dragged it clear and let it fall, and it rang on the gallery floor like a dropped bell, and there was the Knot, bare, in the light of the swinging lamps.

And the plate beside it. Ten bolts, under three hundred years of grey paint. In a star.

He put the Wrench's head on the first bolt. It fit. To a hair. Sallow had said so. It fit as though it had been made for it, which it had.

"One," he said, out loud, "for the hook."

He leaned on the crossbar. The bolt did not move. He leaned harder, with his whole weight, his feet braced on the gallery floor, and the paint and the old lead seal cracked round the bolt-head in a ring, and something deep in the steel gave with a squeal that he felt in his teeth, and the bolt turned.

"Two for the line."

The one across from it. The paint cracked. The squeal.

"Three for the dog. Four for the time."

He did not know if he was doing it right. He did it the way the children did it, skipping in the corridor of the Sump, the way they did not know they were teaching anybody anything. The star. Across, and two round, and across. *Five for the air. Six for the seal.* The alarm whooped. Somebody was shouting his name from the high gallery; he did not look. *Seven the hand on the cold steel wheel.* His hands were bleeding inside his sleeves where the crossbar had taken the skin off. *Eight for the dark and nine for the drop.*

"Ten," he said, "for the Company. Ten, and stop."

The plate was loose. He put his hands on its edge and pulled, and it swung back on a hinge he had never known it had, heavy, slow, grinding, and as it swung its inside face turned toward him in the swinging lamplight, and he saw that it was covered in writing.

* * *

It was cut into the steel. Not painted, not written. Cut, in big square letters, deep, by someone with a sharp point and a lot of time, in the plain face of the old stencils on the walls of the Sump.

IF YOU ARE READING THIS YOU OPENED IT RIGHT.

He read it standing, with his bleeding hands on the plate's edge. He read it in the time it takes to read it, which was not long; and it seemed to him afterward that he had stood there for a year.

WE BUILT THIS. 412 OF US. BONDED. THE COMBINE MEANT TO BURN US AT FIRST LIGHT FOR THE INSURANCE AND WE MADE THEM STOP. THEY PUT US ON FOR LIFE INSTEAD.

THIS IS A LOCK. IT WORKS FROM THIS SIDE ONLY. COUNT IT UP TO LOCK. COUNT IT BACK DOWN TO OPEN. THE SONG IS THE KEY. WE ALL HAVE IT.

DON'T LET THEM OWN THE DOOR. LOCK IT IF YOU HAVE TO.

FIRST LIGHT IS A WAVE. GET BEHIND THE STEEL.

And under that, the song. All of it. The numbers and then the Door. *Sun on your left and the Kettle below. Dog to the notch where the old marks show.* Not *old men*; *old marks*. Not *kiss it goodnight*; *turn it tight*. And beside the Door, two small curved arrows cut into the steel, one marked DARK and one marked LIGHT, the way a Keeper would mark a valve.

And under that, a name.

M. DACE. 0417. GANG FOUR. RIGGER.

*TTH-9 · 0417.* On the base of a flask. In Mother Rook's room, at the end of a short dead-end corridor in the Sump. Against his chest, now, under his habit. *Take it back where it came from.*

AND DOV DACE 0418. AND KOSTYA LEM. AND ABRAM TEAGUE. AND BRACKEN. WHO HELD ON.

And under the names, wired to the steel with a twist of old grey cord through two small burned holes, a short fat pin.

He stood with his hands on the plate.

Four hundred and twelve of them. Bonded. *They put us on for life instead.* The Sump. The oldest decks, the old platform's hab rings, the curving floors and the stencils on the walls under three hundred years of paint, *C-14, ABANDON HOPE*. The bond-families. The marks on the wall in the old bunkroom, row on row, counting the days somebody owed; and the one at the bottom with the deep line cut under it, where Lark put her fingertip for luck. *Teodor says it's where they stopped.* Forty-one names on the Founders' Roll in Basilica Square, gilded, the size of his forearm. *Castellane. Haddad. Lind.* And not one of these. *We built this.* And in the Sump, in the dark, in a skipping song that nobody up the Spokes had ever bothered to listen to, every one of them had been saying so, for three hundred and thirty-six years.

*Don't let them own the door.*

The board said a minute.

He untwisted the pin from the plate. His fingers would not work properly; the cord was old and stiff and his hands were wet. He got it free. He put it in his mouth to hold it, the steel cold and bitter on his tongue. Then he turned round, where he stood, in front of the Knot.

Until the long gallery window was on his left. And through it, past the curve of the Ring, small and dim and red as a banked coal, Hearth.

And below him, through the gallery's floor of glass, beyond the Ring, beyond the Eye's breathing light, filling everything, Kettle.

He looked at the three short lines scribed in the Housing's face beside the Knot. He looked at the arrows on the plate. DARK. LIGHT. He put the Founders' Wrench into the Knot's socket. It went in to the shoulder, snug, as though it had come home.

On the board the *Remittance*'s gold mark touched the far side of the line and went out.

Clear.

And within the same minute, as the order had said, at the Eye's far side, nine new marks came up on the board, side by side, red. Inbound. And under his feet, before he saw them, he felt them arrive: the governor taking the weight of nine warships at once, nine abreast, a weight so great that the whole Spindle groaned, and the lamps swung on their chains, and through the long window the Eye swelled and darkened at its heart, and its breath caught, the way a man's breath catches when he lifts something too heavy for him. Something he should not have lifted. Something he is going to drop.

*Dog to the notch where the old marks show.* He leaned on the Wrench, and the Knot moved. It had not moved in three hundred and thirty-six years, and it moved, a hand's width, inward, grinding, into the Housing's face, until its head lay level with the three scribed lines, and stopped against something deep inside. A step. A shoulder of steel, in the dark, in the notch.

*Twice to the dark.* He turned the Wrench the way the arrow said. Once. The governor's voice under his feet changed. Twice. He felt the dog go down a step, into the key of the notch, and lock there.

*And once to the light.*
